# Deep Reinforcement Learning: Foundations, Advances, and Applications — A Comprehensive Survey

## 1 Foundations of Deep Reinforcement Learning

### 1.1 Markov Decision Processes and Problem Formulation

## 1.1 Markov Decision Processes and Problem Formulation

The Markov Decision Process (MDP) provides the fundamental mathematical framework underlying virtually all reinforcement learning algorithms. Formally, an MDP is defined as a tuple $\mathcal{M} = (\mathcal{S}, \mathcal{A}, \mathcal{T}, \mathcal{R}, \gamma)$, where $\mathcal{S}$ denotes the state space, $\mathcal{A}$ the action space, $\mathcal{T}: \mathcal{S} \times \mathcal{A} \times \mathcal{S} \rightarrow [1]$ the transition probability function, $\mathcal{R}: \mathcal{S} \times \mathcal{A} \rightarrow \mathbb{R}$ the reward function, and $\gamma \in [2]. At each timestep $t$, an agent observes state $s_t \in \mathcal{S}$, selects action $a_t \in \mathcal{A}$ according to its policy $\pi$, transitions to a new state $s_{t+1}$ sampled from $\mathcal{T}(\cdot | s_t, a_t)$, and receives scalar reward $r_t = \mathcal{R}(s_t, a_t)$. The agent's objective is to find a policy $\pi^*$ that maximizes the expected discounted cumulative return $\mathbb{E}_\pi[3]$. This objective, along with the recursive structure of the MDP, lays the groundwork for the Bellman equations discussed in the following section.

The **Markov property** is the cornerstone of this framework, stipulating that the future state distribution depends only on the current state and action, and not on the history of preceding states and actions: $\mathcal{T}(s_{t+1} | s_t, a_t, s_{t-1}, a_{t-1}, \ldots) = \mathcal{T}(s_{t+1} | s_t, a_t)$. This conditional independence assumption allows the sequential decision-making problem to be decomposed recursively via dynamic programming, enabling tractable solution methods [4]. As will be seen in Section 1.2, this recursive decomposition is precisely what the Bellman equations formalize. However, the Markov property also represents a significant modeling assumption, as many real-world environments exhibit non-Markovian characteristics where historical context meaningfully influences future dynamics [5].

### Reward Functions and Structures

The reward function encodes the task objective and is central to shaping agent behavior. Rewards may be dense, providing frequent feedback at every step, or sparse, delivering signal only upon rare task completion events [4]. The design of reward functions is a non-trivial challenge; poorly specified rewards can lead to unintended agent behaviors. Beyond scalar Markov rewards, research has examined the expressivity of multidimensional reward representations [6] and non-Markovian reward specifications where desired behaviors depend on the full trajectory of states and actions [7]. The structural properties of reward functions also carry important theoretical implications, informing concepts such as potential-based reward shaping and the sample complexity of policy evaluation [8]. These structural considerations directly influence the form and tractability of the Bellman equations that govern value function computation.

### State and Action Spaces

MDPs may be defined over discrete or continuous state and action spaces. In classical tabular settings, exact dynamic programming methods such as value iteration and policy iteration — both grounded in the Bellman optimality principle — are applicable when the state space is small and finite. However, as the state space grows — particularly in multi-component engineering systems where states scale exponentially with the number of components — exact methods become computationally intractable, motivating the use of function approximation [9]. In continuous state spaces, compact and efficient representations of the value function become essential for tractable solutions [10]. Deep neural networks address this scalability challenge by serving as powerful function approximators over high-dimensional inputs, forming the bridge between classical MDP theory and modern deep reinforcement learning.

### Partially Observable MDPs

A fundamental limitation of the standard MDP framework is the assumption of full state observability. In most real-world applications, an agent only receives partial, noisy, or incomplete observations of the true underlying state. The **Partially Observable Markov Decision Process (POMDP)** generalizes the MDP by introducing an observation space $\mathcal{O}$ and an observation emission function $\mathcal{Z}: \mathcal{S} \times \mathcal{A} \times \mathcal{O} \rightarrow [1]$, which specifies the probability of observing $o_t \in \mathcal{O}$ given the current state and action [11]. Because the true state is hidden, optimal decision-making in POMDPs requires maintaining a **belief state** — a probability distribution over possible underlying states given the history of observations and actions — which serves as a sufficient statistic for optimal planning [12].

POMDPs are notoriously difficult to solve due to the continuous and high-dimensional nature of the belief space, rendering exact computation intractable in general [13]. Planning algorithms for POMDPs typically rely on approximations, such as discretization of the belief space or point-based methods. Recent work has established quasipolynomial-time algorithms for planning in observable POMDPs under specific assumptions on filter stability [14], while spectral decomposition methods provide principled approaches for reinforcement learning in POMDPs with provable regret bounds [15]. The POMDP-lite subclass, in which hidden variables are constant or change only deterministically, admits more tractable solutions and is suitable for a broad range of robotic tasks [16].

In deep reinforcement learning, partial observability is often addressed by equipping agents with memory mechanisms such as recurrent neural networks or LSTM-based architectures that maintain a compressed history of past observations as a proxy for the belief state [17]. Representation learning approaches further aim to learn abstract state representations that approximately satisfy the Markov property from high-dimensional, partially observable inputs [18; 19].

### Semi-MDPs and Temporal Abstraction

The **Semi-Markov Decision Process (Semi-MDP)** extends the standard MDP by allowing actions to persist for variable durations rather than a fixed single timestep. In a Semi-MDP, an agent selects a temporally extended action (or **option**) that executes for a stochastic duration, after which the environment is observed and the next decision is made. This framework provides the theoretical basis for **hierarchical reinforcement learning** and temporal abstraction, enabling agents to reason and plan at multiple levels of temporal granularity [20]. The options framework, grounded in Semi-MDP theory, allows agents to compose primitive actions into macro-actions and reason over extended time horizons, significantly reducing the effective planning depth and improving computational efficiency [21; 22].

### Non-Markovian Extensions

Beyond POMDPs and Semi-MDPs, researchers have explored a variety of extensions that relax or modify the standard MDP assumptions to better capture real-world complexity. **Regular Decision Processes (RDPs)** and **omega-regular decision processes (ODPs)** extend the MDP framework to handle non-Markovian transition and reward functions that depend on regular or omega-regular properties of the past or future trajectory, respectively [23; 24]. **MDPs with unawareness (MDPUs)** address settings where the decision maker is unaware of certain available actions [25], while **parametric MDPs (pMDPs)** incorporate unknown transition parameters to account for stochastic environmental uncertainties [26]. **Dynamic Contextual MDPs (DCMDPs)** further generalize the contextual MDP framework to handle non-Markovian environments where context evolves over time [5].

Real-time extensions of the MDP formalism have also been proposed to address the mismatch between the classical assumption that the environment remains static during action selection and the reality of real-time systems where state and actions evolve simultaneously [27]. In multi-agent settings, the MDP framework is generalized to **Markov Games** and **Decentralized POMDPs (Dec-POMDPs)**, which model interactions among multiple agents with potentially conflicting or cooperative objectives, giving rise to additional challenges of non-stationarity and exponential scaling of the joint state-action space [22].

### Summary

The MDP and its extensions constitute the formal backbone of deep reinforcement learning. The richness of this framework — spanning fully observable and partially observable settings, finite and infinite horizons, discrete and continuous spaces, single-agent and multi-agent scenarios, and Markovian and non-Markovian dynamics — provides the conceptual language necessary to formalize an enormous diversity of sequential decision-making problems. Crucially, the core MDP formulation, with its Markov property and discounted return objective, furnishes the mathematical structure from which the Bellman equations emerge naturally. Understanding the assumptions, strengths, and limitations of each formulation is therefore essential not only for selecting appropriate algorithms, but also for appreciating how the theoretical guarantees and practical challenges of deep reinforcement learning are ultimately rooted in the recursive optimality principles explored in the following section.

### 1.2 Bellman Equations and Optimality Principles

## 1.2 Bellman Equations and Optimality Principles

Building directly upon the MDP formalism established in Section 1.1—where the agent's objective is to maximize the expected discounted cumulative return $\mathbb{E}_\pi[3]$—the Bellman equations provide the recursive decompositions of value functions that transform this long-horizon optimization problem into a sequence of locally solvable subproblems. Named after Richard Bellman, who introduced the principle of optimality in the 1950s, these equations express the value of a state (or state-action pair) as a function of immediate rewards and the discounted value of successor states. This recursive structure is made possible precisely by the Markov property discussed in the previous section: because future dynamics depend only on the current state and action, the value of any state can be expressed entirely in terms of the values of its immediate successors. The resulting mathematical framework forms the cornerstone of reinforcement learning and provides the theoretical foundations upon which the value-based algorithms examined in Section 1.3 are built.

### The Bellman Expectation Equation

For a given policy $\pi$, the state-value function $V^\pi(s)$ and the action-value function $Q^\pi(s, a)$ satisfy the **Bellman expectation equations**:

$$V^\pi(s) = \sum_{a} \pi(a|s) \sum_{s'} P(s'|s,a) \left[28]$$

$$Q^\pi(s,a) = \sum_{s'} P(s'|s,a) \left[29]$$

where $\gamma \in [30]. The existence of this unique solution and its derivation from first principles, including the vector form of the Bellman equation, have been rigorously formalized in theorem provers, providing machine-verified correctness guarantees [30].

### The Bellman Optimality Equation

The **Bellman optimality equations** characterize the optimal value functions $V^*(s)$ and $Q^*(s,a)$ corresponding to the optimal policy $\pi^*$ introduced in Section 1.1:

$$V^*(s) = \max_{a} \sum_{s'} P(s'|s,a) \left[31]$$

$$Q^*(s,a) = \sum_{s'} P(s'|s,a) \left[32]$$

These nonlinear equations encode the **principle of optimality**: an optimal policy must act optimally from every state it visits, regardless of how that state was reached. Once $Q^*$ is known, the optimal policy is recovered greedily as $\pi^*(s) = \arg\max_a Q^*(s,a)$—a greedy policy improvement principle that, as will be seen in Section 1.3, directly underpins the Q-learning update rule. Formal proofs of Bellman's optimality principle and the existence of universally optimal policies under discount factors less than one have been established through mechanized verification frameworks [33; 30].

### The Bellman Operator and Contraction Properties

The Bellman optimality equation defines the **Bellman optimality operator** $\mathcal{T}^*$:

$$(\mathcal{T}^* V)(s) = \max_{a} \sum_{s'} P(s'|s,a) \left[28]$$

A fundamental property of this operator is that it is a **$\gamma$-contraction** in the sup-norm: $\|\mathcal{T}^* V - \mathcal{T}^* U\|_\infty \leq \gamma \|V - U\|_\infty$ for any two value functions $V$ and $U$. By the Banach fixed-point theorem, this contraction guarantees that the operator has a unique fixed point $V^*$ and that repeated application converges geometrically [33]. This contraction property is the foundation upon which the convergence guarantees for value-based reinforcement learning algorithms in Section 1.3 ultimately rest. Formal Coq proofs of this convergence using metric coinduction have been developed, mechanizing Bellman's optimality principle and establishing a rigorous basis for certified RL implementations [33].

### Value Iteration

**Value iteration** is a dynamic programming algorithm that computes $V^*$ by repeatedly applying the Bellman optimality operator starting from an arbitrary initialization $V_0$:

$$V_{k+1}(s) = \max_{a} \sum_{s'} P(s'|s,a) \left[34]$$

Because $\mathcal{T}^*$ is a contraction, the sequence $\{V_k\}$ converges to $V^*$ at an exponential rate $O(\gamma^k)$. Value iteration terminates in finite time when a sufficiently accurate (epsilon-optimal) value function is desired [30]. Variants of value iteration have been proposed to accelerate convergence; for instance, **successive over-relaxation (SOR) Q-learning** modifies the Bellman update rule to construct a modified fixed-point iteration with provably faster convergence than standard Q-learning, with almost sure convergence guarantees established through stochastic approximation theory [35]. Multi-step extensions of the Bellman optimality equation allow value iteration to converge with a contraction rate of $O(\gamma^n)$ while maintaining only linear computational complexity, enabling naturally off-policy multi-step algorithms without importance correction [36]. These multi-step contraction rates connect naturally to the eligibility trace and $n$-step return methods discussed in Section 1.3, which interpolate between one-step bootstrapping and full Monte Carlo returns.

### Policy Iteration

**Policy iteration** alternates between two phases: **policy evaluation**, which computes $V^\pi$ for the current policy $\pi$ by solving the Bellman expectation equation, and **policy improvement**, which updates the policy greedily with respect to $Q^\pi$:

$$\pi'(s) = \arg\max_a Q^\pi(s,a)$$

Policy iteration is proven to monotonically improve the policy at each iteration and converge to the optimal policy in finite steps for finite MDPs [30]. The relationship between policy iteration and Newton-Raphson methods for solving Bellman equations has been formalized theoretically: **regularized policy iteration**, where the Bellman equation is smoothed with strongly convex regularizers (such as Shannon entropy in soft actor-critic), is strictly equivalent to the Newton-Raphson method applied to the smoothed Bellman equation [37]. This equivalence provides a unified analysis proving global linear convergence at rate $\gamma$ and local quadratic convergence near the optimal value [37]. The entropy-regularized variant connects directly to the policy gradient and actor-critic methods examined in Section 1.3, where entropy regularization plays a central role in stabilizing policy optimization.

Extensions to **continuous time and space** formulations replace the Bellman equation with the Hamilton-Jacobi-Bellman (HJB) partial differential equation. Policy iteration methods in this setting, including differential policy iteration (DPI) and integral policy iteration (IPI), inherit the theoretical properties of monotone improvement and convergence to the HJB solution, while supporting both model-based and partially model-free implementations [38]. The connection between the Bellman framework and Koopman operator theory further enables HJB-based methods to be tractable for high-dimensional nonlinear systems by lifting dynamics into approximately linear coordinates [39].

### Generalizations and Extensions of the Bellman Equation

The classical Bellman framework assumes cumulative additive reward objectives consistent with the MDP formulation of Section 1.1. Several important generalizations have been developed to handle the broader problem classes introduced there, including non-Markovian reward structures and non-standard objectives. For **non-cumulative objectives**, the standard summation in the Bellman update is replaced by a generalized operation corresponding to the target objective (e.g., the bottleneck objective defined by the minimum reward along a trajectory), and sufficient conditions under which these generalized operators are contractive and globally convergent have been established [40]. For **maximum reward formulations**, where agents seek to identify states achieving the highest reward rather than cumulative return, novel functional forms of the Bellman equation and corresponding Bellman operators have been derived with convergence proofs [41]. For **multi-objective RL**, a generalized Bellman equation learns a single parametric representation of optimal policies across the full space of preferences, enabling few-shot adaptation to new tasks [42].

**Risk-sensitive** formulations enlarge the state space with an additional dimension tracking cumulative rewards, enabling a modified value iteration algorithm that optimizes general utility functions of cumulative reward rather than expected values, with guaranteed sample complexity bounds [43]. For **time-inconsistent problems** where Bellman's principle of optimality does not naturally hold, extended dynamic programming theories and backward policy iteration (BPI) algorithms have been developed that adapt policy iteration to seek subgame perfect equilibria [44].

### The Bellman Residual and Approximation

In practical deep reinforcement learning, where the large or continuous state spaces discussed in Section 1.1 render exact Bellman solutions intractable, the **Bellman residual** $\|T^* V_\theta - V_\theta\|$ serves as a proxy optimization objective for learning parametric value functions. However, theoretical and empirical analysis reveals that the Bellman residual is generally a poor proxy for policy optimization, and that directly maximizing expected return tends to be substantially better despite the prevalence of Bellman residual minimization in value-based methods [45]. The relationship between Bellman equation enforcement and test function spaces has been further studied through **Bellman Residual Orthogonalization**, which approximates Bellman equations by enforcing their validity only along user-defined test function spaces, providing confidence intervals for off-policy evaluation and theoretical oracle inequalities for policy optimization [46].

A key question in model-based RL concerns which aspects of the Bellman equation a learned model must correctly reproduce. The **value equivalence principle** formalizes this by defining two models as value equivalent if they yield identical Bellman updates for a given set of policies and functions, showing that simpler models can be learned without compromising policy performance, and providing a theoretical foundation for architectures such as MuZero and the Predictron [47]. Alternative approaches learn a **parameterized projected Bellman operator** directly from data rather than estimating it through samples, generalizing across transitions while avoiding computationally intensive projection steps [48].

### Distributional Perspectives and Convergence Analysis

Beyond scalar value functions, the Bellman equation extends to the **distributional** setting, where the full return distribution satisfies a distributional Bellman operator. This extension is particularly relevant given the reward structure considerations raised in Section 1.1, where the expressivity of reward representations directly influences the form of the value function being estimated. Distributional analyses of RL algorithms with constant step sizes show that value-based methods such as TD($\lambda$) and Q-learning are contractive in the space of distributions of functions, establishing exponentially fast convergence to a stationary distribution and confirming that the mean of this stationary distribution equals the true value function [49].

The **successor state** framework generalizes the Bellman equation by modeling expected future state occupancy, satisfying not only the forward Bellman equation but also a backward Bellman operator and a Bellman-Newton (BN) operator that encodes path compositionality. The BN operator provides provably better asymptotic convergence than standard TD learning, with a rate independent of the environment, though at the cost of increased complexity and sensitivity to sampling noise [50].

For **multi-agent zero-sum games**—corresponding to the Markov Games framework briefly noted as a generalization of MDPs in Section 1.1—distributed asynchronous policy iteration frameworks based on contractive abstract dynamic programming resolve long-standing convergence difficulties of natural policy iteration algorithms by introducing alternating player choices and policy-dependent mappings with uniform sup-norm contraction properties, enabling parallelizable convergent implementations [51].

### Summary

The Bellman equations and the principle of optimality provide the fundamental recursive structure that makes reinforcement learning computationally tractable, translating the MDP optimization objective of Section 1.1 into concrete algorithmic procedures. Their contraction properties guarantee the convergence of value iteration and policy iteration, while their generalizations accommodate risk-sensitive objectives, non-cumulative rewards, time-inconsistency, and distributional representations. As deep reinforcement learning scales these principles to the high-dimensional and continuous state spaces described in Section 1.1, the theoretical insights derived from the Bellman framework continue to inform algorithm design, convergence analysis, and the development of stable, sample-efficient learning methods. These insights flow directly into the value-based algorithms examined in the following section, where temporal difference learning and Q-learning emerge as stochastic approximation counterparts to the exact Bellman operators developed here, and where the challenge of combining function approximation with bootstrapping introduces new stability considerations that extend beyond the theoretical guarantees established in this section.

### 1.3 Value-Based Reinforcement Learning and Q-Learning

## 1.3 Value-Based Reinforcement Learning and Q-Learning

Building upon the Bellman equations and optimality principles established in the previous section, value-based reinforcement learning methods translate these theoretical foundations into practical algorithms for learning optimal behavior. These methods form one of the foundational pillars of the field, providing a principled framework for agents to estimate the long-term utility of states and actions and to derive optimal behaviors from these estimates. The central objective in value-based approaches is to learn a value function—either a state-value function $V(s)$ or an action-value function $Q(s,a)$—that maps states or state-action pairs to expected cumulative discounted returns. Once such a function is learned accurately, an optimal policy can be extracted greedily by selecting actions that maximize the estimated action-value at each decision step, directly instantiating the greedy policy improvement principle underlying the Bellman optimality framework. The progression from these value-based foundations leads naturally to the policy gradient methods examined in the following section, which optimize policies directly rather than deriving them from learned value functions.

### Temporal Difference Learning

Temporal difference (TD) learning occupies a central position among value-based methods, bridging the gap between Monte Carlo approaches, which rely on complete episode returns, and dynamic programming methods, which require full knowledge of environment dynamics. In contrast to the exact Bellman updates that define value iteration, TD methods update value estimates incrementally using bootstrapping: the estimated value at the current state is updated toward a target that combines the observed immediate reward with the discounted estimate of the successor state's value. This one-step backup rule, known as the TD(0) update, can be understood as a stochastic approximation to the Bellman expectation equation, enabling online learning from incomplete episodes without requiring knowledge of transition dynamics. TD learning has been extensively analyzed theoretically, with finite-time convergence bounds established under linear function approximation [52], and its global convergence to the optimal value function has been proven even with overparameterized two-layer neural networks [53; 54].

The variance of TD updates can be a significant practical concern, particularly in stochastic environments. Several approaches have been proposed to address this issue, including model-based frameworks that exploit first-order Taylor series expansions to analytically integrate over stochasticity in TD updates [55], and differential TD learning algorithms that provide consistent and dramatically lower-variance estimates of value functions compared to standard TD methods [56]. Alternative bootstrapping targets have also been explored, such as the $\eta$-return mixture, which combines value-predictive and feature-predictive knowledge to make more efficient use of sampled experience [57].

Eligibility traces offer a natural extension of the basic TD(0) framework through the TD($\lambda$) algorithm, which interpolates between one-step TD updates and full Monte Carlo returns via a decay parameter $\lambda$. This interpolation mirrors the multi-step extensions of the Bellman operator discussed in the previous section, where $n$-step returns yield faster contraction rates. Eligibility traces propagate credit for rewards backward through recent state-action histories, enabling faster learning when rewards are sparse or delayed. The finite-time analysis of TD learning extends naturally to TD($\lambda$) under linear function approximation [52].

Fixed-horizon TD methods provide an alternative formulation in which value functions predict cumulative rewards over a fixed number of future time steps rather than bootstrapping from discounted infinite-horizon estimates. By bootstrapping from shorter-horizon value functions rather than from the value function itself, fixed-horizon TD methods are immune to the stability problems associated with the deadly triad of off-policy learning, function approximation, and bootstrapping, and have been shown to converge under both linear and general function approximation [58].

### SARSA

SARSA (State-Action-Reward-State-Action) is an on-policy TD control algorithm that learns the action-value function $Q(s,a)$ for the policy being followed by the agent. The name reflects the quintuple of experience $(s_t, a_t, r_{t+1}, s_{t+1}, a_{t+1})$ used in each update: the current action-value estimate is updated toward the target $r_{t+1} + \gamma Q(s_{t+1}, a_{t+1})$, where $a_{t+1}$ is the action actually taken under the current policy. Because SARSA updates use the action selected by the behavioral policy at the next step, it is an on-policy method: the learned Q-values reflect the quality of actions under the actual exploration policy rather than under any hypothetical greedy policy. This property can be advantageous in environments where exploration actions carry real risks, as SARSA learns to account for the costs of exploratory behavior—a concern that is also central to the policy gradient methods discussed in the following section, which must similarly balance exploration and exploitation while optimizing a policy directly.

The Q($\sigma$) algorithm unifies SARSA and Q-learning along with Expected SARSA by introducing a parameter $\sigma$ that controls the degree to which updates rely on sampled versus expected action values [59; 60]. This framework has been extended with eligibility traces to yield Q($\sigma$, $\lambda$), which empirically outperforms classical TD control methods such as SARSA($\lambda$) and Q($\lambda$) [59].

Applications of SARSA to real-world decision problems, such as portfolio management and trading over financial markets, have demonstrated its practical viability, with on-policy methods like SARSA showing competitive performance against off-policy alternatives in certain settings [61].

### Q-Learning

Q-learning, introduced by Watkins, is arguably the most influential value-based reinforcement learning algorithm. It is an off-policy TD control method that directly approximates the Bellman optimality equation through sample-based updates, learning the optimal action-value function $Q^*(s,a)$ by updating toward the maximum estimated future value: $Q(s_t, a_t) \leftarrow Q(s_t, a_t) + \alpha [62]$. Unlike SARSA, Q-learning uses the greedy action at the next state in its update target, regardless of the action actually taken by the behavioral policy. This separation between the behavioral policy (used for exploration) and the target policy (implicitly greedy) makes Q-learning an off-policy algorithm, enabling it to learn about the optimal policy even while following an exploratory strategy such as $\epsilon$-greedy. In this sense, each Q-learning update can be understood as a stochastic approximation to the Bellman optimality operator $\mathcal{T}^*$, and the convergence of Q-learning to $Q^*$ follows from the contraction properties of this operator established in the previous section.

In the tabular setting, Q-learning is guaranteed to converge to the optimal Q-function provided all state-action pairs are visited infinitely often and appropriate learning rate conditions are satisfied. The continuous-time counterpart of Q-learning has also been developed within an entropy-regularized diffusion process framework, leading to a "q-learning" theory that is independent of time discretization and connects to policy gradient methods [63]. Extensions to high-dimensional settings with stochastic dynamics have been explored through Hamiltonian Q-learning, which uses Hamiltonian Monte Carlo sampling to generate training data and matrix completion to exploit the low-rank structure of Q-functions [64].

Neural Q-learning, which employs neural networks as function approximators for Q-values, has been shown to globally minimize the mean-squared projected Bellman error at a sublinear rate when using overparameterized two-layer networks, with the associated feature representations converging to their optimal configurations [53; 54]. The continuous-time analysis connects neural Q-learning to soft Q-learning and policy gradient algorithms, establishing a unified theoretical framework [54]—a connection that anticipates the policy gradient methods examined in the following section.

### Structural Properties of Q-Functions

Recent empirical and theoretical investigations have revealed important structural properties of Q-functions that have practical implications for algorithm design. The Q-function in continuous control tasks has been found to exhibit a low-rank structure across a wide range of popular algorithms and environments [65; 66]. This low-rank phenomenon appears to reflect a common learning dynamic in which Q-matrices transition from stochastic high-dimensional representations to smooth low-dimensional ones as training progresses [66]. Leveraging matrix estimation techniques to exploit this structure has been shown to improve both planning efficiency and reinforcement learning performance across control and Atari benchmarks [65].

A positive correlation between the rank of the value matrix and value estimation uncertainty has been identified, motivating uncertainty-aware low-rank Q-matrix estimation methods that selectively reconstruct highly uncertain Q-values using the underlying low-rank structure [66]. These structural insights have also motivated novel algorithmic paradigms such as state-action separable reinforcement learning, where the action space is decoupled from value function learning by focusing updates on next-state values, with a lightweight transition model used to recover the corresponding actions [67].

### Stability Challenges and the Deadly Triad

One of the most significant challenges in value-based reinforcement learning is the potential for divergence when TD learning is combined with function approximation and off-policy training—a combination known as the "deadly triad." This instability arises because function approximation introduces approximation errors that can compound through bootstrapping, while off-policy updates can create distributional mismatches between the data distribution and the on-policy distribution required for convergence guarantees. Importantly, this challenge is distinct from the theoretical convergence guarantees of exact Bellman operators established in the previous section: those guarantees rely on the strict contraction properties of the Bellman operator, which may no longer hold when function approximation is introduced.

The conditions under which divergence occurs in deep Q-learning have been analyzed through a linear approximation lens, revealing that instability arises when the leading-order approximation to the deep Q-update fails to be a contraction in the supremum norm [68]. This analysis led to algorithms capable of stable deep Q-learning for continuous control without conventional stabilization tricks such as target networks or adaptive gradient optimizers [68].

The dynamics of Q-learning and SARSA with $\epsilon$-greedy exploration under function approximation have been characterized using differential inclusion theory, providing the first framework for explaining phenomena such as policy oscillation, chattering, and convergence to suboptimal attractors that are frequently observed in practice but had previously lacked rigorous explanations [69].

Alternative loss functions have been proposed to address the instability of Q-learning under function approximation. A kernel-based loss function that can be optimized using standard gradient descent without risking divergence avoids the need for double samples required by residual gradient methods and works reliably with neural network function approximators on both on- and off-policy data [70]. Convergent actor-critic algorithms derived from the exact gradient of the averaged state-value function objective extend convergent off-policy learning to classical actor-critic architectures with function approximation [71].

The approximate TD learning algorithm has been shown to be a gradient descent procedure when the current policy is reversible—that is, when there exists a stationary distribution under which detailed balance holds—even with nonlinear function approximation, and this connection to the Dirichlet norm provides stability guarantees without relying on contractivity of the Bellman operator [72].

### Limitations in Large State Spaces and the Role of Function Approximation

The tabular formulation of Q-learning and SARSA requires maintaining separate estimates for every state-action pair, which becomes computationally intractable in environments with large or continuous state spaces. This fundamental limitation motivates the use of function approximation—parametric models that generalize value estimates across similar states—and represents the critical juncture at which classical reinforcement learning connects to deep learning. It is also at this juncture that value-based and policy gradient approaches begin to complement one another, as the actor-critic methods discussed in the following section combine value function approximation with direct policy optimization.

The analysis of value-based methods with kernel function approximators or overparameterized neural networks has established polynomial sample complexity bounds for optimistic variants of least-squares value iteration, with regret that scales with the intrinsic complexity of the function class rather than the number of states [73]. This result formally establishes the benefit of function approximation for reinforcement learning in large state spaces.

Gradient-based temporal difference methods, such as GTD2 and related algorithms, provide a path to stability in the off-policy function approximation setting by computing the true gradient of a well-defined objective rather than performing semi-gradient updates. Parameter-free variants of these algorithms that achieve minimax optimal convergence guarantees without expensive hyperparameter tuning have been developed, combining online learning theory with gradient-based TD methods [74].

The combination of deep neural networks with Q-learning—pioneered by the Deep Q-Network (DQN) and its successors—represents the primary pathway through which classical value-based methods scale to high-dimensional sensory inputs. By leveraging the representational power of convolutional neural networks to extract relevant features from raw observations such as game frames, and stabilizing training with mechanisms such as experience replay and target networks, deep value-based methods have achieved remarkable performance across a broad range of complex tasks. The theoretical foundations established by classical tabular Q-learning and TD learning, including their convergence properties, update rules, and the fundamental challenge of the deadly triad, directly inform the design choices and algorithmic innovations that characterize modern deep reinforcement learning. Moreover, many of these insights carry over into the policy gradient methods examined in the next section, where the need to estimate value functions for use as baselines and critics creates a deep and enduring connection between value-based and policy-based approaches to reinforcement learning.

### 1.4 Policy Gradient Methods and the Policy Optimization Perspective

## 1.4 Policy Gradient Methods and the Policy Optimization Perspective

Building upon the value-based foundations established in the previous section—where optimal policies were derived implicitly through action-value function maximization—policy gradient methods represent a complementary and equally foundational paradigm in reinforcement learning. Rather than learning a value function and extracting a policy greedily from it, policy gradient approaches directly parameterize the policy and optimize this parameterization by ascending the gradient of the expected cumulative reward. This direct optimization perspective renders policy gradient methods particularly well-suited for continuous action spaces, stochastic policies, and settings where representing a policy explicitly offers structural advantages that value-based methods cannot easily accommodate. As we shall see, these two perspectives are not mutually exclusive: the actor-critic architectures examined in the following section synthesize them into a unified framework that inherits the strengths of both.

### The Policy Gradient Theorem

The theoretical cornerstone of this approach is the **policy gradient theorem**, which provides an analytically tractable expression for the gradient of the expected return with respect to the policy parameters. Formally, given a parameterized stochastic policy $\pi_\theta(a|s)$, the objective is to maximize the expected discounted return $J(\theta) = \mathbb{E}_{\pi_\theta}\left[3]$. The policy gradient theorem establishes that:

$$\nabla_\theta J(\theta) \propto \sum_s d^{\pi_\theta}(s) \sum_a Q^{\pi_\theta}(s,a) \nabla_\theta \pi_\theta(a|s)$$

where $d^{\pi_\theta}(s)$ is the discounted state visitation distribution under the current policy, and $Q^{\pi_\theta}(s,a)$ is the action-value function whose estimation was the central concern of the previous section. This result elegantly sidesteps the need to differentiate through the environment's transition dynamics, enabling model-free gradient estimation [75; 76].

A critical subtlety concerns the treatment of the discounted state distribution. Most practical implementations drop the discount factor from the state distribution, which introduces a distribution shift between the theoretical objective and the empirically optimized surrogate. This discrepancy has been shown to mean that such algorithms do not optimize any well-defined objective, and in adversarial constructions, their fixed points can be globally suboptimal with respect to both discounted and undiscounted criteria [77]. This concern motivates more principled formulations, including temporal-difference-based approaches that reconstruct the policy gradient from the start state without requiring a specific sampling strategy [78]—an approach that draws directly on the TD learning machinery analyzed in the previous section.

### REINFORCE: The Foundational Monte Carlo Estimator

The simplest practical realization of the policy gradient theorem is the **REINFORCE** algorithm, a Monte Carlo policy gradient estimator. REINFORCE collects full episodes under the current policy and estimates the gradient as:

$$\nabla_\theta J(\theta) \approx \frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{t=0}^{T} G_t^{(i)} \nabla_\theta \log \pi_\theta(a_t^{(i)}|s_t^{(i)})$$

where $G_t$ is the return from time step $t$ onward. While unbiased, REINFORCE suffers from high variance, which is a central challenge limiting its sample efficiency—a limitation that mirrors the variance concerns of Monte Carlo methods discussed in the context of temporal difference learning. Variance reduction is commonly achieved through the use of a baseline—typically a state-value function—subtracted from the return to form an advantage estimate [79; 76]. Further variance reduction has been studied through optimal baselines derived analytically [80], action-dependent control variates motivated by Stein's identity [81], and expected policy gradients that integrate over the action distribution rather than sampling a single action [82].

An alternative to likelihood-ratio estimators is the **direct policy gradient** approach, which combines direct optimization techniques with structured search over discrete action spaces. By finding trajectories that optimize a random objective derived from the return-to-go, direct policy gradients can exhibit exponentially higher probability of sampling informative gradient directions in certain settings compared to REINFORCE and include built-in variance reduction through the structure of their random objectives [83].

### On-Policy Policy Gradient Formulations

On-policy methods collect training data using the policy currently being optimized, ensuring that gradient estimates are consistent with the theoretical prescription of the policy gradient theorem. The most prominent deep learning instantiation is **Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO)**, which belongs to a broader family of algorithms including Trust Region Policy Optimization (TRPO) and Asynchronous Advantage Actor-Critic (A3C/A2C), all of which constrain or regularize the magnitude of policy updates to maintain stability. These algorithms are discussed in greater depth in later sections, but their connection to the policy gradient theorem is direct: they all instantiate the gradient ascent idea while adding safeguards against destructively large parameter updates [75; 84]. Notably, A3C and A2C also combine policy gradient updates with value function learning, anticipating the actor-critic synthesis explored in the following section.

The policy gradient framework has been extended to partially observable environments, where the Markov property over raw observations no longer holds. Generalized advantage functions and policy gradient theorems have been derived for Markovian policies in POMDPs, enabling convergence guarantees to carry over from the fully observable case [85]. Similarly, the framework has been extended to continuous-time formulations, where policy gradient computation is recast in terms of martingale conditions, and to continuing (non-episodic) tasks in non-stationary MDPs, where online convergence to critical points of the initial value function has been established [86; 87].

### Off-Policy Policy Gradient Formulations

A fundamental challenge in on-policy methods is their **sample inefficiency**: data collected under the previous policy is discarded after each update, preventing the reuse of historical experience—a limitation that contrasts sharply with the off-policy Q-learning methods discussed in the previous section, which can in principle learn from arbitrary behavior policies. Off-policy policy gradient methods address this by learning from data generated under a different behavior policy, but this introduces the distribution mismatch problem, wherein the state visitation distribution of the behavior policy diverges from that of the target policy.

Several theoretical frameworks have been proposed to address this. The use of **emphatic weightings**—correction factors derived from the ratio of the target to behavior policy's state distributions—leads to the first provably correct off-policy policy gradient theorem [88]. Without such corrections, canonical off-policy methods such as OffPAC and DPG have been shown to converge to incorrect solutions.

An alternative approach recasts off-policy policy optimization as coordinate ascent. The **Coordinate Ascent Policy Optimization (CAPO)** framework decouples policy improvement from the behavior policy's state distribution entirely, avoiding importance sampling by not using the policy gradient in the improvement step [89]. This approach provides global convergence guarantees and quantifies convergence rates for cyclic and randomized coordinate selection rules.

The relationship between on-policy and off-policy objectives—specifically, the so-called **on-off gap**—has been studied theoretically, with conditions identified under which the excursion (off-policy) objective aligns with the traditional on-policy objective [90]. Empirical evidence demonstrates that when these conditions are violated, performance shortfalls emerge, underscoring the importance of understanding when off-policy surrogates are reliable.

### Sample Reuse and Importance Sampling

A practical mechanism for bridging on-policy and off-policy gradients is **importance sampling**, which re-weights gradient contributions from past experience to correct for the distributional shift induced by policy changes. This technique connects directly to the experience replay mechanisms that stabilize off-policy Q-learning, but applies them in the policy gradient setting. Combining importance sampling with parameter-based exploration—wherein perturbations are applied to the policy parameters rather than actions—yields gradient estimators with provably lower variance [91]. The bias-variance trade-off of such estimators can be characterized theoretically, motivating adaptive schemes for meta-parameter selection that guarantee monotonic performance improvement with high probability [92].

### Policy Gradient for General Utility Functions

The standard policy gradient theorem is derived under the assumption that the objective is a linear function of the state-action occupancy measure—specifically, the expected cumulative discounted reward. Many practical problems, however, require optimizing **non-linear utility functions** of the occupancy measure, such as entropy regularization, constrained optimization, or intrinsic motivation objectives. Policy gradient theorems for these general utility settings have been derived, establishing that the gradient can still be expressed elegantly in terms of a stochastic saddle point problem involving the Fenchel dual of the utility function [93; 94]. Global convergence results at rate $O(1/t)$ have been established by exploiting hidden convexity, extending the reach of policy gradient theory well beyond the standard reward-maximization setting [93].

### Convergence Theory and Optimization Landscape

Despite the empirical success of policy gradient methods, their theoretical convergence properties are non-trivial to establish due to the non-concavity of the value function in the policy parameters—a challenge that parallels, at a higher level, the stability difficulties of value-based methods under function approximation described in the previous section. Recent progress has shown that variants of policy gradient methods with random rollout horizons yield unbiased, bounded-variance gradient estimates, enabling convergence to stationary points at established rates [95]. By introducing periodically enlarged step sizes, saddle points can be escaped under mild assumptions, and convergence to locally optimal policies has been proven under a strict saddle condition [95; 96].

A geometric reinterpretation places policy optimization in the space of probability measures, where it can be viewed as Wasserstein gradient flows. Under this formulation, policy optimization becomes a convex problem in distribution space under certain conditions, and efficient algorithms based on discrete gradient flow approximations can be derived [97]. This perspective enriches the theoretical understanding of policy gradient methods and connects them to the broader literature on variational inference and optimal transport.

Another theoretical lens interprets policy gradient methods through the framework of **optimization by continuation**: optimizing affine Gaussian policies and employing entropy regularization can be understood as implicitly optimizing deterministic policies through a sequence of smoothed surrogate objectives [98]. This interpretation suggests that the variance of stochastic policies should be adapted dynamically as history-dependent functions to avoid local extrema rather than solely to maximize expected return.

### Provable Exploration via Policy Cover

A recurring limitation of standard policy gradient methods is their fundamentally **local** nature: gradient ascent updates improve performance locally but may fail to adequately explore the environment, leading to convergence to suboptimal policies—an exploration challenge that also arises in value-based methods but manifests differently in the policy gradient setting. The **Policy Cover-Policy Gradient (PC-PG)** algorithm addresses this by maintaining an ensemble of learned policies—a policy cover—that collectively ensures broad state space coverage [99]. PC-PG enjoys polynomial sample complexity for both tabular and linear MDPs defined over infinite-dimensional reproducing kernel Hilbert spaces, and provides approximation guarantees under model misspecification including state aggregation errors and distribution shift, combining the exploration strengths of model-based and optimism-based methods with the flexibility of direct policy optimization.

Similarly, extending the policy gradient framework to explicitly enforce off-policy exploration has been studied through the lens of generalized policy updates with arbitrary state densities. A sufficient and necessary condition on the state density of policy updates for convergence to optimality has been established, and convergence rates significantly improving those in prior literature have been proven [100]. This motivates hybrid agents that maintain separate on-policy and off-policy replay buffers, mixing gradient updates from both to simultaneously ensure local improvement and global planning.

### Summary

Policy gradient methods occupy a central position in the deep reinforcement learning landscape precisely because they complement the value-based methods examined in the previous section: where Q-learning and TD learning derive policies implicitly through value maximization, policy gradient methods optimize the policy directly as a first-class object, achieving compatibility with continuous action spaces, rich stochastic policy representations, and deep neural network parameterizations. The foundational policy gradient theorem provides a model-free, tractable expression for the gradient of the expected return, and a rich theoretical ecosystem has grown around it—from variance reduction via baselines and action-dependent control variates, to off-policy corrections via emphatic weightings and coordinate ascent, to convergence guarantees in non-convex landscapes, to extensions covering general utility functions, partial observability, continuous time, and risk-sensitive objectives. Together, these developments establish policy gradient methods not merely as practical heuristics but as theoretically grounded algorithms with well-characterized properties. The natural synthesis of these methods with value function estimation—where a learned critic provides low-variance gradient signals to a policy-optimizing actor—forms the basis of the actor-critic architectures examined in the following section, which represent the dominant paradigm in modern deep reinforcement learning.

### 1.5 Actor-Critic Architectures and Advantage Estimation

## 1.5 Actor-Critic Architectures and Advantage Estimation

Building directly on the policy gradient foundation established in the previous section, actor-critic methods occupy a central position in modern deep reinforcement learning by elegantly unifying the complementary strengths of value-based and policy-based approaches within a single, coherent framework. Where pure policy gradient methods such as REINFORCE suffer from high variance, and pure value-based methods struggle with continuous action spaces and explicit policy representation, actor-critic architectures dissolve this tension through a principled division of labor. At their core, these methods maintain two distinct functional components: an **actor** that parameterizes and updates the policy, and a **critic** that estimates a value function to evaluate the quality of the actor's decisions. This duality allows the agent to benefit simultaneously from the stability and low variance associated with value function estimation and the expressiveness and direct optimization afforded by policy gradient methods — and it is precisely this synthesis that makes actor-critic architectures the natural algorithmic home for the deep neural network function approximators discussed in the following section.

### The Actor and Critic: Roles and Interactions

The actor's responsibility is to select actions according to a parameterized policy $\pi_\theta(a|s)$, where $\theta$ denotes the policy parameters. Rather than exhaustively searching over all possible policies, the actor improves its parameters through gradient ascent on an estimate of the expected cumulative reward. As established in the policy gradient theorem, this gradient can be expressed in terms of an action-value function weighted by the policy's score function — but naive estimates computed from raw episodic returns suffer from extremely high variance, making learning slow and unstable. This is precisely where the critic intervenes: by maintaining an estimate of the value function $V^\pi(s)$ or action-value function $Q^\pi(s,a)$, the critic provides a lower-variance baseline against which the actor's actions can be evaluated, directly instantiating the baseline variance reduction technique discussed in the context of REINFORCE.

The critic is typically trained by minimizing temporal difference (TD) errors, which measure the discrepancy between the predicted value and a bootstrapped target derived from the Bellman equation. These TD errors serve as rich learning signals that propagate reward information efficiently through time, enabling faster credit assignment compared to Monte Carlo returns that must wait until the end of an episode. This bootstrapping property is fundamental to the practical success of actor-critic architectures in long-horizon tasks.

### Advantage Function Estimation

A cornerstone concept in actor-critic methods is the **advantage function**, defined as:

$$A^\pi(s, a) = Q^\pi(s, a) - V^\pi(s)$$

The advantage function quantifies how much better (or worse) a particular action $a$ is compared to the average action taken by the policy in state $s$. By centering the policy gradient update around the advantage rather than the raw return, the variance of gradient estimates is substantially reduced without introducing additional bias into the policy gradient direction. This insight, formalized through the policy gradient theorem, transforms the actor-critic architecture from a theoretical curiosity into a practically effective algorithmic framework.

Several approaches to advantage estimation have been explored in the literature. The **Generalized Advantage Estimation (GAE)** framework introduces a bias-variance trade-off parameter $\lambda$ that interpolates between one-step TD errors (low variance, high bias) and full Monte Carlo returns (high variance, low bias), offering practitioners fine-grained control over this critical trade-off — a trade-off that echoes the on-policy versus off-policy tensions discussed in the preceding section. More recently, **Direct Advantage Estimation (DAE)** has been proposed as a method that interprets the advantage function through the lens of causal effects, directly modeling and estimating the advantage from on-policy data without requiring the explicit computation of action-value functions, and demonstrating that this causal perspective can reduce variance and outperform GAE in several discrete control domains [101].

Another perspective on advantage learning is provided by VA-learning, which directly learns both the advantage function and the value function using bootstrapping, bypassing the Q-function entirely [102]. This approach shares theoretical guarantees with Q-learning while operating in the actor-critic paradigm, and has been shown to improve sample efficiency on benchmark tasks by leveraging the direct advantage signal more effectively than indirect derivations from Q-values.

### Bridging Value-Based and Policy-Based Approaches

The actor-critic framework serves as a natural bridge between purely value-based methods, such as Q-learning and DQN, and purely policy-based methods, such as REINFORCE. Pure policy gradient methods optimize the policy directly but are notoriously sample-inefficient due to high variance. Pure value-based methods are often restricted to discrete action spaces and can suffer from instability due to function approximation errors. Actor-critic architectures dissolve this tension: the critic provides stable, low-variance gradient signals through value estimation, while the actor maintains an explicit, differentiable policy representation that can handle continuous action spaces and stochastic behaviors.

A key architectural decision in actor-critic methods concerns whether the actor and critic share parameters or maintain separate networks. Shared architectures promote efficient feature reuse and have been widely adopted in algorithms such as A3C and PPO — algorithms introduced in connection with the policy gradient theorem in the preceding section — leveraging the intuition that useful representations for value estimation also benefit policy learning. However, research has increasingly challenged this assumption, arguing that value function estimation and policy optimization have fundamentally different statistical properties — in particular, a significant difference in the noise levels of the two learning tasks — and that jointly optimizing them with a shared representation can be suboptimal [103]. Specifically, it has been observed that the critic benefits from a lower **bias** return estimate, while the actor benefits from a lower **variance** return estimate, leading to proposals for architectures that decouple these two objectives while still allowing constrained knowledge transfer between the actor and critic networks [103].

This decoupling perspective is further supported by analyses showing that, particularly in visually complex environments or multi-environment generalization settings, the value network is more prone to overfitting to training data than the policy network, and that appropriate regularization or reduced update frequency for the critic can substantially improve both training efficiency and generalization performance [104; 105]. This concern for generalization anticipates the discussion of neural network function approximators and their representational properties in the following section.

### Temporal Difference Errors as Learning Signals

Central to the actor-critic framework is the use of TD errors as the primary learning signal for both the critic and the actor. The one-step TD error is defined as:

$$\delta_t = r_t + \gamma V^\pi(s_{t+1}) - V^\pi(s_t)$$

Under appropriate conditions, this scalar quantity approximates the advantage $A^\pi(s_t, a_t)$, providing the actor with an immediate, online estimate of whether the action taken was better or worse than expected. This connection between TD errors and advantage estimates underpins the efficiency of actor-critic methods: rather than waiting for full episodic returns, agents can update both the actor and critic at every time step, dramatically accelerating learning.

The theoretical properties of this training procedure have been studied extensively. Convergence analyses using two-timescale stochastic approximation theory have established that actor-critic algorithms, including practical implementations with deep neural networks, converge to locally optimal policies under appropriate learning rate schedules, with the critic updated on a faster timescale and the actor on a slower timescale to ensure the critic tracks the current policy accurately [106; 107]. These theoretical results complement the convergence guarantees for policy gradient methods discussed in the preceding section and provide rigorous grounding for the widespread empirical success of actor-critic methods.

However, a recurring challenge identified in the literature is that the critic is typically trained to minimize TD error — an objective defined in terms of value prediction accuracy — rather than an objective directly aligned with policy improvement. This misalignment can cause the critic to provide misleading gradient signals to the actor. Decision-aware actor-critic frameworks have been proposed to address this issue by designing joint objectives for the actor and critic that are explicitly aligned with the goal of policy improvement, and establishing conditions under which monotonic policy improvement is guaranteed regardless of the choice of function approximation [108].

Another approach to improving the quality of critic gradients is through model-based extensions. By backpropagating through a learned environment model, the pathwise derivative of the value function with respect to actions can be computed, yielding more accurate gradient targets for the critic and enabling the actor to benefit from longer-horizon information [109]. Similarly, MAGE explicitly trains the critic to provide accurate action-value gradients by leveraging learned dynamics models during temporal difference learning, producing a critic that is specifically tailored for policy gradient computation rather than mere value prediction [110].

### Stability and Practical Considerations

Training stability is a perennial concern in actor-critic methods due to the interdependence of the actor and critic: errors in the critic propagate to the actor through the policy gradient, and a poorly optimized actor produces suboptimal data for critic training. Several mechanisms have been developed to mitigate these destabilizing feedback loops.

The use of **target networks** — slowly updated copies of the critic used to compute TD targets — is one of the most impactful stabilization techniques, preventing oscillations and divergence during training. The theoretical impact of target networks on convergence in actor-critic settings has been analyzed rigorously, showing that target-based critics reduce estimation error per update and provide finite-time convergence guarantees that complement asymptotic results [106]. **TD-regularized actor-critic** methods take an orthogonal approach, directly penalizing the actor's learning objective by the TD error of the critic, ensuring that large actor updates are discouraged precisely when the critic is most inaccurate and thereby improving overall stability [111].

Another important consideration is the relative learning rates of the actor and critic. Theoretical analyses consistently recommend that the critic be updated at a faster timescale than the actor to ensure stable convergence [106]. In practice, this is often implemented through higher critic update frequencies or larger critic learning rates, and its importance has been validated in mean-field and continuous-time settings [112; 113].

The value function used by the critic also critically affects the quality of advantage estimates. A novel variant proposes that the critic learn the **residual variance** of the value function — estimating state values relative to their mean rather than as absolute quantities — which can provide more informative gradient signals, particularly in tasks with sparse rewards [114]. This reformulation has demonstrated substantial empirical improvements across continuous control benchmarks, suggesting that the precise definition of the critic's learning target has significant consequences for overall actor-critic performance.

### Extensions and Hierarchical Generalizations

Beyond the standard two-component actor-critic framework, several extensions have been proposed to enrich the architecture's capabilities. The **Double Actor-Critic (DAC)** framework reformulates the options framework — a hierarchical approach to temporal abstraction — as two parallel augmented MDPs, applying actor-critic algorithms independently to learn intra-option policies, termination conditions, and a master policy over options, enabling gradient-based hierarchical reinforcement learning [115]. This extension illustrates how the actor-critic paradigm can be naturally generalized to hierarchical decision-making settings, facilitating transfer learning and improved performance on complex robot simulation tasks.

**Parameter-Based Value Functions (PBVFs)** represent another generalization, where the critic's inputs include not only the state but also the parameters of the actor policy, enabling the value function to generalize across different policies and support zero-shot learning of new policies that outperform any policy seen during training [116]. This approach yields novel off-policy policy gradient theorems and demonstrates that a single critic trained over a distribution of policies can provide richer supervisory signals than the standard single-policy critic — an extension that connects naturally to the off-policy policy gradient formulations discussed in the preceding section.

Architectures that introduce additional components beyond the standard actor-critic dyad have also been explored. The **actor-director-critic** framework adds a director network responsible for classifying action quality, guiding the actor to reduce redundant exploration of low-quality actions in early training while simultaneously employing an improved double estimator to mitigate overestimation [117]. Similarly, the **Actor-Advisor** architecture separates the critic into a policy-aligned actor updated via Monte Carlo returns and an off-policy advisor providing Q-function-based guidance, combining the unbiasedness of Monte Carlo estimation with the efficiency of off-policy learning [118].

### Summary

Actor-critic architectures have established themselves as one of the most productive and versatile paradigms in deep reinforcement learning, situated at the intersection of the policy gradient theory developed in the preceding section and the deep neural network function approximators explored in the section that follows. By combining value function estimation with direct policy optimization through advantage-based learning signals and TD errors, they achieve a compelling balance between variance reduction and computational efficiency that neither pure policy gradient nor pure value-based methods can match alone. The rich body of theoretical work on their convergence properties, combined with continuous architectural innovations addressing stability, decoupling, and generalization, ensures that actor-critic methods will remain central to the advancement of deep reinforcement learning — and their success depends critically on the expressive capacity of the neural network architectures that instantiate both actor and critic, the topic to which we turn next.

### 1.6 Deep Neural Networks as Function Approximators

## 1.6 Deep Neural Network Architectures as Function Approximators

The integration of deep neural networks (DNNs) into reinforcement learning as function approximators represents one of the most transformative developments in the field, building directly on the actor-critic architectures and advantage estimation methods explored in the preceding section. While actor-critic methods provide the algorithmic scaffolding for combining value estimation with policy optimization, their practical power depends critically on the expressive capacity of the function approximators that instantiate both the actor and the critic. DNNs fulfill this role by enabling agents to operate effectively in high-dimensional state and action spaces that were previously intractable for classical tabular or linear methods. Rather than maintaining explicit lookup tables for value functions or policies, DRL agents employ DNNs to generalize across states, learning compact and powerful representations from raw sensory data — representations whose quality and structure, as we will see, have profound implications for the stability and generalization properties discussed in the preceding section and for the exploration strategies examined in the section that follows.

### Convolutional Neural Networks for Visual Inputs

The seminal demonstration of DNNs as value function approximators came with the application of convolutional neural networks (CNNs) to learn control policies directly from raw pixel inputs [119]. In this landmark work, a CNN was trained end-to-end with a variant of Q-learning to estimate action-value functions from raw game frames, achieving superhuman performance on several Atari 2600 games without any hand-crafted feature engineering. This result established CNNs as the de facto architecture for processing visual observations in DRL, leveraging their hierarchical feature extraction capabilities to transform high-dimensional pixel arrays into compact, task-relevant representations — precisely the kind of shared representations that actor-critic architectures rely upon for both policy and value function learning.

CNNs exploit spatial locality and translational equivariance through shared convolutional filters, making them particularly well-suited for visual reinforcement learning environments. Further advances explored equivariant CNN architectures that respect symmetry transformations present in environments, demonstrating that incorporating structural inductive biases can dramatically enhance sample efficiency and performance [120]. Beyond raw performance, representation quality plays a critical role: empirical analyses have shown that a well-organized internal representation within the policy network is a prerequisite for learning good action-selection, and a poor initial representation can cause unrecoverable collapse during training [121]. This finding resonates directly with the arguments raised in the preceding section regarding the risks of shared actor-critic representations and the value of maintaining representational quality throughout training.

### Recurrent Architectures for Partial Observability

A fundamental limitation of feedforward networks is their inability to maintain memory across time steps, rendering them insufficient for partially observable environments where the current observation alone does not fully characterize the underlying state. Recurrent neural networks (RNNs), and particularly Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM) networks, address this by maintaining hidden states that summarize relevant history. The Action-specific Deep Recurrent Q-Network (ADRQN) architecture encodes sequences of action-observation pairs through an LSTM layer to learn latent state representations, enabling effective Q-value estimation in partially observable domains such as flickering Atari games [122]. Similarly, recurrent convolutional neural network (RCNN) architectures have been proposed to naturally represent RL problems, with their forward passes efficiently executing value iteration and belief propagation in partially observable settings [123].

Memory-augmented architectures extend this further by combining spatial structure with recurrent processing. The Neural Map architecture introduces a spatially structured 2D memory image that allows agents to store and retrieve information over long time horizons, surpassing standard LSTM-based memories in challenging maze navigation tasks [124]. These recurrent and memory-augmented architectures are essential when agents must integrate information over extended temporal windows to infer the true underlying state — a challenge that arises frequently in the partially observable settings where the exploration strategies discussed in the following section must operate without access to complete state information.

### Function Approximation for Value Functions, Policies, and Models

DNNs serve as universal function approximators across three core components of DRL systems. As value function approximators, DNNs parameterize Q-functions or state-value functions, mapping states (and potentially actions) to scalar estimates of expected return — directly instantiating the critic component of actor-critic architectures and providing the TD error signals that drive policy improvement. As policy approximators, DNNs directly parameterize stochastic or deterministic policies that map states to action distributions or specific actions, instantiating the actor. As model approximators, DNNs can learn transition dynamics and reward functions of the environment, enabling model-based planning and the model-augmented actor-critic extensions discussed in the preceding section [123].

The representational capacity of DNNs is critical for handling continuous and high-dimensional action spaces. Research has demonstrated the benefit of learning dedicated action representations alongside state representations, extending representation learning from states to actions to improve policy generalization in large-scale continuous action spaces [125]. Structured action space representations, such as hypergraph networks that leverage the combinatorial structure of multi-dimensional discrete action spaces, further demonstrate how architectural inductive biases in DNNs can improve action-value estimation [126].

Value-consistent representation learning methods have been proposed to align learned representations more directly with the objectives of decision-making rather than purely perceptual accuracy, training models to produce consistent action-value predictions across imagined and real states [127]. Additionally, regularization approaches that encourage distinguishable representations across adjacent time steps have been shown to significantly improve performance and sample efficiency across multiple benchmark domains [128]. These representation alignment techniques connect naturally to the concerns raised in the preceding section about critic overfitting and the need for appropriate regularization in the value network — both problems that ultimately trace back to the representational properties of the underlying neural architecture.

### Handling High-Dimensional and Structured Spaces

The expressive power of DNNs allows DRL agents to operate across a diverse range of input modalities and action space structures. Graph neural networks (GNNs) have been integrated as function approximators when inputs possess relational or graph-structured properties, enabling generalization across varying network topologies in routing optimization tasks [129]. For parameterized or hybrid action spaces combining discrete choices with continuous parameters, deep network architectures have been extended to handle these structured outputs, enabling DRL in domains such as simulated RoboCup soccer [130] and multi-agent settings with discrete-continuous hybrid action spaces [131].

Low-dimensional observation filters and pre-processing pipelines can complement DNN approximators by reducing the effective dimensionality of visual inputs, supporting agents in visually complex environments where raw pixel processing is prohibitively expensive [132]. Furthermore, factored action space representations decompose control policies into independent components corresponding to compositional action dimensions, allowing agents to simultaneously learn about multiple actions and improving sample efficiency in structured action spaces [133]. The structured representations enabled by these architectural choices directly influence the quality of the exploration signal available to the agent — a consideration that becomes central in the following section's treatment of count-based and curiosity-driven exploration methods, which depend on the agent's ability to detect and respond to state novelty in high-dimensional spaces.

### Generalization and Robustness of Neural Representations

Despite their power, DNN function approximators in DRL are susceptible to overfitting, distribution shift, and poor generalization to unseen environments — concerns that parallel the instability and non-stationarity challenges identified in the context of experience replay and target networks in the preceding section. Network randomization techniques, which apply random perturbations to input observations through randomized convolutional networks, have been shown to improve generalization by encouraging agents to learn robust, invariant features [134]. Similarly, policy and environment representation decoupling, combined with contrastive learning objectives, enables agents trained on offline experiences to rapidly adapt to new environments with different dynamics [135].

The choice of neural architecture also has profound implications for computational efficiency and deployability. Asymmetric architectures that switch between computationally expensive and lightweight policies have been proposed to reduce inference costs without sacrificing performance, addressing the practical constraint that mobile and embedded platforms cannot sustain high-energy DNN inference [136].
