This concern for computational tractability anticipates the efficiency challenges that arise in the following section's discussion of exploration, where the cost of generating and evaluating intrinsic motivation signals must be weighed against their benefit to sample efficiency.

### Summary

Deep neural networks have fundamentally transformed reinforcement learning by providing flexible, high-capacity function approximators capable of processing diverse input modalities, handling large and structured action spaces, and learning compact representations that support generalization and transfer. The careful design of neural architectures — including CNNs for visual inputs, recurrent networks for partial observability, graph networks for relational structures, and factored representations for compositional action spaces — both enables and constrains the actor-critic algorithms, stability mechanisms, and exploration strategies that constitute the core of modern DRL. The representational properties of these networks determine whether actor-critic training converges reliably, whether exploration signals are informative and robust, and whether learned policies generalize beyond the training distribution. Understanding these architectural foundations is therefore essential context for the exploration methods examined in the following section, which must navigate the high-dimensional representational spaces that DNNs create while managing the fundamental tension between exploiting accumulated knowledge and seeking out novel, informative experiences.

### 1.7 Exploration and Exploitation in Deep Reinforcement Learning

## 1.7 Exploration and Exploitation in Deep Reinforcement Learning

The exploration-exploitation dilemma stands as one of the most fundamental and enduring challenges in reinforcement learning, arising naturally from the same deep neural network function approximators and training instabilities discussed in previous sections. An agent must continually decide whether to exploit its current knowledge to maximize immediate reward or to explore novel states and actions that may yield better long-term outcomes. Striking the right balance is critical: insufficient exploration leads to suboptimal policies trapped in local optima, while excessive exploration wastes valuable interactions and slows convergence. This tension becomes particularly acute in deep reinforcement learning, where the high-dimensional state and action spaces enabled by deep neural networks are vast and rewards may be extremely sparse—and where the stability mechanisms introduced in the preceding section, such as experience replay and target networks, interact intimately with how exploration is conducted.

### Classical Exploration Strategies

The simplest and most widely used exploration strategy is epsilon-greedy, in which an agent selects a random action with probability epsilon and its currently best-known action otherwise. Despite its simplicity, epsilon-greedy and its variants remain competitive across a broad range of domains [137]. However, a core limitation of epsilon-greedy is its temporally unstructured nature: each exploratory action is selected independently, leading to a random walk that fails to generate coherent, directed exploration trajectories. To address this, temporally extended exploration strategies repeat sampled actions for a random duration drawn from distributions inspired by animal foraging behavior, substantially improving the ability to escape local optima [138].

Boltzmann (softmax) exploration offers a probabilistic alternative by sampling actions proportionally to their exponentiated estimated values, allowing uncertainty to modulate exploration intensity. Empirical studies in partially observable settings, such as autonomous driving scenarios, suggest that Boltzmann and Max-Boltzmann strategies can outperform standard epsilon-greedy in balancing the exploration-exploitation trade-off [139]. A more principled adaptive strategy is provided by Bayesian epsilon-greedy methods, which interpret epsilon as a measure of Q-value uniformity and update it via Bayesian model combination, providing monotone convergence guarantees and data-driven adaptation [140].

Beyond these foundational methods, exploration-conscious criteria reformulate the optimization problem itself to reflect the exploration mechanism, resulting in surrogate MDPs whose optimal policies are explicitly calibrated to the exploration strategy in use [141]. This perspective treats exploration not merely as a heuristic add-on but as an integral aspect of the policy optimization objective—a view that connects naturally to the stability concerns raised in the preceding section, since poorly designed exploration can exacerbate the non-stationarity of the data distribution and undermine convergence.

### Count-Based and Density-Based Exploration

Count-based exploration methods provide a theoretically grounded approach by assigning exploration bonuses inversely proportional to the visitation count of states or state-action pairs. In tabular settings, these bonuses provably lead to near-optimal exploration. Extending this framework to high-dimensional, non-tabular settings requires generalizing counts beyond their literal definition—a challenge directly analogous to the representation learning problems encountered when applying deep neural networks as function approximators. A seminal approach derives pseudo-counts from arbitrary density models, enabling count-based exploration with raw pixel inputs in Atari environments and yielding dramatic improvements on hard exploration games such as Montezuma's Revenge [142]. A complementary approach computes generalized phi-pseudocounts by leveraging the feature representation used for value function approximation, awarding higher bonuses to states with less frequently observed features and achieving strong performance on difficult Atari benchmarks [143].

E-values generalize state visit counters by propagating exploratory value across state-action trajectories in a model-free manner, enabling directed exploration through temporal credit assignment [144]. Exploration potential extends these ideas further by measuring how thoroughly an agent has explored its environment class while accounting for the problem's reward structure, yielding a criterion that is both necessary and sufficient for asymptotic optimality [145].

### Intrinsic Motivation and Curiosity-Driven Exploration

When extrinsic rewards are sparse or absent, intrinsic motivation provides an alternative signal to drive exploration by rewarding agents for encountering novel, surprising, or informative experiences. This paradigm draws inspiration from developmental psychology, where curiosity and surprise serve as powerful internal drivers of learning [146]. Critically, intrinsic rewards interact directly with the stability mechanisms discussed previously: they augment the reward signal and therefore influence the quality and distribution of data in the replay buffer, the variance of TD targets, and the representational properties of the learned value function.

Surprise-based intrinsic motivation approximates the KL-divergence between the true environment transition dynamics and a learned model, yielding exploration bonuses that reflect how much the agent's model is violated by new experiences [147]. Closely related approaches use neural network dynamics models to generate exploration bonuses in high-dimensional spaces, enabling scalable curiosity-driven exploration in complex Atari environments [148]. The information content of trajectories can also serve as an exploration objective, encouraging agents to maximize the information gained along their path and generalizing to a discretely compressed latent space for improved sample efficiency [149].

Maximizing information gain in the latent feature space of a value function provides a theoretically grounded and computationally efficient approach to intrinsic motivation, leveraging Bayesian information-theoretic methods to improve exploration in continuous control tasks [150]. Bayesian curiosity approaches employ Bayesian linear regression over latent state embeddings to generate uncertainty-driven intrinsic rewards, demonstrating improved sample efficiency on simulated and physical robotic systems [151]. Implicit generative models based on amortized Stein Variational Gradient Descent approximate Bayesian uncertainty over environment dynamics, consistently outperforming ensemble-based exploration baselines on challenging sparse-reward tasks [152].

Renyi entropy maximization over the state-action space provides a theoretically motivated exploration objective for reward-free reinforcement learning, where an agent must collect data without knowledge of the downstream reward function [153; 146]. The successor-predecessor framework enriches intrinsic exploration by combining prospective information about future state distributions with retrospective information about transition sequences, enabling structure-aware exploration that is more efficient and ethologically plausible than purely forward-looking methods [154].

Graph-structured environments motivate curiosity formulations grounded in network theories of human exploration, where intrinsic rewards target topological features such as information gap and compression progress, enabling agents to generalize exploration strategies to larger and more complex graph environments [155]. Exploration objectives based on reward prediction error distinguish between environments where state novelty correlates with improved reward and those where it does not, providing an exploration signal more directly aligned with the agent's extrinsic objective [156].

### Uncertainty-Driven and Thompson Sampling Approaches

Uncertainty estimation over value functions provides a principled basis for exploration, complementing the variance reduction and representation regularization techniques discussed in the context of training stability. Inducing a distribution over temporal difference errors isolates epistemic uncertainty in value estimates, enabling a dedicated exploration policy that seeks high-uncertainty transitions and produces a curriculum that naturally vanishes as learning converges [157]. Model-based Bayesian exploration explicitly represents uncertainty over model parameters and computes a myopic approximation to the value of information for each action, directly balancing exploration and exploitation based on estimated knowledge gain [158].

Thompson sampling and its deep learning approximations offer another avenue for uncertainty-driven exploration, but approximate posterior methods in deep networks have been shown to underperform in bandit settings. Sample Average Uncertainty (SAU) provides a computationally efficient frequentist alternative that directly estimates outcome uncertainty from value predictions, asymptotically matching Thompson Sampling's uncertainty estimates and regret bounds while scaling readily to deep networks [159]. Model-ensemble approaches evaluate action candidates based on both expected return and future observation novelty, assigning weighted exploitation based on model uncertainty to prevent error propagation in high-dimensional continuous control settings [160].

### Exploration Timing, Multi-Objective Formulations, and Transfer

Beyond the question of how to explore, recent work has addressed the question of when to explore. VDSC leverages the agent's internal state—specifically, value discrepancy and state visitation counts—to determine when exploration is warranted, outperforming both simple and sophisticated baselines across the Atari benchmark suite [137]. A multi-objective reinforcement learning framework treats exploration and exploitation as separate objectives optimized simultaneously, enabling principled control over the exploration-exploitation balance at the policy level without manual tuning of reward weighting [161]. Constrained optimization formulations such as Extrinsic-Intrinsic Policy Optimization (EIPO) automatically tune the importance of intrinsic rewards, suppressing curiosity when extrinsic rewards are sufficient and amplifying it when exploration is needed, yielding consistent performance improvements across sixty-one Atari games [162].

In continuous control settings, intrinsic motivation based on value function error maximization provides a directed exploration strategy that extends to large and diverse state spaces, unifying multiple exploration objectives under a single framework and significantly outperforming undirected noise-based strategies [163]. Goal-oriented trajectories replace single random action choices with random goal selection followed by directed movement, generating longer and more coherent exploratory paths compatible with curiosity-based methods and off-policy agents [164]. Transfer-guided exploration leverages bisimulation distances between related task-environments to lower-bound the optimal advantage of state-action pairs, prioritizing actions with potentially higher returns and complementing traditional exploration algorithms [165].

Learning exploration bonuses from human demonstrations via inverse reinforcement learning enables the transfer of complex motivational structures—including notions of curiosity, safety, and style—to artificial agents, providing richer exploration incentives than novelty alone [166]. Exploration strategies designed for the offline setting, where data collection and policy learning are decoupled, reveal distinct challenges: curiosity-based intrinsic motivation can guide task-agnostic data collection that supports effective offline policy learning across diverse downstream tasks [167]. In multi-agent settings, influence-based exploration methods use mutual information and the value of interaction to encourage agents to coordinate their exploration, significantly improving team performance on sparse-reward cooperative tasks [168; 169].

Experience replay buffer sampling itself can be viewed through an exploration-exploitation lens, with uncertainty in Q-value estimates guiding selection toward more informative transitions and improving convergence and peak performance on classical control benchmarks [170]. This connection between exploration and replay buffer design reinforces the observation made in the preceding section that the distribution of training data is as consequential as the algorithmic update rule. The robustness of exploration methods to noisy or distracting inputs can be enhanced by learning compressed, temporally coherent representations via sequential information bottleneck objectives, enabling intrinsic rewards that capture task-relevant state novelty and resist spurious environmental perturbations [171].

Cognitive science perspectives suggest that human exploration efficiency arises from optimal use of limited computational resources, and meta-learning resource-rational reinforcement learning algorithms that trade performance for description length captures human exploration behavior more accurately than classical strategies such as Boltzmann exploration, UCB, or Thompson sampling [172]. Products of curiosity-driven exploration can also be repurposed for goal-conditioned offline planning by combining model-based planning over learned value landscapes with graph-based value aggregation, correcting estimation artifacts and enabling zero-shot goal-reaching across diverse simulated environments [173].

Together, these methods reflect the rich landscape of exploration strategies in deep reinforcement learning—from simple epsilon-greedy and Boltzmann heuristics to sophisticated uncertainty-driven, information-theoretic, and multi-objective formulations. Each approach makes distinct trade-offs between theoretical grounding, computational cost, and empirical effectiveness across diverse task settings. The choice of exploration strategy is inseparable from the broader design decisions surrounding neural architecture, training stability, and data distribution that have been examined throughout this chapter, and understanding these interactions is essential for building DRL agents that are both sample-efficient and reliably convergent.

### 1.8 Stability, Convergence, and Key Training Mechanisms

## Training Stability and Convergence in Deep Reinforcement Learning

Training deep reinforcement learning agents presents a constellation of stability and convergence challenges that do not arise in standard supervised learning. The fundamental difficulty stems from the interplay of three interacting sources of instability: function approximation with deep neural networks, bootstrapping via temporal difference methods, and the non-stationary nature of the data distribution generated by an evolving policy. Understanding and mitigating these instabilities has been central to the practical success of modern DRL algorithms, and the mechanisms developed to address them—experience replay, target networks, gradient regularization, and variance reduction—lay the groundwork for the exploration strategies examined in the following section, since the distribution and quality of training data are as consequential as the algorithmic update rule itself.

### The Mean Squared Bellman Error and Its Optimization

At the heart of value-based DRL lies the minimization of the Mean Squared Bellman Error (MSBE), which measures how well a parameterized value function satisfies the Bellman consistency equations. Despite its seemingly straightforward formulation, optimizing the MSBE is far from trivial. The target values used in the Bellman backup themselves depend on the current network parameters, creating a moving target problem that renders standard gradient descent unstable. From an optimization perspective, the MSBE is a highly non-convex objective, and the use of semi-gradient algorithms—where gradients are not taken through the target—introduces incomplete gradient information that can lead to divergence [174]. Theoretical analysis reveals that suboptimal local minima can be avoided under over-parameterization when global minima exist, and that second-order information from the full gradient of the MSBE, rather than semi-gradient approximations, is necessary for locally quadratic convergence [174].

### Experience Replay

One of the most impactful mechanisms for stabilizing DRL training is experience replay, which stores past transitions in a replay buffer and samples mini-batches of experiences for training. This approach breaks the temporal correlations inherent in sequentially collected data, effectively transforming the highly correlated online data stream into an approximately independent and identically distributed training set more amenable to stochastic gradient optimization. Beyond decorrelating data, replay buffers improve sample efficiency by allowing each transition to be reused multiple times, reducing the number of environment interactions required for convergence.

However, not all experiences are equally informative. Prioritized experience replay addresses this by sampling transitions proportionally to their temporal difference (TD) error, focusing learning on the most surprising or erroneous experiences. While prioritized replay has proven highly effective in value-based methods, its naive application to actor-critic algorithms creates complications: transitions with large TD errors are not necessarily informative for policy improvement, and training the actor network on such transitions can cause the approximate policy gradient to diverge from the gradient under the optimal Q-function [175]. This motivates the design of actor-aware sampling frameworks that schedule distinct training distributions for actor and critic networks, yielding significant improvements in continuous control settings [175].

The composition of the replay buffer and the distribution of experiences sampled from it fundamentally affect the learning dynamics. It has been shown that Q-learning algorithms can exhibit pathological interactions between the collected data distribution and the policy induced by training, leading to instability and poor performance under noisy, sparse, or delayed reward signals [176]. The DisCor algorithm addresses this by computing and applying a corrective reweighting to the training distribution, demonstrating that the choice of data distribution is as critical as the algorithmic update rule itself [176]. This insight—that replay buffer management is inseparable from training stability—connects directly to the exploration-exploitation considerations addressed in the following section, where the distribution of collected data is shaped not only by algorithmic choices but also by the agent's exploration policy.

### Target Networks

A second cornerstone of stable DRL training is the target network, a periodically synchronized copy of the main value network used exclusively for computing TD targets. By holding the target fixed for a number of update steps before synchronizing, the target network breaks the destabilizing feedback loop between the current Q-function estimate and the targets it is trained to match. Without this mechanism, small changes in the value function parameters immediately alter the targets, creating a moving goalposts problem that frequently leads to divergent or oscillatory behavior.

While target networks are indispensable for stabilizing Q-learning, their periodic nature introduces a delay in value function updates that can slow learning. This trade-off between stability and learning speed motivates alternatives such as self-regularized TD-learning, where the target network is replaced with a regularization mechanism that prevents divergence without sacrificing update frequency [177]. The GRAC algorithm demonstrates that self-regularized critics can match or exceed the performance of target-network-based methods while enabling more responsive value function updates [177].

### Gradient Clipping and Regularization

Gradient clipping is a widely used practical technique that prevents exploding gradients from destabilizing the optimization of deep networks during RL training. By capping the norm or individual components of the gradient vector before applying parameter updates, gradient clipping ensures that no single large TD error drives the parameters far from their current values. This is particularly important in environments with rare but high-magnitude rewards or transitions.

Beyond gradient clipping, regularization of the value function estimator itself plays a critical role in training stability. Spectral normalization, which constrains the Lipschitz constant of individual network layers, has been shown to substantially stabilize DRL training; applying spectral normalization to a Categorical-DQN agent yields performance comparable to the significantly more complex Rainbow agent on challenging Atari benchmarks [178]. This finding suggests that the neural network's learning dynamics, not just the RL objective, are a primary driver of performance, and that constraining the function approximator's capacity through regularization can substitute for elaborate algorithmic improvements [178].

Representation regularization offers another perspective on stability. The representations learned by the Q-network and its target network should, in theory, remain distinguishable across adjacent time steps; violations of this property correspond to representation collapse and can lead to suboptimal policies. Explicit regularization of internal representations to enforce this distinguishable representation property, as in the PEER framework, has been shown to significantly improve both performance and sample efficiency across diverse benchmarks [128].

### Variance Reduction and Estimation Error

A persistent challenge in DRL is the accumulation of approximation errors through bootstrapping. Each Bellman update introduces estimation error, which propagates through the value function and can compound into large biases over many iterations. The Averaged-DQN algorithm addresses this by maintaining and averaging multiple historical Q-value estimates, reducing the variance of the target values used in updates [179]. This simple extension yields substantially more stable training and improved performance by smoothing out fluctuations in the target signal [179].

The update-to-data ratio—the number of gradient updates performed per environment step—is another critical axis for balancing sample efficiency and stability. Increasing the replay ratio improves data utilization but exacerbates plasticity loss, where the network's ability to represent new information degrades over training. Research has shown that the critic network's plasticity loss is the principal bottleneck in visual reinforcement learning, and that catastrophic plasticity loss in early training phases can be irreversible [180]. Adaptive strategies that dynamically adjust the replay ratio based on the critic's current plasticity level can avoid early catastrophic collapse while benefiting from higher reuse rates in later training stages [180]. More broadly, the loss of plasticity in value-based DRL agents cycling through non-stationary tasks has been shown to cause network activations to become sparser and gradients to diminish, with simple architectural interventions such as the CReLU activation function offering effective mitigation [181].

### Non-Stationarity and Reward Signal Quality

The non-stationary nature of the data distribution in DRL, arising from both policy updates and environmental dynamics, creates additional convergence challenges. In multi-agent or time-varying environments, stored transitions in the replay buffer can quickly become outdated as other agents or environmental parameters change. Leniency mechanisms that apply optimistic updates to outdated transitions in multi-agent replay buffers have been shown to facilitate convergence to cooperative behaviors [182].

The quality of the reward signal itself also significantly affects training stability. Noisy, misinterpreted, or failed observations in real-world deployments introduce variance into the reward signal that can disrupt convergence. Approaches that augment existing DRL models with a subtask to directly estimate the variance contained in the reward signal allow the agent to learn feature representations that are robust to reward noise, stabilizing training convergence in environments with unreliable sensors [183].

### Theoretical Insights and the Corrective Feedback Perspective

A deeper theoretical understanding of DRL training dynamics reveals that the lack of corrective feedback is a fundamental driver of instability. In supervised learning, on-policy data collection provides hard negatives that directly correct the model in states the policy is likely to visit. Q-learning and related methods do not inherently benefit from this corrective feedback, making them susceptible to compounding errors in the value function estimate [176]. Recognizing training stability as fundamentally a data distribution problem, rather than purely an algorithmic one, motivates distribution-correction approaches that reshape the training data to provide the corrective feedback missing from standard experience replay [176].

In summary, the stability and convergence of DRL training rest on a carefully orchestrated combination of mechanisms: experience replay for data decorrelation and reuse, target networks and self-regularization for stable Bellman targets, gradient clipping for bounded updates, representation regularization for preventing feature collapse, variance reduction through averaging and multi-step returns, and adaptive strategies for managing plasticity. Each component addresses a distinct failure mode arising from the interaction of deep neural networks with the bootstrapped, non-stationary learning process that characterizes reinforcement learning. Theoretical analysis increasingly illuminates why these components are not mere engineering heuristics but essential ingredients for reliable DRL training. Crucially, these stability mechanisms do not operate in isolation from the rest of the learning system: as the following section makes clear, the design of exploration strategies is deeply intertwined with the data distribution maintained in the replay buffer, the variance of TD targets, and the representational properties of the learned value function—making training stability and exploration two sides of the same coin in practical DRL systems.

## 2 Core Algorithmic Advances in Deep Reinforcement Learning

### 2.1 Value-Based Deep Reinforcement Learning Methods

## 2.1 Value-Based Deep Reinforcement Learning Methods

Value-based deep reinforcement learning methods represent one of the most foundational and extensively studied families of algorithms in the field. These methods learn to approximate an optimal action-value function (Q-function) that maps state-action pairs to expected cumulative rewards, enabling agents to derive greedy policies without explicitly parameterizing or storing the policy itself. The integration of deep neural networks as function approximators for value functions has been transformative, enabling agents to operate directly from high-dimensional sensory inputs such as raw pixel observations. Understanding these value-based approaches provides essential grounding for the policy gradient methods discussed subsequently, as many modern algorithms draw on concepts from both families.

### The Deep Q-Network (DQN) Foundation

The Deep Q-Network (DQN) marked a watershed moment in deep reinforcement learning by demonstrating that a convolutional neural network could learn to approximate the optimal Q-function directly from raw pixel inputs across a diverse range of Atari 2600 games. DQN combined Q-learning with two critical stabilization mechanisms: experience replay, which breaks temporal correlations in training data by storing and randomly sampling past transitions, and target networks, which provide stable training targets by periodically copying the online network weights to a separate target network. These two mechanisms addressed the well-known instability problems that arise when applying neural network function approximation to temporal difference learning, where the non-stationarity of both the data distribution and the learning targets could cause divergence.

Despite its success, DQN suffers from several well-documented limitations. Chief among these is the overestimation bias in Q-value estimation, which arises because the max operator used in the Bellman target simultaneously selects and evaluates actions using the same network. This systematic bias can accumulate over training and lead to suboptimal policies. Additionally, DQN's single-stream architecture does not distinguish between estimating the value of a state and the advantage of specific actions within that state, potentially leading to inefficient value estimation in environments where many actions have similar consequences. These limitations motivated a series of targeted improvements, each addressing a specific failure mode of the original DQN formulation.

### Double DQN: Addressing Overestimation Bias

Double DQN directly addresses the overestimation bias inherent in standard DQN by decoupling the action selection and action evaluation steps in the Bellman target computation. Rather than using the target network to both select and evaluate the greedy action, Double DQN uses the online network to select the action with the highest Q-value and then uses the target network to evaluate that action's value. This simple modification substantially reduces the upward bias in Q-value estimates and has been shown empirically to yield more accurate value estimates and improved policy performance across many Atari benchmarks. The theoretical justification rests on the observation that while the max operator over noisy estimates produces systematically inflated values, the decomposed estimator in Double DQN achieves a more balanced and lower-variance estimate.

### Dueling DQN: Separating Value and Advantage

The Dueling DQN architecture introduces a structural inductive bias into the neural network by separating the computation of the state value function V(s) and the action advantage function A(s,a) into two distinct streams that share a common feature extraction backbone before being combined to produce Q-value estimates. This decomposition is motivated by the insight that in many states, the precise identity of the chosen action has little impact on the outcome, and learning the state value independently can accelerate learning in such situations. The two streams are combined using a carefully designed aggregation operator that ensures identifiability of the separate components while maintaining the Q-value representation. This architectural modification improves the accuracy of Q-value estimates, particularly for actions that do not affect the environment in meaningful ways, and has been shown to boost performance across a wide range of environments without any changes to the underlying training algorithm. Notably, the explicit separation of state value and action advantage in Dueling DQN anticipates the actor-critic architecture used in policy gradient methods, where similar decompositions play a central role.

### Averaged-DQN: Variance Reduction through Ensemble Averaging

A complementary approach to reducing instability in Q-value estimation is Averaged-DQN, which maintains a collection of recent target network snapshots and averages their Q-value predictions when computing Bellman targets. By averaging over multiple past estimates rather than relying on a single target network, Averaged-DQN reduces the variance of the target values used during training. This variance reduction translates into more stable gradient updates and ultimately improved performance and convergence reliability. The key insight is that the instability in DQN training is substantially driven by the high variance of bootstrapped targets, and averaging over an ensemble of historical estimates provides a simple and computationally efficient mechanism to dampen this variance without fundamentally altering the algorithmic structure.

### Prioritized Experience Replay

Prioritized experience replay augments the standard uniform sampling strategy of the DQN replay buffer by assigning higher sampling probabilities to transitions that are associated with larger temporal difference errors. The intuition is that transitions with high TD errors represent experiences from which the agent can still learn substantially, and preferentially replaying these transitions improves data efficiency. This approach connects closely to the broader challenge of improving sample efficiency in value-based methods, as the replay buffer in DQN and its variants constitutes a key mechanism for decoupling data collection from policy updates [9]. The concept of selectively reusing past experience also foreshadows the importance sampling corrections employed in off-policy policy gradient methods, discussed in the following subsection.

### Rainbow: Integrating Multiple Improvements

Rainbow represents a systematic effort to combine multiple independent improvements to DQN into a single unified algorithm. The components integrated in Rainbow include Double DQN for reducing overestimation bias, Dueling DQN for architectural improvements, prioritized experience replay for more efficient use of stored transitions, multi-step returns for better reward propagation, distributional Q-learning for richer value representations, and noisy networks for improved exploration. The empirical evaluation of Rainbow demonstrated that the combination of all these components achieves substantially better performance than any individual component alone, and also revealed important interactions between the different improvements. Notably, multi-step returns and prioritized replay were found to contribute most significantly to performance gains, while the distributional component provided consistent improvements by modeling the full distribution of returns rather than only the expected value.

### Theoretical Considerations and Connections to MDP Fundamentals

The design and analysis of value-based DRL methods are deeply rooted in the theoretical framework of Markov Decision Processes and Bellman equations. The Bellman optimality operator provides the theoretical foundation for Q-learning updates, and the convergence of tabular Q-learning under appropriate conditions motivates the use of neural network approximations in DQN and its variants. However, the introduction of function approximation fundamentally changes the theoretical landscape, as the combination of bootstrapping, off-policy training, and function approximation — the so-called "deadly triad" — can lead to divergence. The mechanisms introduced in DQN (experience replay and target networks) and subsequently refined in Double DQN, Dueling DQN, and Rainbow can be understood as practical engineering solutions to the instabilities that arise from this theoretical tension.

The challenge of managing large state and action spaces, which is central to the motivation for DQN and its successors, arises directly from the curse of dimensionality that plagues tabular MDP solvers. When states and actions scale to high-dimensional or continuous spaces, exact dynamic programming approaches become computationally intractable, and deep neural network function approximators provide the representational capacity needed to generalize across vast state spaces from limited experience [9]. The architectural innovations in Dueling DQN and the algorithmic refinements in Double DQN and Averaged-DQN represent targeted solutions to the specific failure modes of neural Q-learning, collectively advancing the reliability and performance of value-based DRL to a level that enables deployment in complex, real-world environments. These challenges of scalability and stability in value-based methods also motivate the policy gradient approaches covered in the next subsection, which address similar concerns through direct policy optimization rather than indirect value estimation.

### Summary

Value-based deep reinforcement learning has evolved substantially from the foundational DQN algorithm through a series of targeted improvements addressing overestimation bias (Double DQN), architectural inefficiency (Dueling DQN), variance in target estimation (Averaged-DQN), sample efficiency (prioritized experience replay), and multi-component integration (Rainbow). Each innovation has been motivated by careful empirical and theoretical analysis of the failure modes of its predecessors, collectively establishing a rich family of algorithms that form the backbone of modern DRL practice in discrete and structured action spaces. The limitations that persist in these value-based approaches — particularly in continuous action spaces and with respect to sample efficiency — provide direct motivation for the policy gradient methods examined in the following subsection, which offer complementary strengths and have become equally central to the deep reinforcement learning landscape.

### 2.2 Policy Gradient Methods in Deep Reinforcement Learning

## 2.2 Policy Gradient Methods

Policy gradient methods constitute a fundamental family of deep reinforcement learning algorithms that directly optimize a parameterized policy by ascending the gradient of expected cumulative reward. Unlike the value-based approaches examined in the previous subsection, which derive policies implicitly from estimated value functions, policy gradient methods operate in the policy space directly, making them naturally suited for continuous action spaces and stochastic policy representations. While value-based methods such as DQN and its variants excel in discrete action settings, the limitations they face in continuous and high-dimensional action spaces provide direct motivation for the policy gradient framework explored here. These methods also form the natural precursor to the actor-critic architectures discussed in the following subsection, which synthesize ideas from both families.

### REINFORCE and Foundational Policy Gradient Theory

The REINFORCE algorithm, introduced by Williams, represents the earliest and most fundamental policy gradient method. It estimates the policy gradient using Monte Carlo rollouts, computing the gradient as the expectation of the log-probability of taken actions weighted by their cumulative returns. While theoretically sound, REINFORCE suffers from extremely high variance in gradient estimates, as the return signal conflates contributions from all actions taken throughout an episode. This variance problem motivates the use of baselines—typically the value function—to reduce estimator variance without introducing bias. The policy gradient theorem formalizes this framework, establishing that the gradient of the expected return with respect to policy parameters can be expressed in terms of the action-value function [94; 184]. This foundational result generalizes naturally to non-linear utility objectives beyond simple cumulative reward maximization [94], and directly motivates the actor-critic decomposition that underpins the more advanced algorithms examined throughout this section and the next.

### Asynchronous and Synchronous Actor-Critic Methods: A3C and A2C

The Asynchronous Advantage Actor-Critic (A3C) algorithm addresses the sample inefficiency and high variance of REINFORCE by employing multiple parallel worker agents that asynchronously interact with independent environment copies and update a shared global policy. Each worker computes local gradient estimates using n-step returns combined with the advantage function—the difference between estimated action-value and state-value—thereby reducing variance relative to raw Monte Carlo returns. The asynchronous nature of updates provides implicit data decorrelation, circumventing the need for experience replay that characterizes the value-based methods discussed in the previous subsection. The synchronous variant, A2C, collects experience from all workers simultaneously before performing a single synchronized update, often achieving comparable or superior performance with more stable gradient estimates due to uniform batch statistics. Both methods leverage the actor-critic architecture wherein the actor network outputs the policy distribution while the critic network estimates the value function [184; 185]. This actor-critic paradigm, introduced here in the on-policy setting, is substantially extended in the following subsection to the off-policy regime and continuous action spaces through algorithms such as DDPG, TD3, and SAC.

### Trust Region Policy Optimization (TRPO)

A critical limitation of vanilla policy gradient methods is their sensitivity to step size selection: too large an update can catastrophically degrade policy performance, while too small an update results in slow convergence. Trust Region Policy Optimization (TRPO) addresses this challenge by constraining the policy update to a trust region defined by a KL-divergence bound between the old and new policy distributions. Formally, TRPO maximizes a surrogate objective—the importance-weighted ratio of new to old policy action probabilities times the advantage estimate—subject to a hard constraint that the mean KL divergence between consecutive policies does not exceed a specified threshold. This formulation provides monotonic improvement guarantees under certain conditions, ensuring that each policy update either improves or maintains performance. However, the computational cost of TRPO is substantial: enforcing the KL constraint requires computing the Fisher information matrix and solving a constrained optimization problem using conjugate gradient methods combined with a backtracking line search, making it challenging to scale to very large neural network architectures. The divergence-based regularization philosophy of TRPO connects directly to broader ideas in regularized policy iteration and mirror descent frameworks [186; 37].

### Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO)

Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) was developed as a computationally tractable alternative to TRPO that retains its key stability benefits without requiring second-order optimization. PPO introduces a clipped surrogate objective that implicitly enforces a soft trust region by truncating the policy update when the probability ratio between new and old policies deviates beyond a predefined threshold ε. Specifically, the PPO-Clip objective takes the minimum of the unclipped importance-weighted advantage and the clipped ratio multiplied by the advantage, preventing excessively large policy updates in either direction. This clipping mechanism acts as a first-order approximation to the TRPO constraint while remaining fully compatible with stochastic gradient descent. An alternative formulation, PPO-KL, adds a KL-divergence penalty term to the objective rather than clipping, with an adaptive coefficient that adjusts based on whether the observed KL divergence is above or below a target value. PPO has demonstrated strong empirical performance across a wide range of benchmark environments spanning both discrete control tasks, such as Atari games, and continuous locomotion tasks in MuJoCo, largely due to its favorable balance between implementation simplicity, computational efficiency, and training stability. The connection between PPO and physics-informed approaches has also been explored, with the Hamilton-Jacobi-Bellman Proximal Policy Optimization (HJBPPO) algorithm demonstrating that incorporating HJB equation constraints into the value network training of PPO can yield improved performance on continuous control benchmarks [187].

### Theoretical Properties and Convergence Analysis

Theoretical analysis of policy gradient methods has matured considerably in recent years, complementing the practical advances in TRPO and PPO with rigorous convergence guarantees. Global convergence guarantees have been established for actor-critic algorithms under both linear and deep neural network function approximation settings, demonstrating convergence to globally optimal policies at sublinear rates [185]. Policy mirror descent frameworks provide a unified theoretical lens for understanding regularized policy optimization, demonstrating linear convergence to global optima across a broad class of convex regularizers and Bregman divergence formulations [186]. The equivalence between regularized policy iteration and Newton-Raphson methods further clarifies the convergence behavior, showing global linear convergence at rate γ and quadratic convergence in local regions near the optimum [37]. These theoretical insights provide principled justifications for the empirical success of algorithms such as PPO and TRPO and suggest directions for further algorithmic improvement. Notably, these convergence results for on-policy methods are complemented in the following subsection by convergence analyses for off-policy actor-critic algorithms, together providing a more complete theoretical picture of the actor-critic family.

### Off-Policy Policy Gradient and Sample Efficiency

A persistent limitation of on-policy policy gradient methods such as REINFORCE, A3C, and PPO is their reliance on freshly collected experience, which renders them sample-inefficient in environments where data collection is costly. This limitation stands in contrast to the value-based methods of the previous subsection, which naturally accommodate off-policy data through experience replay. Off-policy extensions of the policy gradient framework allow the reuse of data collected under different behavior policies through importance sampling corrections. Nonparametric approaches have been proposed that construct closed-form estimates of value functions using kernel-based regression, enabling fully analytic policy gradient computations that demonstrate superior sample efficiency compared to standard on-policy methods [188]. The theoretical characterization of efficiency losses incurred when moving from on-policy to off-policy data collection has been studied through concentrability coefficients, providing a principled understanding of when off-policy reuse is beneficial [46]. The off-policy actor-critic methods examined in the following subsection—particularly DDPG, TD3, and SAC—represent the most prominent practical instantiations of this off-policy policy gradient philosophy applied to continuous action spaces.

### Empirical Performance and Benchmark Comparisons

Empirical evaluations across standard benchmark suites have consistently demonstrated the competitive performance of policy gradient methods relative to both value-based approaches and optimal control baselines. PPO achieves strong results on the Atari suite while remaining robust and computationally efficient. On continuous control benchmarks such as MuJoCo locomotion tasks, TRPO, PPO, and their variants generally outperform pure value-based approaches, with SAC—a policy gradient method augmented with entropy regularization discussed in detail in the following subsection—achieving state-of-the-art sample efficiency. Comparisons against optimal control baselines have revealed that when environments and reward functions are well-specified, structured optimal control methods can outperform policy gradient methods by several orders of magnitude in convergence speed, suggesting that incorporating control-theoretic structure into policy gradient frameworks remains a productive research direction [189]. Overall, the policy gradient family continues to represent one of the most practically successful and theoretically well-understood branches of deep reinforcement learning, forming the backbone of numerous state-of-the-art systems across robotics, game playing, and real-world decision-making domains, and providing the essential algorithmic foundations upon which the actor-critic methods of the following subsection are built.

### 2.3 Actor-Critic Architectures and Off-Policy Algorithms

Actor-critic architectures represent a powerful class of deep reinforcement learning algorithms that simultaneously learn a policy (actor) and a value function (critic), combining the strengths of policy gradient methods and value-based approaches. Building naturally upon the policy gradient foundations established in the previous section—where A3C and A2C introduced the actor-critic paradigm in the on-policy setting—this section examines the extension of actor-critic methods to the off-policy regime and continuous action spaces. By leveraging the critic as a low-variance baseline for policy updates, actor-critic methods have become the dominant paradigm for continuous control tasks, giving rise to a rich family of off-policy algorithms with increasingly sophisticated mechanisms for stability and efficiency.

## Deep Deterministic Policy Gradient and Its Foundations

The Deep Deterministic Policy Gradient (DDPG) algorithm extended the actor-critic framework to continuous action spaces by combining a deterministic policy gradient with deep neural network function approximators and experience replay. DDPG employs a target network for both the actor and critic to stabilize training, borrowing key insights from DQN-style methods. The critic is trained to minimize the Bellman error using off-policy transitions stored in a replay buffer, while the actor is updated via the deterministic policy gradient theorem. However, DDPG suffers from well-documented instability issues, including overestimation of Q-values, sensitivity to hyperparameters, and brittleness in the presence of function approximation errors. These challenges motivate a series of important extensions that form the backbone of modern continuous control methods.

## Twin Delayed Deep Deterministic Policy Gradient (TD3)

Twin Delayed Deep Deterministic Policy Gradient (TD3) addresses the overestimation bias inherent in DDPG through three key mechanisms. First, it employs a clipped double-Q trick by maintaining two separate critic networks and using the minimum of their outputs as the regression target, directly mitigating upward bias in value estimates. Second, TD3 introduces delayed policy updates, where the actor and target networks are updated less frequently than the critics, allowing the value function to stabilize before it is used to guide policy improvement. Third, target policy smoothing adds a small amount of noise to the target action during critic updates, regularizing the value function against sharp peaks that could destabilize learning. Together, these mechanisms substantially improve sample efficiency and stability compared to DDPG, making TD3 a strong baseline for continuous control benchmarks. Connections to these overestimation correction strategies are also explored in the context of value function learning [190], where jointly approximating both state-value and state-action-value functions can reduce overestimation bias compared to standard Q-function-only methods.

## Soft Actor-Critic (SAC) and Entropy Regularization

Soft Actor-Critic (SAC) introduces a principled entropy regularization framework, augmenting the standard reward maximization objective with a maximum entropy term that encourages the policy to remain as stochastic as possible while still achieving high returns. This entropy-augmented objective leads to policies that are inherently more exploratory, more robust to local optima, and better suited to tasks with multimodal reward landscapes. SAC operates in an off-policy setting, maintaining a replay buffer for sample-efficient learning, and employs the clipped double-Q trick from TD3 to address overestimation bias. The combination of entropy regularization, off-policy learning, and double critics makes SAC one of the most sample-efficient and stable algorithms available for continuous control.

The maximum entropy framework also has deep theoretical connections to distributional and soft value functions. The soft Bellman equation underpins SAC's critic update, and the policy is recovered in closed form as a Gibbs distribution over Q-values. This formulation is closely related to relative entropy regularization approaches [191], where normalizing flows are used to represent complex, multimodal policies whose closed-form optimal distributions can be derived from learned soft Q-values, removing the need for a separate policy optimization step. Similarly, Gaussian policy smoothing approaches [192] demonstrate that the gradients of expected reward with respect to the mean and covariance of a Gaussian policy can be recovered from the gradient and Hessian of a smoothed Q-value function, enabling direct policy updates from the value landscape and supporting proximal optimization via KL-divergence penalties.

## Convergence Properties and Off-Policy Stability

A fundamental challenge in actor-critic methods with function approximation is ensuring convergence under off-policy training. Classical actor-critic algorithms using state-action value functions can suffer from divergence when combined with function approximation and off-policy data due to the deadly triad. Recent work has addressed this by deriving actor-critic algorithms that use state-value functions rather than Q-functions for the actor update, circumventing the curse of dimensionality in large or continuous action spaces [71]. These convergent actor-critic methods guarantee convergence to the optimal solution of the averaged state-value objective while maintaining classical actor-critic desiderata without additional hyperparameters, representing an important theoretical advance toward reliable off-policy learning. These convergence results complement the global optimality guarantees for on-policy actor-critic methods discussed in the previous section, together providing a more complete theoretical picture of actor-critic algorithms across both on-policy and off-policy settings.

## Continuous-Time Perspectives and Unified Frameworks

Beyond the standard discrete-time formulation, continuous-time actor-critic frameworks offer additional theoretical insights. The q-learning perspective in continuous time [63] reveals that conventional Q-functions collapse in the continuous-time limit and must be replaced by instantaneous advantage rate functions (q-functions) related to the Hamiltonian of the underlying control problem. Actor-critic algorithms derived from this framework subsume both SARSA-style and policy gradient-based algorithms as special cases, providing a unifying perspective on the relationship between value-based and policy-based methods in continuous settings.

Additionally, variance reduction in the critic is a persistent concern in actor-critic training. Taylor TD-learning [55] proposes a model-based approach that analytically integrates over stochasticity in action selection and state transitions using a first-order Taylor series expansion of TD updates, yielding lower-variance critic estimates. Combining Taylor TD with TD3 produces the TaTD3 algorithm, which achieves competitive or superior performance compared to state-of-the-art model-free and model-based baselines on standard benchmarks, illustrating that model-based variance reduction can be seamlessly incorporated into off-policy actor-critic frameworks.

## Summary

Actor-critic architectures and off-policy algorithms have undergone rapid development, with DDPG, TD3, and SAC establishing the key design principles of replay-based off-policy learning, overestimation bias correction, delayed policy updates, and entropy regularization. Theoretical advances in convergent off-policy actor-critic methods, continuous-time formulations, and variance-reduced critic learning continue to refine our understanding of these algorithms, while novel policy parameterizations using normalizing flows and Gaussian smoothed value functions expand the expressive power of the actor. The entropy regularization framework central to SAC also provides a natural bridge to the following section on distributional reinforcement learning, where modeling the full return distribution—rather than merely its expectation or a soft variant thereof—offers yet richer learning signals and connects directly to risk-sensitive policy optimization. Together, these contributions form a comprehensive toolkit for stable, sample-efficient policy optimization in continuous action spaces.

### 2.4 Distributional Reinforcement Learning

## 2.4 Distributional Reinforcement Learning

Traditional reinforcement learning algorithms focus on estimating the *expected* cumulative return—a scalar summary statistic—and use this expectation to drive policy improvement. Distributional reinforcement learning (distributional RL) fundamentally departs from this paradigm by modeling the full probability distribution of returns rather than merely their mean. This richer representation captures the intrinsic stochasticity of the environment and provides a more informative learning signal, with far-reaching implications for performance, exploration, and risk-sensitive decision-making. While the actor-critic methods surveyed in the preceding section—particularly SAC—already incorporate entropy regularization as a means of enriching the learning objective beyond scalar expected returns, distributional RL takes this direction further by replacing the scalar value function itself with a full distributional object, offering a qualitatively different and complementary perspective on uncertainty and reward.

### Core Motivation and Theoretical Foundations

The central insight of distributional RL is that two policies may yield the same expected return while differing substantially in their variance, tail behavior, and overall distributional shape. By modeling the full return distribution, an agent can distinguish between a policy that reliably achieves moderate rewards and one that occasionally achieves high rewards but frequently fails catastrophically. This distinction is invisible to scalar value functions, yet highly relevant for real-world deployment in safety-critical or risk-sensitive domains.

Formally, rather than learning a value function $Q(s,a) = \mathbb{E}[193]$, distributional RL algorithms learn the random variable $Z(s,a)$ whose expectation equals $Q(s,a)$. The distributional Bellman operator governs the evolution of this random variable across time steps, and algorithms are designed to approximate it through various parametric distribution representations.

### C51: Categorical Distribution Representation

The categorical algorithm, widely known as C51, was among the first practical distributional RL algorithms to demonstrate that modeling full return distributions could substantially improve agent performance on the Atari benchmark suite. C51 discretizes the return distribution over a fixed support of atoms and learns a categorical probability distribution over those atoms using a distributional Bellman update combined with a projection step. The empirical success of C51 demonstrated that the auxiliary learning signal provided by distributional supervision—learning to predict not just where the expected return lies, but the entire probability mass function—acts as a powerful implicit regularizer and representation shaper. This connection between distributional learning and improved feature representations has since been widely recognized as a key mechanism underlying performance gains.

### QR-DQN: Quantile Regression for Distribution Estimation

Quantile Regression DQN (QR-DQN) takes a different approach to representing return distributions. Rather than fixing a support and learning probabilities, QR-DQN fixes a set of quantile levels and learns the corresponding quantile values through quantile regression loss. This approach avoids the projection step required by C51 and enjoys stronger theoretical properties, including an asymptotic contraction guarantee under the quantile regression Bellman operator. QR-DQN achieves a tighter approximation of the return distribution while remaining computationally tractable, and it further demonstrated that the choice of distribution representation has meaningful consequences for both approximation quality and downstream policy performance. The quantile regression perspective also connects distributional RL naturally to risk-sensitive optimization, since quantiles directly correspond to Value-at-Risk (VaR) measures used in finance and operations research.

### IQN: Implicit Quantile Networks

Implicit Quantile Networks (IQN) generalize the quantile regression approach of QR-DQN by learning an implicit representation of the full quantile function rather than a fixed finite set of quantile estimates. IQN embeds sampled quantile levels into the network via a learned encoding, enabling continuous and flexible sampling from the return distribution at any desired quantile. This implicit representation allows the network to approximate the full inverse cumulative distribution function with greater fidelity and enables principled risk-sensitive policy evaluation by integrating over selected regions of the distribution. IQN demonstrated state-of-the-art performance on Atari games at the time of its introduction and highlighted how the combination of distributional learning with flexible function approximation could yield further gains. The ability to condition on quantile inputs also opens the door to explicitly optimizing risk measures such as Conditional Value-at-Risk (CVaR) by restricting sampling to the lower tail of the return distribution during policy improvement.

### D4PG: Distributional RL for Continuous Control

While C51, QR-DQN, and IQN were primarily evaluated in discrete action settings, the Distributed Distributional Deterministic Policy Gradients (D4PG) algorithm extended distributional RL to continuous action spaces within a distributed actor-critic framework. D4PG combines a distributional critic—based on the categorical representation of C51—with deterministic policy gradients in a large-scale distributed training setup featuring many parallel actors feeding experience into a centralized replay buffer. The distributional critic provides richer gradient information to the policy, and empirical results demonstrated significant improvements over non-distributional baselines on challenging continuous control benchmarks. D4PG thus serves as a natural bridge between the actor-critic methods of the preceding section and distributional principles, illustrating how distributional critics can improve policy gradient quality by providing richer gradient signals. D4PG also illustrates how distributional RL principles naturally compose with other algorithmic advances such as distributed training, prioritized experience replay, and multi-step returns.

### DSAC: Distributional Soft Actor-Critic

Distributional Soft Actor-Critic (DSAC) integrates distributional RL with the maximum-entropy framework of Soft Actor-Critic (SAC), combining entropy regularization with full return distribution modeling. By maintaining a distributional critic within the entropy-regularized RL objective, DSAC benefits from both the exploration and robustness advantages of maximum-entropy RL and the richer learning signal provided by distributional supervision. This combination is particularly well-suited to continuous control tasks where both sample efficiency and policy robustness are critical concerns. The distributional perspective also interacts naturally with the stochastic policy used in SAC, since the policy-induced randomness contributes to the distributional shape of returns in a manner that can be explicitly modeled and leveraged. In this sense, DSAC represents a direct synthesis of the entropy regularization ideas central to the preceding actor-critic subsection and the distributional objectives introduced here, demonstrating that these two paradigms are not only compatible but mutually reinforcing.

### Distributional RL, Exploration, and Risk Sensitivity

Beyond raw performance improvements, distributional RL offers principled mechanisms for addressing exploration and risk sensitivity—two central challenges in deploying RL agents in real-world settings. From an exploration standpoint, the epistemic uncertainty captured in the spread of the return distribution can serve as an intrinsic motivation signal: states or actions associated with high distributional variance are precisely those where the agent's knowledge is most uncertain, suggesting they are high-value candidates for exploration. This connection has motivated several works that use distributional uncertainty as an exploration bonus, bridging distributional RL with count-based and uncertainty-driven exploration methods. This uncertainty-driven perspective also connects naturally to the ensemble-based uncertainty estimation discussed in the following model-based RL subsection, where disagreement among model predictions similarly serves as a signal of epistemic uncertainty and a guide for exploration.

From a risk-sensitivity standpoint, the availability of the full return distribution enables agents to optimize a wide variety of risk measures beyond the expectation. Coherent risk measures such as CVaR, spectral risk measures, and distortion risk measures can all be computed from the return distribution and used as alternative optimization objectives. Risk-sensitive policy gradient methods have been developed that incorporate these distributional objectives [194], and distributional RL provides a natural off-the-shelf mechanism for estimating the necessary distributional quantities from sampled experience. This synergy between distributional representation and risk-sensitive optimization is a promising direction for deploying RL in safety-critical domains such as healthcare, finance, and autonomous systems.

### Connections to Broader Algorithmic Themes

Distributional RL connects meaningfully to several other algorithmic themes surveyed in this section. Its interaction with actor-critic methods—as demonstrated by D4PG and DSAC—shows how distributional critics can improve policy gradient quality by providing richer gradient signals. Its connection to risk-sensitive RL is discussed further in Section 5.9, which explores CVaR, variance-constrained, and distributional formulations in the context of safe DRL. The Rainbow algorithm, discussed in Section 2.1, incorporates distributional RL (specifically C51) as one of its constituent components, demonstrating that distributional representations compose favorably with other DQN extensions. Looking ahead, the model-based RL methods discussed in the following subsection offer a complementary strategy for improving sample efficiency: rather than extracting richer learning signals from the return distribution as distributional RL does, model-based approaches explicitly simulate hypothetical experience through a learned world model, yet both paradigms share the overarching goal of maximizing the utility of collected data. Overall, distributional RL represents one of the most theoretically principled and empirically impactful advances in deep reinforcement learning, transforming the fundamental object of learning from a scalar to a distribution and unlocking qualitatively richer forms of reasoning about uncertainty, risk, and reward.

### 2.5 Model-Based Deep Reinforcement Learning

## 2.5 Model-Based Deep Reinforcement Learning

Model-based deep reinforcement learning (MBDRL) represents a paradigm in which agents learn an explicit model of the environment's transition dynamics and reward structure, subsequently leveraging this model for planning, policy optimization, or data augmentation. In contrast to the value-based and policy gradient methods discussed in earlier subsections — which learn directly from sampled environment interactions — model-based approaches aim to extract more learning signal from fewer real interactions by simulating hypothetical experience through a learned model. This emphasis on sample efficiency is a critical advantage in domains where data collection is expensive or dangerous, and it complements the distributional and actor-critic perspectives already surveyed by offering an orthogonal mechanism for improving learning quality.

### World Models and Latent Space Representations

A central concept in MBDRL is the **world model** — a compact, often latent-space representation of environment dynamics. World models learn to encode high-dimensional observations (e.g., images, sensor readings) into lower-dimensional latent states, within which transition dynamics and rewards can be predicted more reliably. This latent-space approach allows planning and imagination to occur in a compressed representation rather than raw pixel space, substantially reducing computational cost while preserving the essential structure of the environment. These architectures typically combine recurrent neural networks to capture temporal dependencies with variational autoencoders or other generative models to encode observations.

### Dyna-Style Architectures

Inspired by Sutton's classical Dyna framework, modern MBDRL algorithms interleave real environment interaction with simulated rollouts generated by the learned model. In this paradigm, the agent alternates between (i) collecting real transitions to improve the world model, and (ii) generating synthetic experience from the model to train the policy or value function. This hybrid approach effectively multiplies the amount of training data available without proportionally increasing real-world interactions, drawing a conceptual parallel to the experience replay mechanisms discussed in the following subsection, where stored transitions are similarly reused to improve data efficiency. The key design choices in Dyna-style methods include the rollout length (shorter rollouts reduce compounding model errors), the mixture ratio of real versus simulated data, and mechanisms for detecting and mitigating model inaccuracies that could propagate harmful biases into policy learning.

### Differentiable Models and Backpropagation Through Trajectories

A particularly powerful subset of MBDRL methods exploits the differentiability of learned models to compute policy gradients by backpropagating directly through multi-step predicted trajectories. Rather than treating the model as a black-box simulator, these approaches use the model's Jacobians to propagate gradient information from future predicted states back to current policy parameters. [109] exemplifies this approach, demonstrating how pathwise derivatives of a learned model enable more efficient policy optimization within an actor-critic framework. The authors introduce a terminal value function to prevent instability across long horizons and provide a theoretical analysis of monotonic improvement in terms of gradient errors in the model and value function, showing that this approach consistently achieves superior sample efficiency compared to model-free baselines while scaling effectively to long-horizon tasks.

Similarly, [110] (MAGE) proposes explicitly training the critic to provide useful action-value gradients for policy improvement by backpropagating through the learned dynamics model during temporal difference learning. Rather than merely fitting expected returns, MAGE's critic is specifically trained to produce accurate gradient targets, yielding a critic better tailored for the actor's policy improvement updates. Empirical evaluations on MuJoCo continuous control tasks demonstrate that MAGE achieves competitive performance relative to both model-free and model-based state-of-the-art methods.

### Integration with Actor-Critic Frameworks

Integrating model-based components into actor-critic architectures is a natural and widely explored direction, building on the actor-critic foundations laid out in earlier subsections. In [195], the authors combine Model Predictive Control (MPC) with a learned value function critic, where the MPC optimizer serves as an importance sampler that minimizes the cross-entropy to the state distribution of the optimal sampling policy. This formulation allows the policy to leverage multi-step planning guided by a global value estimate, demonstrating that incorporating the value function into the trajectory optimizer's running cost significantly accelerates convergence even under sparse and binary reward signals.

### Trade-offs Between Model Accuracy and Policy Performance

Despite the sample efficiency advantages, MBDRL faces fundamental challenges related to **model error compounding**. Errors in the learned transition model accumulate over multi-step rollouts, leading to increasingly inaccurate simulated trajectories. Policies optimized on these biased rollouts may perform poorly in the true environment — a phenomenon known as **model exploitation** or the **Dyna trap**. Addressing this tension requires careful management of rollout horizon, uncertainty quantification in model predictions, and trust-region style constraints that prevent the policy from deviating too far based on uncertain model predictions.

Ensemble-based uncertainty estimation, where multiple dynamics models are trained simultaneously and disagreement among predictions signals epistemic uncertainty, has emerged as a practical approach to this problem. By steering exploration toward regions of high model uncertainty (active learning of dynamics) and restricting policy optimization to regions where the ensemble agrees, such methods balance the benefits of model-based planning against the risks of model exploitation. This uncertainty-driven perspective also connects naturally to the distributional RL ideas discussed in Section 2.4, where the spread of the return distribution similarly serves as a signal of epistemic uncertainty and exploration potential.

### Latent-Space Planning and Scalability

For high-dimensional observation spaces such as raw pixel inputs, planning in observation space is computationally intractable. Latent-space models address this by learning compact representations in which planning is feasible. Recurrent state-space models, which separate the deterministic and stochastic components of latent transitions, enable imagination of future trajectories that are both computationally efficient and capture meaningful environmental structure. These models have demonstrated strong performance on visually complex tasks by learning to predict the consequences of actions entirely within the latent space, with the policy similarly parameterized over latent states.

### Summary

Model-based DRL offers compelling advantages in sample efficiency by enabling agents to leverage a learned world model for planning and synthetic data generation. Key architectural paradigms include Dyna-style model augmentation, differentiable world models supporting pathwise gradient computation [109][110], MPC-style value-guided planning [195], and latent-space representations that make imagination tractable in high-dimensional domains. The central challenge across all these approaches remains managing model error compounding, and ongoing research in uncertainty-aware model learning, adaptive rollout horizons, and robust policy optimization continues to advance the capability of model-based methods in complex real-world environments. These themes connect forward to the following subsection, which examines how experience replay and multi-step return methods — mechanisms that similarly seek to maximize the utility of collected data — form a complementary substrate for high-performing deep RL systems.

### 2.6 Experience Replay Mechanisms and Multi-Step Returns

## 2.6 Experience Replay and Multi-Step Return Methods

Building directly on the model-based methods surveyed in the preceding section — which sought to improve sample efficiency through learned world models and synthetic data generation — this section examines a complementary and equally foundational family of mechanisms: experience replay and multi-step return estimation. Where model-based approaches improve data efficiency by imagining new transitions, experience replay improves it by systematically reusing real transitions already collected, and multi-step returns improve credit assignment by propagating reward signals over longer temporal horizons. Together, these mechanisms form a critical substrate for high-performing deep RL algorithms and set the stage for the evolutionary and hybrid optimization methods discussed in the following section.

Experience replay is one of the most foundational mechanisms enabling stable and sample-efficient training in deep reinforcement learning. By storing past transitions in a replay buffer and sampling from them during training, experience replay breaks the temporal correlations that arise from sequential interaction with the environment, effectively converting the non-i.i.d. data stream of online RL into a more stationary supervised-like training distribution. This insight, originally central to the stability of DQN [119], proved to be a critical ingredient for combining deep neural networks with Q-learning, as correlated updates from consecutive transitions tend to destabilize gradient-based optimization.

**Uniform and Prioritized Experience Replay**

The simplest form of experience replay employs a uniform replay buffer, where transitions are sampled with equal probability regardless of their informativeness.
