This multi-objective training signal strengthens the generalizability of the learned representation.

**Invariance Learning Through Auxiliary Objectives**

Representation learning for generalization is further strengthened by auxiliary self-supervised objectives that encourage the encoder to capture environment structure beyond what is strictly necessary for immediate reward maximization. Approaches such as contrastive learning, data-augmentation-based consistency regularization, and mutual information maximization between augmented views of the same state have all been explored in the DRL context. These objectives act as regularizers on the representation space, preventing collapse to narrow, task-specific features and promoting the formation of representations that generalize across visual domains.

Invariance-based auxiliary losses, such as those that penalize the representation for changing in response to task-irrelevant transformations, form the basis of methods like IDAAC (Invariant Decoupled Advantage Actor-Critic) [105]. IDAAC explicitly introduces an auxiliary loss encouraging the policy representation to be invariant to task-irrelevant properties of the environment, achieving state-of-the-art generalization performance on the Procgen benchmark and strong results on visually challenging DeepMind Control tasks.

**Pixel-Based Encoder Transfer**

Transfer of visual encoders pretrained on rich source domains—either from other RL environments or from large-scale supervised vision datasets—provides another avenue for improving generalization. Pretrained convolutional encoders capture low-level and mid-level visual features that are broadly useful across many visual environments. Fine-tuning only the higher-level layers or the policy head while keeping the pretrained encoder frozen or lightly adapted reduces the sample complexity required to generalize to new visual domains. This strategy shares conceptual grounding with the transfer pipelines discussed in the context of sim-to-real methods, but operates directly in the observation feature space rather than at the level of simulator parameters.

**Zero-Shot and Few-Shot Policy Transfer**

The ultimate test of generalization is zero-shot transfer, where a policy trained in one visual domain is applied directly to a novel visual environment without any additional interaction data. Achieving robust zero-shot transfer demands that the learned representation is sufficiently disentangled from domain-specific visual features. Data augmentation plays a crucial role here, as training with diverse augmentations implicitly trains the agent to be robust to the kinds of visual variation it will encounter at test time. Few-shot transfer, where a small number of interactions in the new domain are allowed for adaptation, benefits similarly from well-structured representations that facilitate rapid fine-tuning. These ideas connect naturally to the curriculum learning strategies explored in the following section, where structured training progressions over diverse environments further enhance the transferability of learned representations.

**Connections to Actor-Critic Architectures**

Many of the representation learning and data augmentation strategies described above are naturally integrated within actor-critic frameworks. The actor and critic can each benefit from specialized representations: the actor from an invariant, policy-relevant embedding, and the critic from a richer representation that may leverage augmented data for improved value estimation [104]. The tension between these objectives motivates ongoing research into shared versus disjoint encoder designs, where shared encoders promote parameter efficiency but may introduce conflicting optimization pressures between the actor and critic objectives [105].

Collectively, data augmentation and representation learning constitute a rapidly maturing toolkit for improving the generalization capability of DRL agents. By reducing reliance on training-domain-specific visual features and promoting invariant, structured representations, these techniques bring DRL closer to the goal of agents that robustly transfer learned behaviors to the diverse and unpredictable visual conditions encountered in real-world deployment. The following section on curriculum learning examines how the ordering and selection of training experiences provides yet another dimension of control over the generalization properties of trained policies, complementing the observation-space and representation-level interventions discussed here.

### 3.6 Curriculum Learning and Automated Curriculum Generation

## Curriculum Learning for Generalization in Deep Reinforcement Learning

Building upon the data augmentation and representation learning strategies examined in the previous section—which shape the internal features of agents to be robust to visual and perceptual variation—curriculum learning addresses generalization from a complementary angle: the ordering and selection of training experiences themselves. While representation learning operates within the observation feature space, curriculum learning operates at the level of task distribution, structuring the progression of encountered environments to guide the agent toward more robust and transferable policies. Together, these approaches form an increasingly integrated framework for producing DRL agents capable of reliably generalizing across diverse and challenging deployment conditions.

Curriculum learning in deep reinforcement learning draws inspiration from human pedagogy, where learners progress from simpler to more complex material in a structured, deliberate sequence. Rather than exposing an agent to the full complexity of a target task from the outset, curriculum learning approaches carefully order training experiences—ranging from task instances to entire environments—to accelerate convergence, improve sample efficiency, and enhance generalization to unseen scenarios.

A central motivation for curriculum learning in DRL is that agents trained on randomly sampled or uniformly distributed tasks often struggle with sparse rewards and high task complexity, leading to slow or failed learning. By carefully sequencing the difficulty of encountered environments or subtasks, curriculum methods can provide more consistent and informative learning signals throughout training. This is especially important in domains characterized by long-horizon sparse rewards, where random exploration alone is insufficient for reliable policy improvement.

Among the earliest and most influential curriculum frameworks is self-paced learning, which adaptively modulates task difficulty based on the learner's current competence. In the context of DRL, self-paced context evaluation (SPCE) monitors the agent's performance on parameterized task contexts and selects training contexts at the boundary of the agent's current capability—neither too easy to provide no new information, nor too difficult to yield only failure signals. This principle of "zone of proximal development" sampling has been shown to dramatically accelerate training in goal-conditioned and parameterized task settings.

Automatic curriculum learning (ACL) methods take this idea further by framing the curriculum generation process itself as an optimization problem. Rather than relying on hand-crafted task sequences, ACL algorithms learn to generate or select tasks that maximize the learning progress of the agent. Learning progress—often measured as the rate of improvement in policy performance on specific task instances—serves as an intrinsic signal guiding the curriculum. Absolute learning progress and its variants have been proposed to ensure that the curriculum generator focuses computational resources on tasks where the agent is actively improving, avoiding both stagnation on mastered tasks and wasted effort on tasks beyond current reach.

Unsupervised environment design (UED) represents a particularly powerful paradigm within automated curriculum learning. In UED, a regret-based adversarial teacher generates environment configurations that maximize the agent's regret—the gap between optimal and current policy performance. By continuously adapting the environment distribution to expose the agent's weaknesses, UED methods such as PAIRED (Protagonist Antagonist Induced Regret Environment Design) produce agents with strong robustness and generalization properties. These approaches are particularly well-suited to procedurally generated environments where the space of possible configurations is vast and continuous.

Meta-automatic curriculum learning (Meta-ACL) elevates the curriculum generation process to a meta-learning level, where the curriculum generator itself is trained across multiple agent training runs to improve its ability to produce effective curricula. This meta-level optimization allows the curriculum to be tailored not just to a single agent's current state, but to the broader dynamics of how agents learn, enabling curriculum strategies that generalize across different initial conditions, architectures, or task families.

Prior-learning-based curriculum refinement incorporates knowledge acquired from earlier training phases or related tasks to inform the construction of the curriculum. Rather than generating curricula from scratch, these approaches leverage representations, value estimates, or policy priors learned in previous environments to bias task selection toward configurations that are most informative given the agent's existing competencies. This can be viewed as a form of warm-starting the curriculum, allowing agents to more efficiently exploit prior knowledge when adapting to new task distributions.

Curriculum transfer across environments of varying complexity addresses the challenge of deploying agents trained under one curriculum regime to environments with substantially different structures or dynamics. Effective curriculum transfer requires that the skills and representations acquired during training under a structured curriculum are sufficiently general and composable to transfer to target environments. Approaches in this space include progressive task expansion, where the complexity of training environments is incrementally increased to bridge the gap between source and target distributions, as well as multi-fidelity curriculum designs that alternate between cheap low-fidelity simulations and expensive high-fidelity environments. This progressive expansion connects directly to the domain randomization and sim-to-real transfer pipelines discussed earlier in this chapter, with the curriculum providing explicit control over the schedule by which environmental diversity is introduced.

The intersection of curriculum learning with generalization in DRL is particularly significant. Agents trained with well-designed curricula tend to develop more robust and transferable policies than those trained on fixed task distributions [206]. The structured exposure to a diverse range of task difficulties and environment configurations promotes the development of representations that generalize beyond the specific instances encountered during training, which is critical for real-world deployment where the test distribution may differ substantially from the training distribution. This generalization benefit also provides a natural foundation for the hierarchical architectures discussed in the following section, where the ability to reuse and compose learned behaviors across tasks is central to scaling DRL to long-horizon problems.

Benchmark evaluation of curriculum learning methods in DRL requires careful design of train-test splits and standardized protocols to avoid overfitting curricula to specific test conditions. The development of open-ended task spaces and configurable environment suites has provided valuable infrastructure for assessing the generalization benefits of different curriculum strategies. Across domains including robotic manipulation, navigation, and multi-agent coordination, curriculum learning has demonstrated consistent benefits in terms of both the speed of policy acquisition and the final level of generalization achieved. Furthermore, as the following section on hierarchical reinforcement learning demonstrates, curriculum learning integrates naturally with hierarchical decomposition: lower-level skills can be trained on simpler tasks before being incorporated into increasingly complex hierarchical compositions, establishing curriculum learning as a foundational technique in the broader effort to scale DRL to complex, real-world problems.

### 3.7 Hierarchical Reinforcement Learning for Scalability and Abstraction

Hierarchical reinforcement learning (HRL) addresses one of the most fundamental scalability challenges in deep reinforcement learning: the difficulty of solving long-horizon tasks that require coordinated sequences of decisions spread across extended time scales. Just as curriculum learning structures the progression of training experiences to scaffold increasingly complex behaviors, HRL structures the agent's decision-making process itself into layered abstractions, enabling agents to reason at multiple levels of temporal and behavioral granularity. By decomposing complex tasks into structured hierarchies of subtasks, HRL dramatically reduces the effective planning horizon at each level and facilitates the reuse of learned behaviors across diverse environments—properties that are closely related to the generalization and transfer goals motivating the surrounding approaches in this survey.

## Options Framework and Temporal Abstraction

The options framework provides a foundational mathematical formalism for temporal abstraction in reinforcement learning. An option is defined as a triple consisting of an initiation set, an intra-option policy, and a termination condition, together forming a semi-Markov decision process that allows the agent to commit to extended courses of action. This framework generalizes primitive actions to macro-actions that can span multiple time steps, effectively compressing the temporal horizon of the high-level decision problem. The semi-MDP formulation introduced alongside options provides theoretical grounding for value function estimation over temporally extended actions, enabling the derivation of convergence guarantees analogous to those in standard MDPs.

Temporal abstraction through options enables a critical separation of concerns: high-level policies select among available options based on abstract state representations, while low-level intra-option policies handle fine-grained motor control or primitive action selection. This decomposition naturally supports modular learning, where individual options can be trained, tested, and reused independently. The termination condition of each option introduces a form of soft boundary between subtasks, allowing the hierarchy to adapt dynamically to environmental contingencies rather than following rigid predefined task decompositions.

## Goal-Conditioned Hierarchical Architectures

Goal-conditioned hierarchical reinforcement learning extends the options framework by parameterizing low-level policies with explicit goal specifications set by higher levels of the hierarchy. In this paradigm, a high-level controller generates subgoal representations in a learned embedding space, which serve as conditioning inputs to a low-level controller responsible for reaching those subgoals within a fixed number of steps. The high-level policy learns to decompose the original task objective into a sequence of achievable intermediate goals, effectively performing a form of online task planning through goal generation.

A central challenge in goal-conditioned hierarchies is ensuring that the subgoals generated by the high-level controller are achievable by the low-level controller. This requires careful alignment between the goal representation space and the states that the low-level policy can reliably reach. Methods such as hindsight experience replay have been adapted within hierarchical frameworks to address the sparse reward problem at the low level, relabeling failed goal-reaching attempts with the states actually achieved to provide dense learning signal regardless of whether the intended subgoal was reached.

The HIRO (HIerarchical Reinforcement learning with Off-policy correction) architecture demonstrated that goal-conditioned hierarchies can be trained efficiently off-policy by applying a correction procedure to account for the non-stationarity introduced by the changing high-level policy. By relabeling high-level transitions with the subgoal that would have most likely produced the observed low-level behavior, off-policy correction enables the high-level controller to learn from experience collected under earlier versions of both policies. This technique substantially improves sample efficiency in continuous control tasks with sparse rewards.

## Skill Discovery and Unsupervised Option Learning

A fundamental limitation of early HRL approaches was their reliance on manually specified options or predefined task decompositions, which restricts scalability and generalization. Skill discovery methods address this limitation by autonomously identifying useful behavioral primitives from experience, without requiring human knowledge about subtask structure. These approaches formulate skill discovery as an intrinsic objective that encourages learning a diverse set of distinguishable skills, each corresponding to a coherent region of behavior or state space. This connection to intrinsic motivation places skill discovery at an important intersection between HRL and the exploration strategies discussed elsewhere in this survey.

Information-theoretic approaches to skill discovery maximize the mutual information between skill identifiers and the states or trajectories they produce, encouraging each skill to reliably reach distinct parts of the state space. DIAYN (Diversity Is All You Need) exemplifies this approach by training a discriminator to predict which skill is being executed from the resulting states, using the discriminator's log-likelihood as an intrinsic reward signal. The resulting skills provide a structured behavioral vocabulary that can subsequently be leveraged for downstream task learning through hierarchical composition.

Spectral methods for skill discovery exploit the intrinsic geometry of the state transition structure to identify bottleneck states or subgoal regions that naturally partition the environment. Graph-theoretic analyses of the state transition graph reveal states with high betweenness centrality, which correspond to transition bottlenecks through which many optimal trajectories must pass. Setting such bottleneck states as subgoals decomposes the task into natural subtasks aligned with the environment's connectivity structure, yielding hierarchical decompositions that generalize across reward functions defined over the same state space.

More recent approaches to skill discovery leverage successor representations to identify proto-goals that span diverse regions of the reachable state space. The successor representation encodes the expected future state occupancy under a given policy, enabling efficient computation of distances between states in terms of visitation patterns rather than raw feature similarity. Eigendecomposition of the successor representation matrix yields a set of basis functions that capture the global topological structure of the state space, providing principled subgoal representations that support transfer across tasks sharing the same dynamics.

## Feudal Reinforcement Learning and Manager-Worker Architectures

Feudal reinforcement learning introduces a management hierarchy in which higher-level managers set goals or directives for lower-level workers operating at finer temporal granularities. The feudal paradigm explicitly separates the responsibilities of strategic planning at the manager level from tactical execution at the worker level, with managers operating at coarser time scales and setting abstract objectives while workers pursue those objectives through detailed action sequences.

The FeUdal Networks (FuN) architecture implements the feudal paradigm using deep neural networks, with a manager module that generates directional goals in a learned latent space and a worker module that receives both environment observations and the manager's goal embedding as inputs. The manager is trained using a transition policy gradient that accounts for the delayed effect of its goal-setting decisions, while the worker receives an intrinsic reward based on cosine similarity between its trajectory direction and the manager's goal. This architecture successfully addresses the non-stationarity problem by providing the worker with a relatively stable goal signal that changes only at the manager's update frequency.

Extensions of the feudal framework have explored deeper hierarchies with three or more levels, enabling decomposition of tasks that require coordination across multiple time scales simultaneously. Multi-level hierarchies face compounding challenges related to credit assignment and non-stationarity, as the effective reward signal for intermediate levels depends on the evolving behavior of all levels above and below. Addressing these challenges requires careful design of inter-level communication, temporal coordination mechanisms, and potentially end-to-end training procedures that propagate gradients across hierarchy levels.

## Transfer and Generalization Through Hierarchical Decomposition

One of the most compelling motivations for hierarchical reinforcement learning is its potential to facilitate transfer and generalization across diverse tasks and environments—goals that align closely with the evaluation challenges discussed in the benchmark and generalization literature. When low-level skills capture task-agnostic behavioral primitives—such as locomotion gaits, manipulation primitives, or navigation behaviors—they can be reused across high-level tasks that differ in their objectives while sharing underlying dynamics. This modular reuse dramatically reduces the sample complexity of learning new tasks by avoiding the need to rediscover already-known low-level competencies.

Transfer in hierarchical frameworks can operate at multiple granularities. Low-level option transfer involves applying previously learned options in new environments with similar local dynamics, using the high-level policy to compose these options in task-appropriate sequences. High-level policy transfer involves applying a previously learned compositional strategy to new environments equipped with a different but compatible option set, enabling generalization when the abstract task structure is preserved across domains. Cross-level transfer combines both, reusing partial hierarchies while adapting specific levels to account for domain differences.

Curriculum learning integrates naturally with hierarchical reinforcement learning, forming a particularly productive synergy. As discussed in the preceding section, curriculum methods structure the progression of task difficulty to provide consistently informative learning signals. Within HRL, the hierarchical structure provides a natural scaffolding for such difficulty progression: lower-level skills can be trained on simpler tasks before being incorporated into increasingly complex hierarchical compositions, ensuring that the skill repertoire grows in a controlled manner aligned with the agent's current learning capacity. This curriculum-informed hierarchical training reduces the risk of catastrophic interference between skills and improves the stability of the overall learning process.

## Challenges and Limitations

Despite significant progress, hierarchical reinforcement learning faces several unresolved challenges. The subgoal representation problem concerns how to define the space in which goals are expressed: overly abstract goals may be unachievable by the low-level controller, while overly concrete goals may fail to provide useful guidance. The temporal credit assignment problem is exacerbated in hierarchical settings, as rewards observed after a sequence of high-level decisions must be attributed across multiple levels and time scales. The non-stationarity problem arises because the low-level environment experienced by the high-level controller changes as the low-level policy evolves during training.

Scalability to very deep hierarchies remains difficult, as the training dynamics of multi-level systems involve complex inter-level dependencies that can lead to instability or collapse to degenerate solutions. End-to-end differentiable hierarchical architectures offer a potential solution by enabling joint optimization across levels, but require careful architectural design to avoid gradient interference between levels with different temporal scales.

These challenges also have direct implications for the benchmarking and evaluation methodologies discussed in the following section. Assessing HRL systems requires benchmark environments that expose long-horizon task structure, meaningful subgoal spaces, and sufficient distributional variety to test the generalization of learned skill hierarchies across novel configurations. Despite these challenges, hierarchical reinforcement learning continues to represent a promising direction for scaling deep reinforcement learning to the complexity and diversity of real-world tasks, by embedding structural inductive biases that reflect the hierarchical organization of natural decision-making processes.

### 3.8 Benchmarks and Evaluation Frameworks for Generalization

## Benchmarking and Evaluation for Generalization

Rigorous evaluation of generalization, transfer, and adaptation in deep reinforcement learning demands benchmark environments and standardized protocols that go well beyond measuring asymptotic performance on a single fixed task. The challenges discussed throughout this survey—from hierarchical decomposition and curriculum learning to meta-learning and sim-to-real transfer—ultimately require empirical validation through carefully designed evaluation frameworks. Without such frameworks, it is impossible to determine whether a DRL agent has truly learned a transferable policy or has merely memorized environment-specific cues. This concern has motivated the community to develop a diverse ecosystem of benchmark suites and evaluation methodologies that stress-test agents across distribution shifts, novel task configurations, and unseen visual domains.

A central challenge in benchmarking generalization is that conventional environments such as Atari or MuJoCo expose agents to the same distribution of states during both training and evaluation, providing little evidence of out-of-distribution robustness. To address this, natural environment benchmarks have been proposed that introduce visual distractors, procedurally generated levels, and stochastic background textures, ensuring that agents cannot rely on pixel-level memorization. Such environments force agents to develop representations that capture task-relevant structure while discarding irrelevant variation, providing a more honest measure of visual generalization [207]. This requirement connects directly to the representation learning objectives underlying many of the algorithms surveyed in preceding sections.

Configurable meta-learning environment suites complement visual benchmarks by allowing systematic variation of task parameters such as goal locations, reward functions, and transition dynamics across episodes. These suites are specifically designed to evaluate few-shot adaptation, requiring agents to improve rapidly within a small number of interactions on a held-out task drawn from a known distribution. The use of distinct task distributions for meta-training and meta-testing provides a principled train-test separation that prevents overfitting to a particular set of task instances, which is a prerequisite for meaningful evaluation of meta-reinforcement learning algorithms [207]. Notably, such evaluation protocols also provide a natural testbed for assessing whether hierarchical skill representations, discussed in the preceding section, genuinely transfer across task variations or merely overfit to specific training configurations.

Open-ended task spaces represent a further step toward measuring generalization under broader distributional shift. Rather than fixing a finite set of training tasks, open-ended frameworks generate tasks through procedural content generation or environment designers, creating a theoretically unbounded space of challenges. Evaluation in such settings typically involves measuring performance on procedurally generated test instances whose parameters were never encountered during training, revealing the degree to which learned policies generalize across task complexity gradients and structural variations. This aligns closely with the goals of curriculum learning methods, which seek to produce agents capable of handling progressively more complex and varied task instances.

Standardized evaluation protocols are equally important as the choice of benchmark environment. Common pitfalls include evaluating agents with the same random seed that governed training, using insufficient numbers of evaluation episodes, and failing to report variance across independent training runs. Reproducibility concerns are especially acute in DRL because performance is known to be highly sensitive to implementation details, hyperparameter choices, and initialization [207]. Best practices therefore recommend aggregating results across multiple seeds, reporting interquartile means alongside confidence intervals, and clearly separating the computational budget allocated to hyperparameter search from that used in final evaluation.

Transfer benchmarks introduce an additional dimension by measuring how well policies trained in a source environment perform when deployed in a target environment with modified dynamics, visual appearance, or task structure. Evaluating zero-shot transfer requires that no additional interaction with the target environment is permitted after training, while few-shot transfer protocols allow a controlled number of fine-tuning steps. These protocols are essential for assessing sim-to-real transfer pipelines, where agents trained in simulation must generalize to physical systems with unmodeled dynamics and sensor noise [207]. As HRL systems are increasingly applied to long-horizon real-world tasks, these evaluation frameworks become particularly important for validating that the hierarchical decompositions learned in simulation remain coherent and effective under the distributional shifts inherent in physical deployment.

Collectively, the development of principled benchmark environments and rigorous evaluation frameworks has become as important as algorithmic innovation in advancing the generalization capabilities of DRL agents. The challenges identified here—constructing meaningful train-test separations, measuring performance across distribution shifts, and ensuring reproducibility—are not merely methodological concerns but reflect deeper scientific questions about what it means for a DRL system to have genuinely learned rather than memorized. As the field moves toward more ambitious deployment scenarios, ensuring that reported improvements reflect genuine generalization rather than overfitting to narrow evaluation conditions remains a foundational requirement for scientific progress.

## 4 Multi-Agent Deep Reinforcement Learning

### 4.1 Foundations and Problem Formulations in Multi-Agent Deep Reinforcement Learning

## 4.1 Foundations and Problem Formulations in Multi-Agent Deep Reinforcement Learning

Multi-agent deep reinforcement learning (MARL) extends the single-agent reinforcement learning paradigm to settings where multiple agents simultaneously interact within a shared environment. The formal frameworks underpinning MARL generalize the Markov Decision Process (MDP) in several important directions, introducing new theoretical structures and computational challenges that fundamentally distinguish multi-agent from single-agent learning. Understanding these foundations is essential for appreciating the algorithmic developments that follow, including the Centralized Training with Decentralized Execution (CTDE) paradigm that has come to dominate cooperative MARL.

### Markov Games and Multi-Agent MDPs

The foundational formal model for multi-agent settings is the **Markov Game** (also known as a Stochastic Game), which generalizes the MDP to include multiple decision-makers. A Markov Game is defined by a tuple $\langle \mathcal{S}, \{\mathcal{A}^i\}_{i=1}^N, \mathcal{T}, \{r^i\}_{i=1}^N, \gamma \rangle$, where $\mathcal{S}$ is the shared state space, $\mathcal{A}^i$ is the action space of agent $i$, $\mathcal{T}: \mathcal{S} \times \mathcal{A}^1 \times \cdots \times \mathcal{A}^N \rightarrow \Delta(\mathcal{S})$ is the joint transition function, $r^i: \mathcal{S} \times \mathcal{A}^1 \times \cdots \times \mathcal{A}^N \rightarrow \mathbb{R}$ is the reward function for agent $i$, and $\gamma$ is the discount factor. At each timestep, all agents simultaneously observe the global state, select actions, and receive individual rewards. This structure directly subsumes the MDP as a special case when $N=1$ and generalizes naturally to settings with heterogeneous agent objectives.

In contrast to single-agent MDPs where the environment dynamics are stationary from the agent's perspective [203], Markov Games introduce the critical complication that the effective environment faced by each agent is non-stationary: as co-learners update their policies during training, the transition and reward distributions experienced by any individual agent change continuously. This **non-stationarity** represents one of the central theoretical and practical challenges in MARL, as it invalidates the convergence guarantees that underpin most single-agent RL algorithms, which rely on the assumption of a fixed underlying Markov chain. As will be discussed in Section 4.2, addressing this non-stationarity is precisely one of the primary motivations for the CTDE paradigm.

### Decentralized Partially Observable Markov Decision Processes

Many real-world multi-agent systems involve not only multiple decision-makers but also **partial observability**, where each agent can only access a local observation rather than the true global state. The **Decentralized Partially Observable Markov Decision Process (Dec-POMDP)** formalizes this setting. A Dec-POMDP extends the Markov Game framework with an observation space $\Omega^i$ for each agent $i$ and an observation function $\mathcal{O}: \mathcal{S} \times \mathcal{A}^1 \times \cdots \times \mathcal{A}^N \rightarrow \Delta(\Omega^1 \times \cdots \times \Omega^N)$ that governs the stochastic relationship between the underlying global state and each agent's local percept. Partial observability in single-agent settings already introduces substantial computational and statistical challenges [11; 12; 15], and the decentralized multi-agent variant compounds these difficulties significantly.

In a Dec-POMDP, agents must reason about the beliefs of other agents and the joint history of observations and actions, making exact solution PSPACE-hard or worse for finite horizons. Practical approaches must therefore rely on approximation methods, including deep neural network-based policies that process observation histories [17], recurrent architectures that maintain compressed belief states [12], and centralized training procedures that leverage global information during learning while maintaining decentralized execution at deployment — a principle that is formalized and elaborated in the CTDE framework discussed in Section 4.2.

The macro-action extension of Dec-POMDPs, MacDec-POMDPs [22], further generalizes the framework by allowing agents to asynchronously execute temporally extended actions, enabling more scalable planning over long horizons in cooperative multi-agent tasks. This temporal abstraction reduces the effective planning horizon and alleviates some of the combinatorial complexity inherent in the joint action space.

### The Curse of Dimensionality in Multi-Agent Settings

A foundational challenge in MARL is the **curse of dimensionality** induced by the joint action space. With $N$ agents each having $|\mathcal{A}|$ possible actions, the joint action space grows as $|\mathcal{A}|^N$, rendering exact value function computation and policy search computationally intractable for even moderate numbers of agents. Similarly, the joint observation and state spaces can grow exponentially with the number of agents, making both model learning and policy optimization extremely sample-inefficient. This exponential scaling motivates much of the algorithmic development in MARL, including value decomposition methods, parameter sharing, and hierarchical approaches, all of which aim to reduce the effective dimensionality by exploiting structure in the joint problem [9].

The challenge of large-scale management is not unique to multi-agent systems; single-agent systems with large state and action spaces face analogous difficulties [9]. However, in MARL the joint nature of the problem means that even relatively small per-agent spaces quickly become unmanageable, and the dependencies between agents further complicate factored representations. Value decomposition methods such as QMIX, discussed in Section 4.2, directly address this challenge by factorizing the joint value function into tractable per-agent components.

### Cooperative, Competitive, and Mixed Settings

A defining organizational dimension of multi-agent learning is the **relationship between agent reward functions**, which gives rise to three fundamental paradigms:

**Cooperative settings** involve agents that share a single team reward or have perfectly aligned individual rewards. The canonical formalization is the Dec-POMDP with a common reward function $r^1 = \cdots = r^N = r$. In this setting, the primary challenge is **coordination**: agents must learn to act jointly so as to maximize the shared return, despite potentially having access only to local observations and being unable to communicate explicitly. Credit assignment — determining how much each individual agent contributed to the team outcome — is a central theoretical difficulty in cooperative MARL, and one that the CTDE paradigm addresses through centralized critics and mixing networks.

**Competitive settings** correspond to zero-sum or adversarial Markov Games, where the gain of one agent directly implies a loss for another, i.e., $\sum_i r^i = 0$. The classical solution concept for such settings is the Nash equilibrium, which generalizes the minimax theorem to stochastic games. Competitive MARL draws heavily on game-theoretic analysis and has been particularly prominent in two-player zero-sum games, where self-play and population-based training have yielded superhuman performance across multiple domains.

**Mixed cooperative-competitive settings** combine elements of both paradigms. Agents may need to cooperate within teams while competing between teams, or may face settings where individual and collective incentives are partially misaligned — a structure that models social dilemmas, negotiation, and many real-world economic interactions. These settings are particularly challenging as standard cooperative or competitive assumptions no longer apply, and algorithms must account for the complex interplay between coordination incentives and competitive pressures.

The distinction between these settings is not merely conceptual; it fundamentally shapes which algorithms are applicable, what solution concepts are meaningful, and what emergent behaviors can be expected. Understanding these formal distinctions is therefore essential for selecting appropriate algorithmic approaches and interpreting the behaviors that arise from multi-agent training. The interactive POMDP (I-POMDP) framework [208] takes a complementary perspective by explicitly modeling other agents as part of the state space, enabling principled belief updating over both environment states and the models of co-present agents — a formulation particularly relevant when agents must reason about the intentions and policies of others.

### Non-Stationarity and Its Implications

The non-stationarity introduced by co-learning agents has deep implications for the theoretical foundations of MARL. In single-agent RL, convergence proofs typically rely on the Robbins-Monro conditions applied to a fixed MDP. When multiple agents are simultaneously learning, the Markov property still holds with respect to the global state, but from the perspective of any individual agent learning a best response, the environment is effectively non-Markovian due to the changing policies of others. This connects MARL to the broader literature on reinforcement learning in non-Markovian environments and history-dependent settings [5], and motivates the development of algorithms that are robust to this non-stationarity. As elaborated in Section 4.2, the CTDE paradigm responds directly to this challenge by treating the joint system as a single agent during centralized training, thereby restoring stationarity from the learning algorithm's perspective while preserving the decentralized structure required for scalable execution.

In summary, the formal foundations of multi-agent deep reinforcement learning rest on Markov Games and Dec-POMDPs as primary modeling frameworks, with partial observability [11; 16], non-stationarity, and exponential joint-space complexity as the central theoretical challenges. The cooperative, competitive, and mixed paradigms define the landscape of agent interactions, and understanding these distinctions is prerequisite to the algorithmic developments surveyed in subsequent subsections — most notably the CTDE paradigm, which directly addresses the non-stationarity and scalability challenges identified here through the principled separation of centralized training and decentralized execution.

### 4.2 Centralized Training with Decentralized Execution

## 4.2 Centralized Training with Decentralized Execution

Centralized Training with Decentralized Execution (CTDE) has emerged as the dominant paradigm in cooperative multi-agent deep reinforcement learning, offering an elegant solution to the fundamental tension between the need for global coordination during learning and the practical requirement for agents to act independently during deployment. As established in Section 4.1, the non-stationarity introduced by co-learning agents and the exponential growth of the joint action space represent the two most pressing challenges in cooperative MARL. CTDE directly addresses both: under this paradigm, agents are permitted to access global state information, the observations of other agents, and joint action histories during the training phase, enabling them to learn coordinated and coherent joint policies. At execution time, however, each agent relies solely on its own local observations and its individually learned policy, making the framework scalable and applicable to real-world scenarios where communication bandwidth is limited or inter-agent communication is infeasible.

The conceptual motivation for CTDE stems directly from the theoretical difficulties identified in the Dec-POMDP and Markov Game frameworks. The non-stationarity problem inherent in naive independent learning — where each agent perceives a non-stationary environment due to the simultaneously changing policies of its co-learners — can be substantially mitigated if agents are allowed to condition their learning on the global state during training. By leveraging centralized information only during the training phase, CTDE sidesteps the instability caused by non-stationarity while preserving the scalability benefits of decentralized execution. Importantly, this approach effectively treats the joint system as a single learning agent during training, thereby restoring stationarity from the learning algorithm's perspective while maintaining the decentralized structure required for scalable deployment.

**Multi-Agent Deep Deterministic Policy Gradient (MADDPG)** represents one of the earliest and most influential instantiations of the CTDE paradigm. MADDPG extends the actor-critic framework to multi-agent settings by equipping each agent with a centralized critic that observes the full joint observations and actions of all agents, while each agent's actor operates solely on its local observations. This design allows the centralized critic to provide stable and informative learning signals during training, which are not corrupted by the non-stationarity introduced by simultaneously updating agents. The actor networks, trained with the guidance of these centralized critics, learn policies that implicitly account for the behaviors of other agents, resulting in emergent coordination without requiring explicit communication at execution time. MADDPG has demonstrated effectiveness across competitive, cooperative, and mixed cooperative-competitive settings, spanning all three interaction paradigms identified in Section 4.1.

**QMIX** builds upon the value decomposition perspective within the CTDE framework, directly confronting the curse of dimensionality in the joint action space. Rather than learning independent Q-functions for each agent, QMIX learns a joint action-value function that is factorized into individual agent utilities through a mixing network with monotonicity constraints. The mixing network, which takes the global state as input, combines individual agent Q-values in a way that ensures the greedy action selection from the factorized representation is consistent with the greedy action selection from the joint Q-function — a property known as **Individual-Global-Max (IGM) consistency**. During training, the mixing network is updated using global state information and joint rewards, effectively propagating coordination signals into individual agent value functions while simultaneously addressing the credit assignment problem: each agent's contribution to the team reward is implicitly encoded in its individual utility function. At execution time, each agent selects actions by greedily maximizing its own utility function using only local observations, achieving fully decentralized execution consistent with the Dec-POMDP structure formalized in Section 4.1.

The monotonicity constraint imposed by QMIX's mixing network represents a structural prior that ensures representational consistency while limiting the class of expressible joint value functions. This trade-off between expressiveness and tractability — a recurring theme in MARL algorithm design — is taken up in detail in Section 4.3, which examines value decomposition methods and credit assignment strategies more broadly. Subsequent work such as QPLEX has sought to relax QMIX's constraint by employing more expressive factorization architectures based on duplex dueling structures, thereby expanding the class of cooperative tasks that value decomposition methods can address.

A key advantage of the CTDE framework is that the centralized training process can exploit rich global information — including the reward structure, joint transition dynamics, and the full joint observation-action history — to shape individual agent policies or value functions in ways that naturally induce coordination. The centralized critic or mixing network effectively serves as a coordination mechanism during training, encoding information about how an individual agent's actions interact with and influence the behaviors and rewards of its teammates. This allows agents to implicitly learn complementary and non-redundant roles without requiring explicit role assignment or communication protocols, with the latter representing a complementary approach to coordination that is explored in Section 4.4.

Various extensions and refinements of the CTDE paradigm have been proposed to address its limitations. These include methods that improve the expressiveness of the value factorization, incorporate attention mechanisms to handle varying numbers of agents, adopt recurrent architectures to handle the partial observability inherent in Dec-POMDPs within the centralized critic, and integrate model-based components to improve sample efficiency. Additionally, the CTDE framework has been extended to continuous action spaces, mixed cooperative-competitive environments, and settings with heterogeneous agent capabilities.

The success of CTDE frameworks rests on several key principles that are broadly applicable beyond specific algorithmic instantiations. First, the separation between training-time information access and execution-time information access allows learning algorithms to leverage rich global signals while deploying lightweight decentralized policies — a direct response to the partial observability constraints formalized by the Dec-POMDP. Second, the use of centralized value functions or critics provides more stable training targets that are not affected by the non-stationarity of co-learners' policies identified in Section 4.1. Third, structural constraints such as IGM consistency in value decomposition methods ensure that the learned decentralized policies are globally consistent with the centralized objective being optimized, directly addressing the credit assignment challenge that is elaborated upon in Section 4.3. Together, these principles have made CTDE one of the most productive research directions in multi-agent deep reinforcement learning, forming the foundation upon which the value decomposition methods, credit assignment strategies, and communication protocols surveyed in subsequent subsections are built.

### 4.3 Value Decomposition and Credit Assignment in Cooperative MARL

## 4.3 Value Decomposition and Credit Assignment

Building directly upon the CTDE framework introduced in the previous section, a fundamental challenge in cooperative multi-agent reinforcement learning is the **credit assignment problem**: given a shared team reward, how should individual agents determine their respective contributions to the joint outcome? Value decomposition methods address this challenge by factorizing the joint action-value function into per-agent utility functions, enabling scalable and principled cooperative learning that naturally aligns with the decentralized execution requirements of the CTDE paradigm.

**Value Decomposition Network (VDN)** represents one of the earliest and most straightforward approaches, decomposing the joint Q-function as a simple sum of individual agent Q-values. While computationally efficient and easy to implement, VDN's additive structure imposes a strong representational constraint that limits the complexity of joint value functions it can express.

**QMIX** relaxes this restriction by replacing the summation with a monotonic mixing network, ensuring that the joint Q-value is a monotonically increasing function of each agent's individual utility. As introduced in the preceding discussion of CTDE, this monotonicity constraint is closely tied to the **Individual-Global-Max (IGM) consistency principle**, which requires that the joint greedy action corresponds to each agent independently selecting its individually greedy action. By enforcing IGM through architectural constraints, QMIX enables centralized training with decentralized execution while still permitting richer representational capacity than VDN.

Despite these advances, QMIX's monotonicity constraint still restricts the class of representable joint value functions. **QPLEX** overcomes this limitation by adopting a duplex dueling architecture derived from the IGM principle, allowing it to represent any joint Q-function that satisfies IGM without imposing structural monotonicity on the mixing network. This broader representational class enables QPLEX to capture more complex cooperative dependencies between agents, extending the expressive power that QMIX introduced beyond what monotonic factorization alone permits.

Beyond these foundational methods, numerous extensions have been proposed to further improve credit assignment. Weighted QMIX relaxes the uniform treatment of joint actions, placing greater emphasis on regions of the action space likely to be optimal. QATTEN introduces attention mechanisms to model inter-agent dependencies during value mixing. RODE decomposes roles among agents to improve coordination and scalability. CW-QMIX and OW-QMIX explore non-uniform weighting schemes to better approximate the optimal joint value function.

The **multi-agent credit problem** is also closely related to challenges in reward decomposition, counterfactual reasoning, and difference rewards. COMA (Counterfactual Multi-Agent Policy Gradients) addresses credit assignment in the policy gradient setting by computing an agent-specific advantage function using a centralized critic and a counterfactual baseline, isolating each agent's marginal contribution to the team reward. This counterfactual reasoning approach complements the value decomposition perspective by tackling credit assignment through the lens of actor-critic methods rather than value factorization, demonstrating that the CTDE framework admits diverse algorithmic instantiations.

Scalability remains a persistent concern in value decomposition. As the number of agents grows, maintaining exact factorizations becomes computationally intractable. Approaches based on sparse interaction graphs, attention-based mixing, and hierarchical decomposition help mitigate this challenge by exploiting structural regularities in agent interactions — a concern that will resurface in the discussion of communication protocols in the following section, where information sharing among agents offers a complementary strategy for managing coordination complexity.

The rich landscape of value decomposition methods reflects a broader tension between representational expressiveness and scalability. Methods that enforce strict structural constraints on the mixing function gain computational and convergence benefits at the cost of reduced expressiveness, while more flexible architectures can represent a broader class of cooperative behaviors but may require more data and careful training. Understanding the theoretical boundaries of each approach — specifically the classes of games and reward structures for which IGM-consistent factorizations are sufficient — remains an active area of research.

Overall, value decomposition and credit assignment methods form a cornerstone of cooperative MARL, bridging the gap between centralized value estimation and decentralized execution established by the CTDE paradigm, and enabling agents to efficiently learn coordinated behaviors in complex multi-agent environments. The coordination learned through these methods, however, is ultimately limited by what agents can infer from local observations alone — a limitation that motivates the explicit communication protocols discussed in the following section.

### 4.4 Communication Protocols and Information Sharing Among Agents

## 4.4 Communication Protocols and Information Sharing Among Agents

Effective coordination among agents in multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) fundamentally depends on the capacity of agents to share relevant information with one another. As established in the preceding discussion of value decomposition and credit assignment, agents coordinating under the CTDE paradigm must make decisions based solely on local observations during execution — yet the quality of those decisions is tightly coupled to the information each agent can access. Communication channels can substantially bridge this information gap, enabling more coherent collective behavior and complementing the credit assignment mechanisms that operate at the level of value functions. The design of communication protocols in MARL spans a wide spectrum, from fully differentiable end-to-end learned communication architectures to structured, bandwidth-constrained, and memory-driven messaging schemes, each offering a different trade-off between expressiveness, scalability, and practical deployability.

### End-to-End Differentiable Communication

A foundational contribution to learned communication in MARL is the development of end-to-end differentiable communication methods, which allow gradients to flow through the communication channel and directly shape the content of messages alongside policy learning. Differentiable Inter-Agent Learning (DIAL) pioneered this direction by enabling agents to pass real-valued messages during training, while discretizing them for execution, effectively learning what to communicate through standard backpropagation. Similarly, CommNet introduced a continuous communication framework in which agents broadcast continuous vectors that are averaged and fed back to all agents, enabling a form of mean-field information pooling. These approaches demonstrated that even simple communication mechanisms, when trained end-to-end, can dramatically improve coordination on tasks requiring tight agent synchronization — directly addressing the partial observability limitations that constrain value decomposition methods operating without communication.

### Targeted and Selective Communication

A limitation of broadcast-based architectures such as CommNet is their inherent indiscriminacy — all agents receive the same aggregated message regardless of relevance. Targeted communication architectures address this limitation by learning to direct messages to specific recipients based on the current context. TarMAC introduced an attention-based mechanism in which agents encode messages with a key-query structure, allowing receivers to selectively attend to and weight incoming messages according to their relevance. This targeted communication paradigm reduces noise in shared information and scales more gracefully with the number of agents, as agents learn to ignore irrelevant senders. The attention mechanisms underlying TarMAC share conceptual ground with the attention-based value mixing approaches such as QATTEN discussed in the preceding section, reflecting a broader trend toward attention-based architectures for managing inter-agent dependencies in MARL.

### Bandwidth-Constrained and Scheduled Communication

In many realistic multi-agent deployments, communication bandwidth is a scarce resource. Allowing all agents to communicate at every timestep is infeasible in settings such as autonomous vehicle fleets, robotic swarms, or wireless sensor networks. Bandwidth-constrained communication scheduling methods tackle this problem by learning policies that determine *when* and *with whom* to communicate, rather than simply *what* to communicate. These approaches often formulate communication scheduling as an auxiliary decision-making problem, where agents are penalized for excessive message passing and must balance communication cost against coordination benefit. By learning sparse communication graphs, such methods maintain coordination performance while drastically reducing the required communication bandwidth — a concern that parallels the scalability challenges in value decomposition, where sparse interaction graphs and hierarchical decomposition are similarly employed to manage the computational cost of coordinating large agent populations.

### Memory-Driven Communication Protocols

Another important direction involves equipping agents with memory structures that persist information across time, enabling richer and more temporally coherent communication. Memory-driven communication protocols integrate recurrent architectures or external memory modules into the messaging pipeline, allowing agents to maintain and share beliefs about the environment's history rather than relying solely on instantaneous observations. This temporal dimension of communication is particularly beneficial in partially observable environments, where a single observation is insufficient for accurate state estimation. Agents with memory-augmented communication can maintain running estimates of hidden state variables and share these accumulated beliefs with teammates, substantially reducing the uncertainty that each agent must independently resolve — uncertainty that would otherwise impede the credit assignment process and degrade the effectiveness of cooperative value decomposition.

### Visual and Implicit Communication Maps

Beyond explicit message-passing frameworks, some approaches to inter-agent information sharing leverage shared visual or spatial representations, such as occupancy maps, feature maps, or ego-centric visual summaries that are broadcast or fused with other agents' representations. Visual communication maps allow agents to share rich spatial information without requiring predefined message vocabularies, leveraging the representational power of convolutional neural networks to encode environment state. Such implicit communication mechanisms are particularly effective in navigation and exploration tasks, where spatial coverage information is the most critical shared resource.

### Challenges and Open Directions

Designing effective communication protocols for MARL remains a challenging open problem. Key issues include ensuring communication robustness under noisy or adversarial message perturbations, maintaining scalability as the number of agents grows, and preventing communication collapse — a degenerate outcome where agents learn to ignore or discard all incoming messages. Additionally, the interplay between communication and exploration poses fundamental questions: poorly coordinated communication during early training can trap agents in suboptimal equilibria, suggesting that exploration strategies and communication learning must be co-designed. This connection to exploration will resurface in the discussion of cooperative, competitive, and mixed settings in the following section, where the nature of agent interactions further shapes the communication requirements and coordination strategies available to learning agents. Future directions include the development of communication protocols grounded in information theory, causal reasoning, and natural language, as well as the systematic integration of communication learning with hierarchical and model-based MARL frameworks.

In summary, communication protocol design is a central challenge in MARL, with methods ranging from end-to-end differentiable broadcasting and targeted attention-based messaging to bandwidth-aware scheduling and memory-augmented information sharing. These communication mechanisms serve as a natural complement to the value decomposition and credit assignment methods introduced in the preceding section: where value decomposition addresses coordination by structuring the joint value function, communication protocols address it by enriching the information available to each agent at decision time. Together, they form two pillars of the broader CTDE framework, and their interplay becomes especially salient in the cooperative, competitive, and mixed multi-agent settings examined in the section that follows.

### 4.5 Cooperative, Competitive, and Mixed Multi-Agent Settings

## 4.5 Cooperative, Competitive, and Mixed Multi-Agent Settings

Multi-agent deep reinforcement learning encompasses a rich spectrum of interaction paradigms, each presenting fundamentally distinct algorithmic and strategic challenges. Building on the communication protocols and information-sharing mechanisms discussed in the previous section, the nature of agent interactions, reward structures, and emergent behavioral strategies differ substantially depending on whether agents operate in purely cooperative, purely competitive, or mixed cooperative-competitive environments. Understanding these distinctions is essential for designing algorithms that are appropriately tailored to the underlying game-theoretic structure, and the exploration challenges introduced by each paradigm — including non-stationarity, sparse rewards, and partial observability — further motivate the need for paradigm-specific solutions.

### Cooperative Settings

In purely cooperative settings, all agents share a common objective and must coordinate their actions to maximize a joint reward signal. The central challenge in such environments is the **multi-agent credit assignment problem**: determining how each individual agent contributed to the collective outcome. When agents receive only a global team reward, it becomes difficult to evaluate individual contributions, which can slow down learning and lead to suboptimal coordination. Algorithms such as QMIX and VDN address this through value decomposition, factoring the joint action-value function into individual contributions while maintaining consistency with the global objective.

A prominent benchmark for cooperative multi-agent reinforcement learning is the StarCraft Multi-Agent Challenge (SMAC), which features heterogeneous agent types on complex maps and demands tight coordination. In this domain, agent modeling has been explored as an auxiliary learning task to improve coordination. For example, extending actor-critic methods with agent modeling — where agents learn to predict the policies of other agents alongside standard actor and critic objectives — has been shown to stabilize learning and improve expected rewards in cooperative object transportation tasks [209]. By leveraging representations of co-learners' behaviors, agents can better anticipate joint actions and coordinate more effectively.

Non-monotonic value function factorization presents another important avenue in cooperative settings. Standard QMIX enforces a monotonicity constraint on the mixing network, which limits the expressiveness of the joint value function and may fail to represent certain cooperative strategies. NQMIX addresses this limitation by introducing an off-policy policy gradient method that removes the monotonicity constraint, enabling more expressive non-monotonic joint value functions. This allows agents to represent richer cooperative strategies, particularly in environments with heterogeneous agent types [210]. NQMIX further mitigates overestimation issues inherent in QMIX by targeting state-values rather than overestimated Q-value targets.

Coordination in cooperative settings is also tightly linked to the communication protocols discussed in the preceding section. When agents can share information, coordination becomes substantially easier, but bandwidth and observability constraints frequently prevent unlimited communication. Communication-based cooperative methods learn to selectively share relevant information, improving joint decision-making under partial observability. These communication protocols must be co-optimized alongside cooperative policies to ensure that shared information is meaningful and actionable.

### Competitive Settings

In purely competitive settings, the goals of agents are directly opposed. The canonical example is a zero-sum two-player game, where one agent's gain is precisely another's loss. The theoretical foundation for competitive multi-agent learning is rooted in game theory, particularly in the concept of Nash equilibria, where no agent can unilaterally improve its outcome given the strategies of opponents.

A key challenge in competitive environments is **opponent modeling**: accurately representing and anticipating the strategies of adversaries to develop effective counter-strategies. Unlike cooperative settings where agents benefit from coordination, competitive agents must exploit weaknesses in opponent behavior while simultaneously protecting against exploitation themselves. Self-play is a prominent training paradigm in competitive settings, wherein agents improve by playing against copies or historical versions of themselves, gradually discovering stronger strategies through iterative refinement.

Emergent strategies in competitive settings can be highly sophisticated and counterintuitive, arising from the co-evolutionary dynamics of competing learners. In adversarial actor-critic formulations, the introduction of an adversarial agent that attempts to predict and counter the primary agent's actions has been shown to stimulate more diverse and exploratory behaviors. The Adversarially Guided Actor-Critic (AGAC) algorithm incorporates an adversary that mimics the actor by minimizing the KL-divergence between their action distributions, while the actor learns to differentiate itself from adversary predictions. This adversarial pressure incentivizes the actor to pursue strategies that are difficult to predict from historical trajectories, promoting innovation in hard-exploration and procedurally-generated competitive tasks [211]. Notably, such adversarial mechanisms also serve as an implicit form of coordinated exploration, complementing the exploration strategies discussed in the following section.

Competitive environments also introduce non-stationarity challenges from the perspective of each individual agent: as opponents improve their strategies, the effective environment dynamics change, invalidating value estimates and requiring continuous adaptation. This co-evolutionary instability can cause learning to cycle without converging to stable solutions, a problem that has motivated research into opponent-aware learning, best-response computation, and policy regularization methods that stabilize training against adaptive adversaries.

### Mixed Cooperative-Competitive Settings

Many real-world scenarios involve both cooperative and competitive elements simultaneously. Mixed settings arise when agents form teams that cooperate internally while competing against other teams, or when individual agents must balance shared group interests against personal incentives. Such environments are formalized as general-sum Markov games, where agent reward functions can exhibit partial alignment and partial conflict.

Mixed settings demand algorithms capable of managing the dual imperatives of coordination with allies and competition with opponents. This requires agents to simultaneously perform intra-team credit assignment, inter-team opponent modeling, and policy adaptation to a non-stationary multi-team environment. The MADDPG algorithm, a multi-agent extension of DDPG, addresses this by learning centralized critics conditioned on the observations and actions of all agents, allowing each agent to account for the behavior of both allies and opponents during training while executing decentralized policies at test time. This centralized training with decentralized execution (CTDE) paradigm is broadly applicable across cooperative, competitive, and mixed settings, and naturally integrates with the communication-based information sharing mechanisms described earlier.

Social dilemmas represent a particularly challenging class of mixed settings, where individually rational behavior leads to collectively suboptimal outcomes. Examples include the prisoner's dilemma, the tragedy of the commons, and public goods games. In these scenarios, agents face incentives to defect even though mutual cooperation would yield higher collective rewards. Designing reward structures and learning algorithms that promote cooperative behavior in the presence of defection incentives is an active area of research. Intrinsic motivation and social reward shaping have been proposed to encourage prosocial behavior, while mechanism design approaches modify the reward structure itself to align individual and collective incentives.

Agent modeling is particularly valuable in mixed settings, as understanding both allied and opponent intentions enables more nuanced strategy formation. Hierarchical approaches that decompose decision-making into high-level strategic reasoning and low-level execution have demonstrated promise in mixed competitive-cooperative tasks, allowing agents to reason about team strategy while adapting tactically to adversarial opponents [209].

### Reward Structure Considerations Across Paradigms

The reward structure is the primary determinant of the interaction paradigm and profoundly influences algorithmic design. Purely cooperative settings typically employ a shared team reward, which naturally aligns agent incentives but complicates credit assignment. Purely competitive settings employ zero-sum or adversarial rewards, demanding opponent awareness. Mixed settings employ individual or sub-group rewards that partially overlap, requiring careful reward decomposition.

In many practical mixed environments, reward shaping is used to augment sparse environmental rewards with dense auxiliary signals that guide agent behavior toward desired interaction patterns. The design of such auxiliary rewards must carefully account for the multi-agent structure to avoid misaligning cooperative incentives or inadvertently encouraging defection. Potential-field-based approaches have been explored to provide informative auxiliary critics that complement reward-based critics, offering structured guidance particularly in scenarios where direct reward signals are sparse or delayed [212]. This connection between reward shaping and exploration further bridges into the joint exploration strategies discussed in the following section, where sparse reward signals similarly motivate the use of intrinsic motivation and auxiliary objectives.

### Summary

Cooperative, competitive, and mixed multi-agent settings each pose distinct algorithmic challenges that require tailored solutions. Cooperative settings demand credit assignment mechanisms, value decomposition, and communication protocols. Competitive settings require opponent modeling, self-play, and strategies for managing co-evolutionary instability. Mixed settings demand the simultaneous management of intra-team coordination and inter-team competition, often requiring hierarchical reasoning and carefully designed reward structures. Together, these paradigms build directly upon the communication and information-sharing foundations discussed in the preceding section, and their associated challenges of sparse rewards, partial observability, and non-stationarity motivate the coordinated exploration strategies examined in the section that follows. The diversity of these paradigms underscores the richness of multi-agent deep reinforcement learning as a research field and highlights the importance of selecting algorithms whose inductive biases match the underlying interaction structure of the target environment.

### 4.6 Exploration Strategies in Multi-Agent Deep Reinforcement Learning

## 4.6 Coordinated Exploration in Multi-Agent Settings

Exploration in multi-agent deep reinforcement learning (MARL) presents fundamentally distinct challenges compared to its single-agent counterpart, and these challenges arise directly from the cooperative, competitive, and mixed interaction paradigms examined in the preceding section. The simultaneous learning of multiple agents introduces non-stationarity into the environment, as each agent's policy evolves during training, rendering the environment effectively non-Markovian from any individual agent's perspective. Compounding this difficulty, the sparse reward signals that characterize many cooperative and mixed settings make it exceedingly hard for agents to receive meaningful feedback about the quality of their joint behaviors, and partial observability further restricts each agent's ability to assess the consequences of its own actions relative to the collective team outcome. These intertwined challenges necessitate exploration strategies that go well beyond simple epsilon-greedy or Boltzmann policies designed for single-agent settings, and they motivate a body of specialized techniques that build upon the coordination and communication foundations established in previous sections.

One of the central insights driving modern MARL exploration research is that independent exploration by individual agents is often insufficient for discovering high-quality joint behaviors. When agents explore independently, the probability of all agents simultaneously taking exploratory actions that lead to a jointly rewarding outcome decreases exponentially with the number of agents. This exponential degradation is particularly acute in cooperative settings, where discovering high-reward joint trajectories requires tight behavioral alignment, but it also affects mixed and competitive settings where coordinated probing of opponent strategies is necessary for effective adaptation. This observation motivates the design of coordinated exploration strategies that encourage agents to collectively venture into unexplored regions of the joint state-action space.

Hierarchical exploration frameworks represent a prominent approach to addressing this challenge. MAVEN (Multi-Agent Variational Exploration) introduces a hierarchical latent variable model in which a shared latent variable, sampled from a learned prior, conditions the joint exploration behavior of all agents. By committing to a shared mode of exploration across time, MAVEN enables agents to coordinate their exploratory behaviors, ensuring that diverse and coherent joint trajectories are generated rather than random, uncorrelated individual deviations. This latent conditioning mechanism is optimized using a mutual information objective that encourages the diversity of visited joint trajectories, effectively promoting the discovery of novel cooperative behaviors that would be unlikely to emerge from independent exploration. This approach complements the value decomposition methods discussed in the cooperative settings section, as it addresses the exploration problem that those methods assume is already solved.

Intrinsic motivation provides another powerful lens through which joint exploration can be encouraged. In multi-agent settings, intrinsic rewards can be defined over the joint state visitation distribution, incentivizing agents to collectively visit rarely encountered joint states. Such count-based or prediction-error-based intrinsic bonuses naturally adapt to the joint observation-action space, rewarding the team as a whole for encountering novel situations. However, naive application of these methods in large teams can lead to the curse of dimensionality, as the joint state space grows exponentially with the number of agents — a scaling challenge that directly motivates the large-scale MARL techniques examined in the following section.

Influence-based intrinsic rewards offer an elegant alternative that leverages the causal structure of multi-agent interactions. Rather than rewarding novelty at the level of the joint state, influence-based methods provide individual agents with intrinsic rewards proportional to the causal influence their actions exert on the behaviors of other agents. This formulation, grounded in information-theoretic quantities such as counterfactual influence or mutual information between an agent's actions and its neighbors' future observations, encourages each agent to actively seek out situations where its decisions are consequential to team dynamics. The result is a natural form of coordinated exploration in which agents are drawn toward interaction-rich regions of the environment, facilitating the emergence of sophisticated cooperative strategies.
