**Physics-Model-Integrated Safety Guarantees.** A recurring challenge in deploying DRL in physical systems is that learned policies may violate physical constraints or produce unrealizable commands that differ from the implicit assumptions embedded in the training environment. Integrating physics-based models directly into safety guarantees — for instance, by combining model-predictive safety filters with DRL policy outputs or embedding known dynamical constraints into the policy architecture — offers a principled approach, but such integration introduces its own challenges. The physics models are themselves approximate, and discrepancies between modeled and actual dynamics can invalidate the safety certificates that depend on them. Research into methods that gracefully degrade their safety guarantees in proportion to model uncertainty is therefore needed, as are approaches that can update their physics models online while maintaining provable safety bounds. This challenge connects directly to the uncertainty estimation problem: the same epistemic uncertainty that motivates risk-sensitive objectives also undermines the reliability of physics-based safety certificates when agents operate in poorly characterized environments.

**Reliability Assessment Frameworks.** Beyond formal verification, practical deployment of DRL in safety-critical settings requires reliability assessment frameworks that can evaluate the probability of unsafe behavior across a distribution of operating conditions, including rare and adversarially constructed scenarios. Current evaluation methodologies often rely on empirical testing in simulation, which cannot exhaustively cover the space of possible failures, particularly for black-swan events. Developing principled frameworks for reliability quantification — drawing on tools from probabilistic risk assessment, importance sampling, and falsification-based testing — is an urgent priority. Such frameworks must account for distribution shift between training and deployment, the non-stationarity of real environments, and the complex failure modes that can emerge from the interplay of learned policies with physical systems and other agents.

**Practical Checklists and Ethical Guidelines.** Beyond algorithmic and theoretical challenges, responsible deployment of DRL systems requires community consensus around practical safety checklists and ethical guidelines that can be applied throughout the development pipeline. Such checklists should address questions of data quality, model documentation, testing coverage, failure mode analysis, and monitoring during deployment. Ethical guidelines must grapple with the accountability structures that govern autonomous decision-making, the fairness implications of learned policies that may encode historical biases, and the transparency obligations owed to users and affected parties. These concerns form a natural bridge to the broader ethical dimensions addressed in the following section: without a robust technical foundation for safety — encompassing adversarial robustness, verified correctness, and reliable uncertainty quantification — the ethical goals of fairness, transparency, and human oversight cannot be meaningfully pursued. While initial proposals exist in adjacent fields such as autonomous systems and machine learning fairness, DRL-specific guidance remains underdeveloped. Building interdisciplinary frameworks that translate formal safety requirements into actionable engineering practices, and that embed human oversight into the DRL development lifecycle, is therefore an essential complement to purely technical advances in the field.

In summary, the path toward safe and robust DRL encompasses a broad set of interlocking open problems that span algorithmic design, formal methods, systems engineering, and ethics. Progress will require not only advances in individual techniques but also the development of integrative frameworks that can bridge the gap between the strong theoretical guarantees sought by formal methods researchers and the practical reliability demanded by engineers and regulators responsible for deploying these systems in the real world. Achieving this integration is a prerequisite for the responsible and accountable deployment of DRL that the following section addresses from an ethical and societal perspective.

### 7.8 Ethical Considerations and Responsible AI in DRL

## Ethical Dimensions of Deep Reinforcement Learning

As deep reinforcement learning systems are increasingly deployed in high-stakes domains such as healthcare, autonomous driving, finance, and public infrastructure, the ethical dimensions of their design and deployment have become critically important. These concerns do not arise in isolation: they rest directly on the technical safety foundation discussed in the preceding section, where adversarial robustness, formal verification, and reliable uncertainty quantification were identified as prerequisites for responsible deployment. Responsible AI in DRL encompasses a broad range of concerns, including fairness, accountability, transparency, data privacy, and the alignment of autonomous decision-making with human values and societal norms. Addressing these dimensions is also essential groundwork for the offline reinforcement learning challenges that follow, where privacy-sensitive datasets and limited human oversight introduce their own ethical complexities.

**Fairness and Accountability.** DRL agents optimize for reward signals that may inadvertently encode biases present in training data or reward specifications. When such systems are deployed at scale—for example, in resource allocation, criminal justice, or personalized healthcare interventions—discriminatory outcomes can arise without any explicit intent. Ensuring fairness requires careful auditing of both the reward function and the data distributions used during training. Accountability frameworks must clearly delineate responsibility among developers, deployers, and operators when DRL systems cause harm, particularly in settings where autonomous decision-making operates with minimal human oversight. These accountability structures are inseparable from the practical safety checklists and ethical guidelines introduced in the preceding section: without clear lines of responsibility, even technically robust systems can cause unaddressed harms at the institutional level.

**Transparency and Interpretability.** The opacity of deep neural network-based policies presents a fundamental challenge for responsible deployment. Without interpretable explanations of agent behavior, it is extremely difficult for regulators, users, or affected parties to audit decisions or contest outcomes. Efforts to improve interpretability—through saliency-based visualization, symbolic policy extraction, and counterfactual explanations—are not merely technical conveniences but ethical necessities [236; 237]. The lack of transparency also hinders identification of reward hacking or specification gaming, where agents achieve high reward through unintended and potentially harmful strategies. This challenge is tightly coupled to the adversarial robustness gaps discussed previously: an agent whose internal representations are opaque is equally difficult to verify and to audit for discriminatory or unsafe behavior.

**Data Privacy in Offline RL.** Offline reinforcement learning, which learns policies from pre-collected datasets without further environment interaction, introduces distinct privacy considerations that connect directly to the offline RL challenges addressed in the following section. Training datasets may contain sensitive personal information—medical records, financial transactions, or behavioral logs—raising concerns about dataset auditing, consent, and intellectual property protection. The provenance and licensing of offline datasets must be carefully documented, and mechanisms for privacy-preserving learning, such as differential privacy and federated learning, need to be integrated into offline RL pipelines to prevent unauthorized exploitation of sensitive data. These privacy requirements interact with the distribution shift and confounded data problems central to offline RL, since privacy-preserving constraints may further restrict the coverage and quality of the data available for policy learning.

**Autonomous Decision-Making and Human Oversight.** As DRL agents assume greater autonomy in consequential decisions, the question of meaningful human oversight becomes paramount. Fully autonomous systems may act in ways that are difficult to predict, monitor, or override in real time. Embedding human oversight into the DRL development pipeline—through human-in-the-loop training, approval mechanisms for high-stakes actions, and run-time intervention systems—is essential to preserve human agency and prevent irreversible harms. This is especially relevant in safety-critical domains where the cost of erroneous autonomous decisions is exceptionally high. The reliability assessment frameworks and physics-model-integrated safety guarantees discussed in the preceding section provide necessary but not sufficient conditions for safe autonomy; human oversight mechanisms are the complementary institutional layer that ensures these technical guarantees translate into accountable real-world behavior.

**Embedding Human Values.** A core challenge in responsible DRL is the alignment problem: ensuring that the objectives being optimized truly reflect human values and societal preferences. Reward functions are typically designed by engineers and may fail to capture the full complexity of human ethical judgments. Approaches such as reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF), inverse reinforcement learning, and reward shaping guided by ethical principles offer promising directions for better value alignment [238]. Frameworks that incorporate signal temporal logic or natural language constraints into reward design provide structured means to encode safety and liveness specifications, reducing the risk of misaligned behavior. These structured specification approaches also carry direct implications for offline RL, where the absence of online feedback makes the quality of the reward signal and value alignment particularly consequential.

**Responsible Development Practices.** Beyond individual algorithms, the responsible development of DRL systems requires institutional practices including standardized impact assessments, red-teaming for adversarial robustness and safety failures, documentation of training procedures and datasets, and independent auditing. The broader research community has a role to play in establishing norms for reproducibility and transparency, including disclosure of negative results and limitations. Checklists and ethical guidelines tailored to DRL applications can help practitioners systematically identify and mitigate ethical risks before deployment [218; 197]. Such practices build directly on the practical checklists and interdisciplinary frameworks advocated in the preceding safety section, extending them from the technical domain into governance and organizational culture.

In summary, addressing the ethical dimensions of DRL at scale requires an interdisciplinary effort that integrates technical safeguards—such as interpretability, robustness, and privacy preservation—with governance frameworks, human oversight mechanisms, and a culture of responsible innovation. The concerns raised here do not stand apart from the technical challenges of safe and robust DRL or the practical demands of offline policy learning; rather, they form an interconnected layer of requirements that must be satisfied jointly if DRL systems are to be deployed responsibly. As DRL systems grow more capable and pervasive, the alignment of these systems with human values and the protection of affected communities must be treated as first-order research and engineering priorities.

### 7.9 Offline Reinforcement Learning and Data-Driven Policy Learning

## Offline Reinforcement Learning: Challenges and Opportunities

Offline reinforcement learning (offline RL), also known as batch reinforcement learning or data-driven policy learning, has emerged as a compelling paradigm for addressing the fundamental cost and safety concerns associated with online data collection in real-world environments. Unlike online RL, where agents must interact directly with the environment to gather experience, offline RL aims to learn effective policies entirely from pre-collected static datasets, making it particularly attractive for high-stakes domains such as healthcare, autonomous driving, and robotics, where live experimentation is either prohibitively expensive or ethically problematic. These safety and ethical motivations connect naturally to the responsible AI considerations discussed in the preceding section, and the structured representations explored in neuro-symbolic approaches offer complementary tools for improving the reliability of offline policy learning.

One of the central and most challenging obstacles in offline RL is the problem of distribution shift. Because the agent cannot collect new data, it must rely solely on a fixed dataset that may not cover all relevant states and actions. When a learned policy induces state-action distributions that deviate significantly from those present in the training data, standard Q-learning and actor-critic methods tend to produce overoptimistic value estimates for out-of-distribution actions, leading to catastrophic policy degradation. This overestimation phenomenon, well-documented in online settings as well, is dramatically amplified in the offline context, where there is no corrective feedback mechanism to ground unrealistic value predictions. Addressing this challenge has motivated a family of pessimistic or conservative approaches that penalize value estimates for rarely-observed or unsupported state-action pairs, effectively constraining the learned policy to remain close to the behavioral distribution present in the offline dataset.

Sample efficiency, which has long been a critical bottleneck in deep reinforcement learning more broadly, takes on a different character in the offline setting. Rather than reducing the number of environment interactions required to converge, offline RL research focuses on maximizing the utility extracted from a fixed and potentially limited corpus of transitions. This has driven substantial interest in data valuation methods that assess the relative importance of individual data points or trajectory segments to the overall quality of the learned policy. By identifying and upweighting high-quality demonstrations while downweighting noisy, suboptimal, or misleading experiences, these methods aim to improve both sample efficiency and final policy performance without requiring additional environment interactions.

Domain mismatch represents another significant open challenge for offline RL, particularly in scenarios where the dataset was collected under conditions that differ systematically from the target deployment environment. This mismatch can arise from differences in the behavioral policy used to collect the data, changes in environment dynamics, variations in reward structure, or distributional shifts in observation space. Robust offline RL methods must therefore develop mechanisms for detecting and compensating for such mismatches, a problem that is closely related to transfer learning and domain adaptation but with unique constraints imposed by the absence of online interaction.

The challenge of confounded observational data adds further complexity to offline RL, especially when datasets are drawn from naturalistic or semi-naturalistic settings where a human operator or a non-stationary policy was responsible for data collection. In such cases, the observed state-action-reward tuples may reflect latent confounders that are not captured in the recorded state representation, leading to biased value estimates and causal misattribution. Causal reinforcement learning approaches and methods borrowing from the econometrics literature on observational causal inference have been proposed to address this issue, though significant theoretical and practical challenges remain.

Benchmark suites play a crucial role in driving progress in offline RL by providing standardized datasets and evaluation protocols that enable fair comparison across methods. The development of offline RL benchmarks that reflect the diversity of real-world data collection conditions—including varying dataset qualities, coverage levels, and degrees of suboptimality in the behavioral policy—is an active area of research. These benchmarks help the community assess whether proposed methods generalize across different data regimes or are narrowly tailored to particular favorable conditions.

Optimal transport-based approaches have also been explored as a principled framework for reward labeling and policy learning in offline settings. By framing the alignment between offline data distributions and target behavior distributions as an optimal transport problem, these methods provide geometrically grounded measures of distributional distance that can guide policy optimization without requiring explicit reward annotation in all cases.

Looking forward, the integration of offline RL with large-scale pretraining paradigms, model-based approaches, and multi-task learning frameworks represents a particularly promising direction. Leveraging the structure of large pretrained representations to initialize value functions or policies before offline fine-tuning could substantially reduce the sensitivity to distribution shift and improve generalization to unseen deployment conditions. Such integration also opens natural connections to the neuro-symbolic architectures discussed in the following section, where structured symbolic knowledge and relational representations could serve as powerful inductive biases that help offline agents reason about unobserved or sparsely covered regions of the state space. Addressing the intertwined challenges of distribution shift, confounded data, and domain mismatch will be essential for realizing the full potential of offline RL as a practical framework for data-driven policy learning in complex, safety-critical real-world applications.

### 7.10 Neuro-Symbolic Integration and Structured Knowledge in DRL

## Neuro-Symbolic Integration and Structured Knowledge in DRL

The integration of symbolic reasoning and structured knowledge into deep reinforcement learning represents one of the most promising yet challenging frontiers in the field, sitting naturally at the intersection of the responsible AI concerns raised in the context of offline RL and the foundation model paradigms explored in subsequent sections. Pure neural approaches, while powerful, often struggle with data efficiency, interpretability, and the ability to transfer learned knowledge across domains—limitations that are especially acute in the offline setting, where distribution shift and confounded data further constrain what can be learned from fixed corpora. Neuro-symbolic architectures seek to address these limitations by combining the representational strength of deep neural networks with the logical precision, compositionality, and interpretability of symbolic systems, offering inductive biases that could complement both offline policy learning and large-scale pretrained representations.

**Motivation and Core Challenges**

Deep reinforcement learning agents typically operate as black boxes, learning policies through trial-and-error interactions without explicit access to structured world knowledge or logical rules. This leads to several well-documented problems: agents require enormous amounts of experience to discover behaviors that a human could derive from basic logical principles; learned policies are brittle and fail to generalize when the environment changes superficially; and the resulting policies are opaque, making it difficult to audit or certify their behavior. Neuro-symbolic integration directly targets these failure modes by injecting structured inductive biases into the learning pipeline—biases that, as will become evident in the discussion of foundation models, can serve as powerful complements to the semantic knowledge encoded in large pretrained models.

A central motivation is the observation that many real-world tasks have natural hierarchical and relational structures. For example, robotic manipulation tasks can be decomposed into symbolic planning steps (grasp, place, push), each of which can be executed by a learned low-level neural controller. Similarly, game-playing agents can benefit from high-level strategic reasoning expressed in logical terms, combined with neural perception and motor control. This hierarchical decomposition aligns naturally with options frameworks and hierarchical reinforcement learning, suggesting deep synergies between symbolic abstraction and temporal abstraction in RL.

**Symbolic Planning and Hierarchical Task Networks**

Symbolic planning methods, such as those based on PDDL (Planning Domain Definition Language) or Hierarchical Task Networks (HTNs), provide a structured way to decompose complex long-horizon tasks into sequences of simpler subtasks. In neuro-symbolic DRL, these planning components act as high-level controllers that guide the search space of a neural policy, dramatically reducing the burden on low-level learning. The planner can leverage declarative knowledge about object properties, preconditions, and effects to generate candidate action sequences, while the neural component handles the perception and execution details that are difficult to specify symbolically.

This decomposition is particularly powerful for tasks requiring combinatorial reasoning over objects and their relationships. Entity-based representations, which explicitly model individual objects and their relational properties rather than flattening everything into a monolithic state vector, provide a natural interface between symbolic and neural components. Such representations enable better generalization, since a policy that understands relationships between abstract entities can potentially transfer to environments with different numbers or configurations of objects—a property directly relevant to the domain mismatch challenges discussed in offline RL.

**Logic-Based Policies and Inductive Logic Programming**

Logic-based policy representations offer an alternative to neural network parameterizations that is inherently interpretable and compositional. Decision tree policies, for instance, can be extracted from trained neural networks or learned directly from experience, producing compact rule sets that are amenable to human inspection and formal verification. Inductive Logic Programming (ILP) methods extend this idea by learning logical rules from positive and negative examples of agent behavior, enabling the discovery of relational and recursive policies that generalize across problem instances.

A notable challenge in logic-based policy learning is scalability: pure logical inference can be computationally intractable in large state spaces, and learning logical rules from raw sensory data requires bridging the perception gap. Hybrid approaches address this by using neural networks for perception and feature extraction, while logical components operate over learned abstract representations. This separation of concerns allows each module to focus on what it does best, with neural components handling continuous, high-dimensional data and symbolic components handling discrete, relational reasoning.

**Hybrid Neuro-Symbolic Architectures**

Several architectural patterns have emerged for integrating neural and symbolic components in DRL. One prominent approach is to use a neural network as an approximate value function or policy for a symbolic MDP, where states and actions are represented symbolically but value estimation is handled neurally. Another approach embeds symbolic knowledge as structured constraints or priors over the neural policy, guiding exploration and ensuring that certain logical invariants are maintained throughout training.

Declarative knowledge integration can take the form of signal temporal logic specifications, answer set programming constraints, or probabilistic logical rules embedded into the reward signal or policy architecture. Such approaches not only improve safety and compliance with domain requirements but also accelerate learning by providing informative feedback signals that go beyond scalar reward. The intersection with safe reinforcement learning is particularly relevant: logic-based safety specifications can be enforced through symbolic shields or barrier functions that intervene when the neural policy proposes logically or physically unsafe actions—connecting directly to the responsible AI and safety motivations that have driven interest in offline RL.

Knowledge graph embeddings and entity-relational representations provide another avenue for structured knowledge integration. By pre-training agent representations on structured knowledge about the environment—such as object categories, physical properties, and causal relationships—agents can achieve significantly better zero-shot and few-shot generalization to novel tasks. This connects neuro-symbolic DRL to the broader field of representation learning and transfer, and anticipates the discussion of foundation models in the following section, where the question of how pretrained representations can be aligned with downstream RL objectives becomes central.

**Data Efficiency, Interpretability, and Transferability**

The primary benefits of neuro-symbolic integration manifest across three dimensions. First, data efficiency improves because symbolic knowledge provides strong inductive biases that constrain the hypothesis space and guide exploration toward relevant regions of the state-action space. Rather than discovering the concept of "object permanence" from scratch through millions of interactions, an agent with symbolic knowledge of object persistence can immediately reason about occluded objects and plan accordingly. This efficiency gain is especially valuable in the offline setting, where the agent cannot collect additional data to compensate for sparse coverage.

Second, interpretability improves because symbolic components produce human-readable explanations of agent reasoning. A planner that generates a sequence of logical actions provides a natural explanation for why the agent takes a particular trajectory, in contrast to the opaque weight matrices of a pure neural policy. This interpretability is not merely cosmetic: it enables domain experts to identify and correct systematic errors in agent reasoning, inspect policy behavior for compliance with regulatory requirements, and build trust in deployed systems—all of which are critical concerns in the safety-critical applications that motivate both offline RL and responsible AI research.

Third, transferability improves because symbolic abstractions capture task structure at a level that is invariant to superficial variations in the environment. A logical rule that governs how to achieve a goal in one environment may transfer directly to a different environment where the same logical relationships hold, even if the low-level sensory inputs are completely different. This compositionality—the ability to recombine symbolic knowledge components to address novel tasks—represents a fundamental advantage over purely neural approaches that must relearn from scratch in each new domain, and provides a complementary perspective to the transfer capabilities sought through large-scale pretraining.

**Open Challenges and Research Directions**

Despite significant progress, neuro-symbolic DRL faces several open challenges. The symbol grounding problem—how to reliably map continuous sensory inputs to discrete symbolic representations—remains incompletely solved, particularly in unstructured real-world environments. Noise and ambiguity in perception can cause symbolic components to receive incorrect inputs, leading to reasoning failures that are difficult to diagnose and correct.

Scalability is another major concern. While symbolic planners excel in domains with well-defined, compact state spaces, their computational complexity grows rapidly with the number of objects and relations in the world. Approximate inference methods, neural-guided search, and learned heuristics for symbolic planning are active areas of research that aim to make symbolic reasoning tractable at scale. The integration of large language models as sources of structured commonsense knowledge for DRL agents represents a particularly exciting emerging direction—one that connects directly to the foundation model paradigm explored in the following section—potentially enabling agents to leverage vast repositories of human knowledge expressed in natural language without requiring hand-engineered symbolic vocabularies.

Learning the symbolic vocabulary itself—determining which abstractions are useful for a given task—is a meta-level challenge that requires automated concept discovery and abstraction learning. Rather than hand-specifying the symbolic language, future systems should be able to discover useful symbolic abstractions from experience, identifying the entities, relations, and predicates that best capture the structure of the task. This connects to the broader agenda of unsupervised representation learning and disentanglement, where the goal is to learn factorized, semantically meaningful representations without explicit supervision, and mirrors the implicit structure-discovery that occurs in large pretrained models.

Finally, the theoretical foundations of neuro-symbolic DRL require further development. While convergence guarantees and sample complexity bounds exist for pure neural and pure symbolic approaches separately, the theoretical properties of hybrid systems—particularly with respect to the interaction between neural learning dynamics and symbolic reasoning—remain poorly understood. Establishing rigorous theoretical frameworks for neuro-symbolic DRL will be essential for principled algorithm design and reliable deployment in safety-critical applications, and will complement the reproducibility and evaluation challenges that the survey addresses in subsequent sections.

In summary, neuro-symbolic integration represents a compelling research direction that addresses some of the most fundamental limitations of current DRL systems. By combining the flexibility and scalability of neural learning with the precision, interpretability, and compositionality of symbolic reasoning, hybrid architectures have the potential to enable DRL agents that are simultaneously more capable, more interpretable, and more trustworthy than either approach alone can achieve. These properties position neuro-symbolic methods as natural precursors and complements to the foundation model paradigm: where symbolic systems provide hand-crafted structural knowledge, foundation models offer learned structural knowledge extracted from vast data, and the most capable future agents may draw on both sources in concert.

### 7.11 Foundation Models and Large-Scale Pretraining for Decision-Making

## Foundation Models and Pretrained Representations in DRL

The emergence of large pretrained foundation models—spanning language, vision, and multimodal domains—represents one of the most compelling frontiers in deep reinforcement learning research. Building on the neuro-symbolic perspective explored in the previous section, which highlighted the importance of structured knowledge and symbolic abstraction for improving data efficiency and generalization, the paradigm of foundation models offers a complementary and increasingly influential approach: rather than hand-engineering symbolic priors, these models extract rich semantic and structural knowledge from vast corpora of diverse data, offering a principled path toward addressing the persistent challenges of sample inefficiency, catastrophic forgetting, and limited generalization that continue to constrain conventional DRL approaches.

**Adapter-Based Fine-Tuning and Representation Stability**

A central theme in this paradigm is the use of adapter-based fine-tuning strategies, which allow large pretrained models to be adapted to downstream control tasks while preserving the generalizable representations learned during pretraining. Rather than fine-tuning all parameters—which risks overwriting valuable prior knowledge and leads to catastrophic forgetting—adapter modules insert lightweight, task-specific components into frozen pretrained architectures. This design philosophy reflects insights gained from the actor-critic literature, where stability of learned representations is recognized as critical: just as target networks and experience replay were introduced to stabilize value learning in DRL, adapter-based approaches stabilize representation transfer by minimizing destructive interference between pretraining and fine-tuning objectives.

**Language and Vision Pretraining for Sequential Decision-Making**

Transfer from pretrained language models to decision-making has attracted particular attention. Large language models possess emergent reasoning capabilities and broad world knowledge that can inform goal specification, reward shaping, and high-level planning in DRL agents. By conditioning policies on language instructions or using language models to decompose complex tasks into structured subtasks, agents can leverage prior semantic knowledge to navigate sparse reward environments more efficiently. This connection to high-level task decomposition echoes the symbolic planning perspective discussed previously, but replaces hand-crafted logical rules with knowledge implicitly encoded in large-scale neural models. Similarly, pretrained vision encoders derived from large-scale image datasets provide powerful visual representations that reduce the burden on DRL agents learning from raw pixel observations, enabling faster convergence and better zero-shot generalization to novel visual domains.

**Universal Adaptation and the Stability-Plasticity Trade-off**

Universal adaptation strategies aim to go further by developing single frameworks capable of transferring pretrained knowledge across heterogeneous task distributions, action spaces, and observation modalities. These strategies must contend with the fundamental tension between plasticity—the ability to acquire new skills—and stability—the preservation of previously learned knowledge. This tension echoes challenges well-documented in the actor-critic literature, where algorithms such as Soft Actor-Critic [239] balance entropy regularization with reward maximization to maintain exploratory behavior without sacrificing policy quality, and where methods addressing estimation bias [240; 241] demonstrate that careful architectural and algorithmic design is necessary for stable learning in complex continuous environments.

**Aligning Pretraining Objectives with Reinforcement Learning**

The integration of foundation models with DRL also raises important questions about how to align the objectives of pretraining with those of reinforcement learning. Pretrained models optimize predictive or generative objectives over static datasets, while DRL agents must optimize long-horizon cumulative reward through active interaction. Bridging this gap requires careful consideration of fine-tuning protocols, reward signal design, and the degree to which pretrained representations should be allowed to evolve during downstream RL training. Techniques such as policy distillation, where the knowledge encoded in large pretrained models is compressed into smaller, deployable policies, offer a practical avenue for deployment in resource-constrained real-world settings.

**Open Challenges and Future Directions**

Looking forward, the intersection of foundation models and DRL holds considerable promise for developing agents that exhibit human-like flexibility and generalization. However, realizing this promise requires resolving open challenges that connect directly to broader concerns about scalability and reproducibility in the field. These include the computational cost of deploying large models in online interaction settings, the risk of distributional mismatch between pretraining data and target environments, and the need for evaluation frameworks that can rigorously measure the benefits of pretraining for sequential decision-making—challenges that will be examined more closely in the following section on benchmarking and reproducibility. Progress in these directions will likely draw on advances across the broader DRL algorithmic landscape, including improvements in off-policy learning stability [71], exploration strategies [242], and sample efficiency [243], ultimately enabling the development of foundation-model-based DRL agents capable of robust performance across the full diversity of real-world tasks.

### 7.12 Scalability, Reproducibility, and Benchmarking Challenges

## Scalability, Reproducibility, and Benchmarking in Deep Reinforcement Learning

Building directly on the challenges introduced at the close of the previous section—where the computational cost of deploying large foundation models, distributional mismatch, and the need for rigorous evaluation frameworks were identified as critical open problems—this section examines the broader landscape of scalability, reproducibility, and benchmarking as foundational bottlenecks that pervade the entire field of deep reinforcement learning. As DRL algorithms grow more sophisticated and are applied to increasingly complex domains, the community faces a convergence of interconnected difficulties that threaten both scientific rigor and practical progress.

**Reproducibility and Sensitivity to Hyperparameters**

One of the most pervasive issues in DRL research is the difficulty of reproducing published results. Unlike supervised learning, where a fixed dataset allows straightforward comparison, DRL results are sensitive to numerous implementation details including random seeds, environment wrappers, reward normalization schemes, and subtle architectural choices. Small variations in hyperparameters such as learning rates, replay buffer sizes, target network update frequencies, or exploration schedules can lead to dramatically different outcomes. This sensitivity is compounded by the non-stationary nature of the learning process, where the data distribution shifts as the policy evolves—a challenge that is further amplified when pretrained representations are introduced into the training pipeline, as discussed in the previous section. Studies revisiting landmark algorithms have shown that reported performance improvements sometimes reflect implementation-level optimizations rather than genuine algorithmic advances. For instance, revisiting Rainbow and its component algorithms reveals that the relative contributions of individual components can differ substantially depending on the evaluation protocol and computational budget employed [244], highlighting how conclusions drawn at large computational scale may not generalize to resource-constrained settings.

**Computational Cost and Access Inequality**

The escalating computational demands of state-of-the-art DRL research present a structural challenge to the broader research community. Flagship results on benchmarks such as the Atari Learning Environment increasingly require hundreds of millions or even billions of environment interaction steps, expensive distributed training infrastructure, and large-scale hyperparameter sweeps. This creates a significant disparity between well-funded institutions with ample computational resources and the broader community of researchers who cannot afford such infrastructure. The concern about computational accessibility raised in the context of foundation model deployment extends here to the algorithmic development process itself: as noted in discussions of algorithmic advances, the emphasis on large-scale environments inadvertently narrows participation and may bias the field toward methods that perform well under abundant resource conditions rather than those that are most principled or generalizable [244]. Addressing this challenge requires the development of lightweight yet informative benchmark suites, standardized evaluation protocols that decouple algorithmic quality from computational budget, and community norms that reward sample efficiency and reproducibility alongside raw performance.

**Benchmarking Limitations and Lack of Standardization**

Current DRL benchmarks, while invaluable for driving progress, suffer from several well-documented limitations. Atari 2600 games, MuJoCo locomotion tasks, and similar environments have become default evaluation platforms, but they do not capture the full complexity of real-world deployment scenarios, including partial observability, non-stationarity, safety constraints, multi-agent interactions, and distribution shift. Algorithms that achieve superhuman performance on Atari, reaching extraordinary human-normalized scores through massive computation [245], may nonetheless fail to transfer to practical applications. The disconnect between benchmark performance and real-world utility is particularly salient in light of the previous section's discussion of foundation models, where generalization across heterogeneous task distributions is a primary goal yet existing benchmarks provide limited means to measure it. This motivates the development of richer, more diverse evaluation frameworks that stress-test generalization, robustness, and sample efficiency simultaneously. Furthermore, inconsistent evaluation protocols—differing in the number of evaluation episodes, the handling of stochastic environments, or the reporting of median versus mean performance—make cross-paper comparisons unreliable.

**Distributional and Ensemble Methods for Consistency**

Ensemble-based methods and distributional approaches offer a promising direction for improving both the reliability and consistency of DRL training. By maintaining multiple value estimates or full return distributions, these approaches reduce sensitivity to individual random seeds and provide richer uncertainty information that can guide more stable learning [246; 247]. Diverse projection ensembles, for instance, exploit disagreement between distributional representations to improve exploration and provide more robust performance estimates across different environment instantiations [246]. Similarly, distributional critics in actor-critic frameworks can reduce variance in policy gradient estimates, contributing to more reproducible training trajectories [241; 248]. However, the computational overhead of ensemble methods and distributional representations must be carefully managed to ensure scalability, reinforcing the tension between algorithmic sophistication and practical accessibility discussed throughout this section.

**Distributed Training and Scalability Challenges**

Scaling DRL to complex real-world problems often necessitates distributed training architectures where multiple actors collect experience in parallel while a centralized learner updates the policy. While such architectures dramatically increase throughput, they introduce new reproducibility challenges: asynchronous updates, varying network latency, and hardware heterogeneity can all affect learning dynamics in ways that are difficult to control or report. These challenges are compounded when integrating large pretrained models into distributed pipelines, where the cost of model inference and gradient communication adds further variability. Ensuring that distributed implementations are reproducible across different hardware configurations and cluster environments remains an open engineering and scientific challenge. The development of standardized distributed training platforms, analogous to established frameworks in supervised deep learning, would significantly improve the community's ability to build cumulatively on prior work.

**Toward Principled Benchmarking and Open Science**

Addressing scalability, reproducibility, and benchmarking challenges requires coordinated community effort across several dimensions. First, the field would benefit from standardized evaluation suites that include environments of varying complexity, from small-scale tabular problems that yield scientific insight with low computational cost [244], to challenging continuous control and partially observable tasks that reflect real-world demands. Second, establishing community norms around seed reporting, ablation studies, and statistical significance testing would improve the reliability of published claims. Third, open-source implementations with frozen dependencies and containerized environments would reduce the burden of reproduction. Finally, developing theoretical frameworks that explain when and why algorithmic innovations provide genuine benefits—beyond empirical performance on specific benchmarks—remains essential for separating lasting contributions from benchmark-specific tuning, a question that the distributional RL community has begun to address through rigorous comparative analyses [249; 250].

In summary, scalability, reproducibility, and benchmarking challenges represent foundational bottlenecks that, if left unaddressed, risk undermining the cumulative scientific progress of the DRL field. These challenges do not exist in isolation: they interact directly with the algorithmic advances, foundation model integrations, and real-world deployment ambitions surveyed throughout this work. Tackling them requires not only algorithmic innovations but also cultural and infrastructural changes that prioritize openness, rigor, and inclusive participation across the research community—laying the groundwork for a more robust and accessible future for deep reinforcement learning research.
